Commentary on the Gospel of Matthew

Latin source: Jerome's commentary on the Gospel of Matthew, in four books, with a preface addressed to Eusebius of Cremona (written at Bethlehem shortly before Easter 398), from the critical text of D. Hurst and M. Adriaen, Corpus Christianorum, Series Latina 77 (Turnhout, 1969), pp. 1–283, verified against the page images and collated with Migne's text (Patrologia Latina 26, Paris, 1845). All Scripture is translated as Jerome quotes it, from his own Latin; the Gospel lemmata are rendered from his text, not conformed to any modern Bible. Italics mark the lemmata and the other Scripture he quotes. Psalms keep the Latin (LXX) numbering, with the Hebrew number in parentheses. Greek is kept as printed, with a short English gloss in parentheses, and Hebrew and Syriac words stay in Jerome's Latin letters. The chapter summaries (capitula) printed before the text and the edition's critical apparatus are not translated. This English translation is released into the public domain.

Preface

That there were many who wrote gospels, the evangelist Luke testifies, saying: Since indeed many have attempted to set in order a narrative of the things that have been fulfilled among us, as those handed them down to us who from the beginning themselves saw the word and ministered to it; and the records that have lasted up to the present time declare it too, records which, published by various authors, were the beginnings of various heresies, such as the one according to the Egyptians, and those of Thomas and of Matthias and of Bartholomew, of the Twelve Apostles also, and of Basilides and of Apelles and of the rest, whom it would take very long to enumerate; since for the present it is necessary to say only this, that there have been certain men who, without the Spirit and grace of God, attempted rather to set in order a narrative than to weave together the truth of history. To them that prophetic word can rightly be fitted: Woe to those who prophesy from their own heart, who walk after their own spirit, who say: The Lord says, and the Lord did not send them; of them the Savior too speaks in the Gospel of John: All who came before me were thieves and robbers. Who came, not who were sent; for he himself says: They came, and I did not send them; in those who come there is the presumption of rashness, in those who are sent the obedience of service. But the Church, which was founded upon the rock by the voice of the Lord, which the king brought into his chamber, and to which, like a young roe and a fawn of the harts, he put forth his hand through the opening of a hidden descent, pouring forth four rivers in the likeness of paradise, has four corners and four rings as well, by which, like the ark of the covenant and the guardian of the law of the Lord, she is carried on immovable poles. First of all is Matthew, the tax collector, surnamed Levi, who published a Gospel in Judea in the Hebrew language, chiefly for the sake of those from among the Jews who had believed in Jesus and, now that the truth of the Gospel had succeeded it, were by no means keeping the shadow of the law; second Mark, the interpreter of the apostle Peter and first bishop of the church of Alexandria, who did not himself see the Lord Savior, but narrated the things he had heard his master preaching, according to the truth of the events rather than their order; third Luke the physician, by nation a Syrian of Antioch, whose praise is in the Gospel, who, himself also a disciple of the apostle Paul, composed his volume in the regions of Achaia and Boeotia, going further back on certain things and, as he himself confesses in his prologue, describing things heard rather than seen; last John, apostle and evangelist, whom Jesus loved most, who, reclining upon the Lord's breast, drank the purest streams of doctrine, and who alone was found worthy to hear from the cross: Behold your mother. He, when he was in Asia and already then the seeds of the heretics were sprouting, of Cerinthus, of Ebion and of the rest who deny that Christ has come in the flesh, whom he himself also calls antichrists in his letter and whom the apostle Paul frequently strikes, was compelled by almost all the bishops of Asia at that time and by deputations from many churches to write more profoundly about the divinity of the Savior, and to break through, so to speak, to the very Word of God with a rashness not so much bold as blessed. Hence the history of the Church also relates that, when he was being pressed by the brethren to write, he answered that he would do so if a fast were proclaimed and all prayed to the Lord in common; and when it had been fulfilled, filled to the full with revelation, he burst forth into that prologue which came from heaven: In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God; this was in the beginning with God. That these four Gospels, then, were foretold long before, the book of Ezekiel too proves, in which the first vision is woven together thus: And in the midst as it were the likeness of four living creatures, and their countenance the face of a man and the face of a lion and the face of a calf and the face of an eagle. The first, the face of a man, signifies Matthew, who began to write as if about a man: The book of the generation of Jesus Christ, the son of David, the son of Abraham; the second signifies Mark, in whom is heard the voice of a lion roaring in the desert: The voice of one crying in the desert: Prepare the way of the Lord, make his paths straight; the third, that of the calf, prefigures that the evangelist Luke took his beginning from Zechariah the priest; the fourth signifies John the evangelist, who, taking up the wings of an eagle and hastening to higher things, discourses on the Word of God. The rest that follows tends to the same sense: their legs straight, and their feet winged, and wherever the spirit went, they went and did not turn back, and their backs full of eyes, and sparks and lamps running to and fro in the midst, and a wheel within a wheel, and in each one four faces. Hence the Apocalypse of John too, after its account of the twenty-four elders, who, holding harps and bowls, worship the Lamb of God, brings in lightnings and thunders and seven spirits running to and fro and a sea of glass and four living creatures full of eyes, saying: The first living creature like a lion, and the second like a calf, and the third like a man, and the fourth like a flying eagle; and a little after: They were full, it says, of eyes, and they had no rest day and night, saying: Holy, holy, holy, Lord God almighty, who was and who is and who is to come. By all these things it is plainly shown that only four Gospels ought to be received, and that all the dirges of the apocrypha are to be sung by dead heretics rather than by living churchmen. And I wonder greatly, dearest Eusebius, why, being about to sail suddenly for Rome, you wished this to be given you by me as a sort of provision for the journey, that in expounding Matthew briefly I should be compressed in words and expansive in sense. If you had remembered my answer, you would never be asking in a few days for a work of years. For first, it is difficult to read all who have written on the Gospels; then it is far more difficult to apply judgment and pick out what is best. I confess that very many years ago I read Origen's twenty-five volumes on Matthew and as many of his homilies and his brief, verse-by-verse (commatic) kind of interpretation, and the commentaries of Theophilus, bishop of the city of Antioch, and the short works also of Hippolytus the martyr and of Theodore of Heraclea and of Apollinaris of Laodicea and of Didymus of Alexandria, and among the Latins of Hilary, Victorinus and Fortunatianus; from these, even if I plucked only small things, something worthy of remembrance would be written. But you compel me to dictate in two weeks, with Easter already at hand and the winds blowing, so that there is no telling when the secretaries are to take it down, when the sheets are to be written, when corrected, in what space of time they are to be set out in a clean copy; and this above all since you know that I have been so ill for three months that I am only now barely beginning to walk, and cannot make up for the greatness of the labor by the shortness of the time. Therefore, setting aside the authority of the ancients, whom I have been given the opportunity neither to read nor to follow, I have briefly set out the historical interpretation, which you especially asked for, and now and then I have mixed in the flowers of spiritual understanding, reserving the finished work for the future. But if my life is longer, or if you on your return fulfill your promises, then I shall strive to complete what remains; nay rather, with the foundations laid and the walls partly built, I shall set upon them a most beautiful roof, so that you may know what difference there is between the sudden boldness of dictating and the lamplit diligence of writing. Certainly you know, and I should blush to call you as witness to a lie of mine, that I dictated the present little work with such speed that you would have thought I was reading the words of others rather than composing my own. And do not think this was said out of arrogance and confidence in my talent, but because I wish to show you how much you count with me, since I have chosen to run a risk among the learned rather than refuse you anything when you asked so earnestly. So I beg you that, if the style is rather unpolished and the speech does not run on with its usual glide, you ascribe this to haste, not to lack of skill; and that, when you come to Rome, you give copies to Principia, the virgin of Christ, who asked me to write on the Song of Songs, a work from which I was shut out by a long illness and have put off my hope into the future; binding you by this condition, that if you withhold from her what was written for you, she too may shut up in her little book-chest the things that are later to be written for her.

Book One

Matthew 1

The book of the generation of Jesus Christ. In Isaiah we read: His generation, who shall declare it? Let us not think, then, that the Gospel is contrary to the prophet, so that what he said was impossible to utter, this one begins to narrate; for there it is said of the generation of the divinity, here of the incarnation. And he began from the things of the flesh, so that through the man we might begin to learn of God.

The son of David, the son of Abraham. The order is reversed, but changed of necessity. For if he had put Abraham first and David afterwards, he would have had to repeat Abraham again in order to weave together the series of the generation. And he named him the son of these two, passing over the rest, for this reason: that to these alone the promise concerning Christ was made; to Abraham: In your seed, he says, shall all nations be blessed, which is Christ; to David: Of the fruit of your womb I will set upon your throne.

And Judah begot Perez and Zerah of Tamar. It is to be noted that in the genealogy of the Savior none of the holy women is taken up, but those whom Scripture rebukes, so that he who had come for the sake of sinners, being born of sinful women, might blot out the sins of all. Hence in what follows too Ruth the Moabitess is set down, and Bathsheba the wife of Uriah.

And Nahshon begot Salmon. This is Salmon, prince of the tribe of Judah, as we read in Numbers.

And Joram begot Uzziah, and Uzziah begot Jotham. In the fourth book of Kings we read that Ahaziah was begotten of Joram; and when he died, Jehosheba, the daughter of King Joram, the sister of Ahaziah, took Joash, her brother's son, and rescued him from the slaughter that was being carried out by Athaliah; to him succeeded in the kingdom his son Amaziah, after whom reigned his son Azariah, who is also called Uzziah, and to him succeeded Jotham his son. You see, therefore, that according to the faith of history there were three kings in between whom the Gospel has passed over here; for Joram did not beget Uzziah but Ahaziah and the rest whom we have enumerated. But because it was the evangelist's purpose to set three sets of fourteen in different states of the times, and because Joram had mingled himself with the family of the most impious Jezebel, therefore his memory is taken away down to the third generation, lest it be set in the order of the holy nativity.

And after the transmigration of Babylon Jeconiah begot Shealtiel. If we wish to set Jeconiah at the end of the first set of fourteen, in the following one there will be not fourteen but thirteen. Let us know, therefore, that the first Jeconiah is the same as Jehoiakim, and the second is the son, not the father; of these the former is written with K and M, and the latter with X and N, which through the fault of copyists and the length of time has been confused among the Greeks and the Latins.

And Jacob begot Joseph. This passage Julian the Augustus throws at us as a discrepancy of the evangelists: why the evangelist Matthew called Joseph the son of Jacob, and Luke called him the son of Heli; not understanding the custom of the Scriptures, that one is his father according to nature, the other according to the law. For we know that this was commanded through Moses at God's bidding: that if a brother or a kinsman has died without children, another should take his wife to raise up seed for his brother or kinsman. On this matter both Africanus, the writer of chronicles, and Eusebius of Caesarea have argued more fully in the books on the discrepancies of the Gospels.

He begot Joseph, the husband of Mary. When you hear husband, let no suspicion of marriage steal into your mind; but remember the custom of the Scriptures, that betrothed women are called wives.

And from the transmigration of Babylon to Christ, fourteen generations. Count from Jeconiah to Joseph and you will find thirteen generations. The fourteenth generation, however, will be reckoned to Christ himself.

Now the generation of Christ was thus. The diligent reader may ask and say: Since Joseph is not the father of the Lord Savior, what has the order of the generation, brought down to Joseph, to do with the Lord? To him we shall answer, first, that it is not the custom of the Scriptures for the order of women to be woven into the generations; then, that Joseph and Mary were of one tribe, which is why by the law he was obliged to take her as a kinswoman; and that they are registered together in Bethlehem, as being, clearly, begotten of one stock.

When his mother Mary had been betrothed. Why is he conceived not of a simple virgin but of one betrothed? First, so that through the generation of Joseph the origin of Mary might be shown; second, so that she might not be stoned by the Jews as an adulteress; third, so that in fleeing to Egypt she might have a comfort. The martyr Ignatius also added a fourth reason why he was conceived of a betrothed woman: so that, he says, his birth might be hidden from the devil, while he supposes him begotten not of a virgin but of a wife.

Before they came together she was found to be with child of the Holy Spirit. She was found by no other than by Joseph, who, by an almost husbandly freedom, knew everything about his future wife. And as for the saying: Before they came together, it does not follow that they came together afterwards, but Scripture shows what was not done.

But Joseph her husband, since he was just and did not wish to expose her, wished to put her away secretly. If anyone is joined to a harlot, he becomes one body. And it is commanded in the law that not only the guilty but also those privy to crimes are liable for the sin. How is Joseph, when he conceals the crime of his wife, described as just? But this is a testimony for Mary: that Joseph, knowing her chastity and wondering at what had happened, hides in silence the thing whose mystery he did not know.

Joseph, son of David, do not fear to take Mary your wife. We have already said above too that betrothed women are called wives, which the book Against Helvidius teaches more fully. And the angel speaks to him in a dream with the affection of one who soothes, to approve the justice of his silence. At the same time it is to be noted that Joseph is said to be the son of David, so that Mary also might be shown to be of the stock of David.

And you shall call his name Jesus; for he himself will save his people. Jesus in the Hebrew language means savior. The evangelist, therefore, has marked the etymology of his name, saying: You shall call his name: Savior, because he himself will save his people.

Now all this was done that what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled, saying: Behold, a virgin shall have in the womb and shall bear. Where the evangelist Matthew says: shall have in the womb, in the prophet it is written: shall receive in the womb. But the prophet, because he foretells things to come, signifies what is to be and writes shall receive; the evangelist, because he narrates history not of the future but of the past, changed shall receive and put shall have. For one who has is by no means going to receive. Something of the kind we read in the psalms too: Ascending on high he led captivity captive, he received gifts among men. The Apostle, setting down this testimony, did not say received, but gave, because there it was signified of the future that he would receive, here it is told of him who had already given what he had received.

And Joseph, rising from sleep, did as the angel of the Lord commanded him. He took his wife and did not know her until she bore her firstborn son. From this passage certain people most perversely suspect that Mary had other sons as well, saying that no one is called firstborn unless he also has brothers; whereas this is the usage of the divine Scriptures, that they call firstborn not him whom brothers follow but him who is born first. Read the little book Against Helvidius mentioned above.

Matthew 2

For we have seen his star in the east. To the confusion of the Jews, so that they might learn of Christ's nativity from the Gentiles, a star rises in the east, which they knew was to come from the prophecy of Balaam (whose successors these men were). Read the book of Numbers. And the magi are brought by the guidance of the star into Judea, so that the priests, asked by the magi where Christ was to be born, might be left without excuse concerning his coming.

And they said to him: In Bethlehem of Judea. This is an error of the copyists; for we think that it was first published by the evangelist as we read in the Hebrew itself: of Judah, not of Judea. For what Bethlehem is there of other nations, that Judea should be put here to distinguish it from that? But of Judah is written for this reason, that there is also another Bethlehem in Galilee. Read the book of Joshua son of Nun. Finally, in the very testimony that is taken from the prophecy of Micah, it reads thus: And you, Bethlehem, land of Judah.

And opening their treasures they offered him gifts, gold, frankincense and myrrh. Most beautifully the presbyter Juvencus has comprised the mysteries of the gifts in one little verse: Incense, gold, myrrh to the king, the man and the God they bring as gifts.

And having received an answer in sleep that they should not return to Herod, they went back by another way into their own country. Those who offered gifts to the Lord accordingly receive an answer. And the answer (which in Greek is called χρηματισθέντες) is given not through an angel but through the Lord himself, so that the privilege of Joseph's merits might be shown. And they return by another way, because they were not to be mingled with the unbelief of the Jews.

Behold, an angel of the Lord appeared in sleep to Joseph, saying: Arise, and take the child and his mother, and flee into Egypt. When he took the child and his mother to cross into Egypt, he takes them by night and in darkness; but when he returns into Judea, neither night nor darkness is set down in the Gospel.

That what was spoken by the Lord through the prophet might be fulfilled, saying: Out of Egypt I have called my son. Let those who deny the truth of the Hebrew volumes answer where this is read in the LXX translators. And when they have not found it, we shall tell them that it is written in the prophet Hosea, as the copies too can prove which we have lately published. But we can also relieve this passage in another way, for the sake of the contentious, whose custom the apostle Paul denies that he or the Church of Christ has; and we bring forward a testimony from Numbers, where Balaam says: God has called him out of Egypt, his glory is like that of the unicorn.

Then was fulfilled what was spoken through the prophet Jeremiah, saying: A voice was heard in Ramah, weeping and great wailing, Rachel weeping for her sons, and she would not be consoled, because they are not. Of Rachel was born Benjamin, in whose tribe Bethlehem does not lie. It is asked, therefore, how Rachel weeps for the sons of Judah, that is, of Bethlehem, as if they were her own. We shall answer briefly: because she was buried near Bethlehem in Ephrathah (from the inner lodging of her little body she received the name of mother); or else because Judah and Benjamin were two joined tribes, and Herod had commanded that the boys be killed not only in Bethlehem but in all its borders round about; by occasion of Bethlehem we understand that many of Benjamin also were slain. And she weeps for her sons and does not receive consolation according to a twofold understanding: either because she thought them dead for ever, or because she would not be consoled for those whom she knew would live. And as for the saying: in Ramah, let us not think it is the name of the place which is near Gibeah; but Ramah is interpreted height, so that the sense is: A voice was heard on high, that is, spread far and wide.

Those who sought the child's life are dead. From this passage we understand that not only Herod but also the priests and scribes at that time had been plotting the death of the Lord.

He, rising, took the child and his mother. He did not say: he took his son and his wife, but the child and his mother, as a foster father, not a husband.

But hearing that Archelaus was reigning in Judea in place of Herod his father. Many, through ignorance of history, slip into error, thinking that the Herod by whom the Lord is mocked in his passion is the same as the one who is now reported to have died. So that Herod who afterwards made friends with Pilate is the son of this Herod, the brother of Archelaus, whom Tiberius Caesar banished to Lyons, which is a city of the Gauls, and made his brother Herod successor to the kingdom. Read the history of Josephus.

And coming he dwelt in a city that is called Nazareth, that what was spoken through the prophets might be fulfilled: For he shall be called a nazarene. If he had set down a fixed example from the Scriptures, he would never say: what was spoken through the prophets, but simply: what was spoken through the prophet; but as it is, by naming the prophets in the plural, he shows that he took from the Scriptures not the words but the sense. Nazarene is interpreted holy. And that the Lord would be holy, all Scripture records. We can also say otherwise, that it is written in Isaiah even in the same words according to the Hebrew truth: A rod shall go forth from the root of Jesse, and a nazarene shall rise up from the root.

Matthew 3

Repent, for the kingdom of heaven has drawn near. John the Baptist is the first to preach the kingdom of heaven, so that the forerunner of the Lord might be honored with this privilege.

For this is he who was spoken of through the prophet Isaiah, saying: The voice of one crying in the desert: Prepare the way of the Lord, make his paths straight. He was preparing the souls of believers, in which the Lord was going to walk, so that he, the pure one, might walk in most pure ways, saying: I will dwell in them and walk among them, and I will be their God, and they shall be my people. Porphyry compares this passage with the beginning of the evangelist Mark, in which it is written: The beginning of the Gospel of Jesus Christ, the Son of God; as it is written in the prophet Isaiah: Behold, I send my angel before your face, who shall prepare your way; the voice of one crying in the desert: Prepare the way of the Lord, make his paths straight. For since the testimony is woven together from Malachi and Isaiah, he asks how we can think the example was taken as from Isaiah alone. To him churchmen have answered most fully. We, however, think either that the name of Isaiah was added through the fault of copyists, which we can prove in other places as well, or at any rate that one body has been made out of different testimonies of the Scriptures. Read Psalm 13 (14) and you will find this same thing.

Now John himself had a garment of the hair of camels and a leather girdle about his loins. Of hair, he says, he had, not of wool. One thing is the mark of austere clothing, another of softer luxury. And the leather girdle with which Elijah too was girded is a σύμβολον (symbol) of mortification. Moreover, what follows,

His food was locusts and wild honey, is fitting for a dweller in the wilderness, so that he might satisfy not the delights of food but the necessity of human flesh.

God is able from these stones to raise up sons to Abraham. He calls the heathen stones because of the hardness of their heart. Read Ezekiel: I will take away, he says, from you the heart of stone, and I will give a heart of flesh. In stone hardness is shown, in flesh softness; or else he simply indicates the power of God, that he who made all things out of nothing can also bring forth a people from the hardest rocks.

Behold, the axe is laid to the root of the trees. The preaching of the word of the Gospel, which is a sword sharp on both sides, is called an axe, according to the prophet Jeremiah, who compares the word of the Lord to an axe cutting the rock.

Whose shoes I am not worthy to carry. In another Gospel: Whose, he says, shoe's strap I am not worthy to loose. Here humility is shown, there a mystery: that Christ is the bridegroom and John does not deserve to loose the bridegroom's strap, lest his house be called, according to the law of Moses and the example of Ruth, the house of the unshod.

He will baptize you in the Holy Spirit and fire. Either because the Holy Spirit is fire, as the Acts of the Apostles teach, at whose descent there sat as it were fire upon the tongues of the believers, and the word of the Lord was fulfilled who said: I came to send fire upon the earth, and how I wish that it may burn; or because in the present we are baptized with the spirit and in the future with fire, the Apostle too agreeing with this sense: The fire will test what sort each one's work is.

Then Jesus came from Galilee to the Jordan to John, to be baptized by him. For a threefold reason the Savior received baptism from John: first, so that, since he had been born a man, he might fulfill all the justice and humility of the law; second, so that by his own baptism he might approve the baptism of John; third, so that, sanctifying the waters of the Jordan by the descent of the dove, he might show the coming of the Holy Spirit in the washing of believers.

Allow it now. He said now beautifully, to show that Christ was to be baptized in water, John by Christ in the Spirit; or else, otherwise, allow it now, so that I who have taken up the form of a servant may fulfill its humility too. Otherwise know that on the day of judgment you are to be baptized with my baptism. Allow it now, says the Lord; I have also another baptism with which I am to be baptized; you baptize me in water so that I may baptize you, for my sake, in your own blood.

For thus it befits us to fulfill all justice. He did not add the justice of the law or of nature, so that we may understand both; and if God received baptism from a man, let no one disdain to receive it from a fellow servant.

The heavens were opened to him, and he saw the Spirit descending like a dove, coming upon him, and the rest. The mystery of the Trinity is shown in the baptism. The Lord is baptized, the Spirit descends in the form of a dove, the voice of the Father is heard bearing witness to the Son. And the heavens are opened, not by an unbarring of the elements, but to spiritual eyes, with which Ezekiel too, at the beginning of his volume, records that they were opened. The dove sat, moreover, upon the head of Jesus, lest anyone should think that the voice of the Father was addressed to John and not to the Lord.

Matthew 4

Then he was led in the desert by the Spirit. No doubt, by the Holy Spirit. For there follows:

To be tempted by the devil. But he is led not unwillingly, nor as a captive, but by the will to fight.

And when he had fasted forty days and forty nights, afterward he was hungry. In this number the mystery of the forty days is shown to us, in which Moses too fasted on Mount Sinai and Elijah beside Mount Horeb. But the body is allowed to be hungry so that the devil may be given the occasion to tempt.

Say that these stones become loaves. It is fittingly said to one who is hungry: Say that these stones become loaves. But you are held fast by two contraries, devil: if at his command stones can become loaves, then you tempt in vain one who is of such great power; but if he cannot do it, in vain do you suspect him of being the Son of God: If you are the Son of God, say that these stones become loaves.

Who answering said: It is written: Not by bread alone does man live, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God. The testimony is taken from Deuteronomy. And the Lord answered thus because his purpose was to overcome the devil by humility, not by power. At the same time it must be noted that, had the Lord not begun to fast, the devil would have had no occasion to tempt, according to that saying: Son, as you come to the service of God, prepare your soul for temptation. But the Savior's answer itself also shows that it was a man who was tempted: Not by bread alone does man live, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God. So if anyone does not feed on the word of God, he does not live.

Then the devil took him up into the holy city. This taking up that is spoken of does not come from any weakness of the Lord, but from the pride of the enemy, who thinks the Savior's will is necessity. From this passage, moreover, it is understood what that is which is written in another place: They went into the holy city and appeared to many.

He set him on the pinnacle of the temple, so that the one he had tempted by hunger he might tempt also by vainglory.

If you are the Son of God, throw yourself down. In all the temptations the devil works at this: to find out whether he is the Son of God. But the Lord so tempers his answer as to leave him in doubt.

Throw yourself down. The voice of the devil, by which he always desires that all should fall down; Throw yourself, he says; he can persuade, he cannot cast down.

He has commanded his angels concerning you, that they bear you up in their hands, lest perhaps you strike your foot against a stone. This we read in Psalm 90 (91). But there the prophecy is not about Christ but about a holy man. So the devil interprets the Scriptures badly. Surely, if he really knew it was written of the Savior, he ought also to have quoted what follows in the same psalm against himself: You shall walk upon the asp and the basilisk, and you shall trample the lion and the dragon. Of the help of the angels he speaks as though to a weak man; of his own trampling, like a shifty evader, he is silent.

Jesus said to him: Again it is written: You shall not tempt the Lord your God. The false arrows of the devil, drawn from the Scriptures, he breaks with the true shields of the Scriptures. And it should be noted that he brought forward the necessary testimonies from Deuteronomy only, to show the mysteries of the second law.

Again the devil takes him up onto a very high mountain and shows him all the kingdoms of the world and their glory. The glory of the world, which is to pass away with the world, is displayed on a mountain and on a height. But the Lord comes down to low places and plains, so as to overcome the devil by humility. The devil, moreover, hurries to lead him to the mountains, so that through the mountains by which he himself had fallen the rest too may fall, according to that saying of the Apostle: Lest, puffed up, he fall into the judgment of the devil.

All these I will give you, if falling down you worship me. Arrogant and proud, he says this too out of boastfulness, not because the devil has power in the whole world so as to be able to give all kingdoms, since we know that very many holy men were made kings by God. If falling down, he says, you worship me. So whoever is going to worship the devil has fallen beforehand.

Then Jesus said to him: Go, Satan; it is written: You shall worship the Lord your God and him alone shall you serve. Satan and the apostle Peter are not, as most think, condemned by the same sentence. For to Peter it is said: Go behind me, Satan, that is, follow me, you who are contrary to my will; but here he hears: go, Satan, and behind is not said to him, so that it is understood: go into the eternal fire that has been prepared for you and your angels.

You shall worship the Lord your God. The devil, saying to the Savior: If falling down you worship me, hears on the contrary that it is rather he himself who ought to worship him as his Lord and God.

Then the devil left him, and angels came and were ministering to him. The temptation comes first so that victory may follow. Angels minister so that the dignity of the victor may be proven.

Land of Zebulun and land of Naphtali, and the rest. These were the first to hear the Lord preaching, so that where Israel's first captivity at the hands of the Assyrians had been, there the proclamation of the Redeemer should arise.

From then on Jesus began to preach and to say: Repent, for the kingdom of heaven will draw near. With John handed over, he himself rightly begins to preach. As the law ceases, the Gospel accordingly arises. And if the Savior preaches the same things that John the Baptist had said before, he shows that he is the Son of the same God whose prophet the latter is.

Come after me, and I will make you become fishers of men. These were the first called to follow the Lord; fishermen and unlettered men are sent to preach, lest the faith of believers be thought to come about not by the power of God but by eloquence and learning.

And the lunatics and the paralytics, and he cured them. Not truly lunatics, but those who were thought to be lunatics because of the deceit of demons, who, observing the lunar seasons, wished to defame the creature so that blasphemies might overflow onto the Creator.

Matthew 5

But seeing the crowds he went up onto the mountain, and when he had sat down his disciples came to him. The Lord climbs up to the mountains so as to draw the crowds with him to higher things; but the crowds are not able to go up, and the disciples follow, to whom also he speaks not standing but seated and drawn in. For they could not have understood him shining in his majesty. According to the letter, some of the simpler brothers think that he taught the beatitudes and the rest that follows on the Mount of Olives, which is by no means so. For from what precedes and what follows the place is shown to be in Galilee, and we think it is either Tabor or some other high mountain. In short, after he finished his discourses there follows at once: But when he had entered Capernaum.

Blessed are the poor in spirit. This is what we read elsewhere: And he will save the humble in spirit. But lest anyone should think that poverty, which is sometimes borne of necessity, is being proclaimed by the Lord, he added in spirit, so that you would understand humility, not want. Blessed are the poor in spirit, who for the sake of the Holy Spirit are poor by their own will. Hence the Savior too speaks through Isaiah about poor men of this kind: The Lord has anointed me; for this reason he has sent me to preach the Gospel to the poor.

Blessed are the meek, for they shall possess the land. Not the land of Judea, nor the land of this world, not the cursed land bringing forth thorns and thistles, which every most cruel man and warrior possesses more than others, but the land that the psalmist longs for: I believe that I shall see the good things of the Lord in the land of the living. A possessor of this kind, and after his victory a triumphant one, is also described in Psalm 44 (45): And press on, and proceed prosperously, and reign, for the sake of truth and meekness and justice. For no one possesses this earthly land through meekness, but through pride.

Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted. The mourning set down here is not for those dead by the common law of nature, but for those dead in sins and vices. Thus Samuel also wept for Saul, because the Lord had repented that he had anointed him king over Israel; thus the apostle Paul too says that he weeps and mourns for those who after fornication and impurity have not repented.

Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for justice. It is not enough for us to want justice unless we suffer hunger for justice, so that under this example we may understand that we are never just enough, but always hunger for the works of justice.

Blessed are the merciful. Mercy is understood not only in almsgiving but in every sin of a brother, if we bear one another's burdens.

Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God. Those whom no consciousness of sin accuses. The Pure One is seen by the pure heart; the temple of God cannot be polluted.

Blessed are the peacemakers. Those who make peace first in their own heart, then among brothers at variance. For what does it profit that others are brought to peace through you, when in your own mind there are wars of vices?

Blessed are those who suffer persecution for the sake of justice. He added pointedly: for the sake of justice. For many suffer persecution for their own sins and are not just. And at the same time consider that the eighth beatitude, that of the true circumcision, ends in martyrdom.

Blessed are you when they curse you and persecute you and say every evil against you, lying. That curse is to be despised, and creates blessedness, which is uttered by the mouth of one who curses falsely. Hence he also specifically defined what the blessed curse is, saying: every curse against you, lying, for my sake. So where Christ is the cause, there a curse is to be desired.

Rejoice and exult. I do not know which of us could fulfill this: that our reputation be torn with reproaches and we exult in the Lord. He who pursues vainglory cannot fulfill it. We ought therefore to rejoice and exult, so that a reward may be prepared for us in the heavens. We read it elegantly written in a certain book: Do not seek glory, and you will not grieve when you are without glory.

You are the salt of the earth. The apostles are called salt because through them the whole race of men is seasoned.

But if the salt has vanished, with what will it be salted? If the teacher has gone astray, by what other teacher will he be corrected?

It is good for nothing any more except to be cast out and trodden underfoot by men. The example is taken from agriculture. For salt, as it is necessary for seasoning foods and for drying meats, so it has no other use. Indeed, we read in the Scriptures that certain cities were sown with salt by the anger of their conquerors, so that no shoot might spring up in them. Let teachers and bishops therefore beware, and see that the mighty mightily endure torments and that there is no remedy, but that the falls of the greater lead down to Tartarus.

You are the light of the world. A city set upon a mountain cannot be hidden, nor do they light a lamp and put it under a bushel, and the rest. He teaches boldness in preaching, so that the apostles may not hide themselves out of fear and be like a lamp under a bushel, but may come forward with all freedom, so as to preach on the housetops what they have heard in the chambers.

Do not think that I have come to destroy the law or the prophets; I have not come to destroy but to fulfill. Either because he fulfilled what had been prophesied about himself through others; or because the things which before, on account of the weakness of the hearers, had been rough and imperfect, he completed by his preaching, taking away anger and the requital of retaliation, and shutting out even the lust hidden in the mind.

Until heaven and earth pass away. New heavens and a new earth are promised to us, which the Lord God will make. If, then, new things are to be created, it follows that the old will pass away. But as for what follows:

One iota or one apex shall not pass from the law until all things come to pass, it is shown by the figure of the letter that even the things thought least in the law are full of spiritual mysteries, and that all things are summed up in the Gospel. Of what learning, then, and of what doctrine is it to show that even the various sacrifices, and the things in the victims that seem superstitious, are fulfilled daily?

Whoever destroys one of these least commandments and teaches men so shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven; but whoever does and teaches them shall be called great in the kingdom of heaven. This passage hangs together with the testimony above, in which he had said: One iota or one apex shall not pass from the law until all things come to pass. He therefore censures the Pharisees, who, despising the commandments of God, set up their own traditions: their teaching among the peoples does them no good if they destroy even a little of what is commanded in the law. But we can also understand it otherwise: that the learning of a master, if he is liable even to a small sin, brings him down from the highest rank, and that it does no good to teach justice which the least fault destroys, and that perfect blessedness is to fulfill in deed what you have taught in word.

Everyone who is angry with his brother. In certain codices there is added: without cause. But in the true ones the sentence is unqualified and anger is wholly taken away, as Scripture says: Whoever is angry with his brother. For if we are commanded to offer the other cheek to one who strikes us, and to love our enemies and pray for those who persecute us, every occasion of anger is taken away. Therefore there must be erased: without cause, for the anger of man does not work the justice of God.

But whoever says to his brother: Racha. This word is properly a word of the Hebrews. For Racha means κενός, that is, empty or void, one whom we can call, by a common insult, 'brainless'. If we are to render an account for an idle word, how much more for an insult? But it is also added pointedly: Whoever says to his brother: Racha. For no one is our brother except one who has the same Father with us. Since, then, he believes in God just as we do and knows Christ, the wisdom of God, by what reasoning can he be branded with the charge of folly?

But whoever says: Fool, shall be liable to Gehenna. Ἀπὸ κοινοῦ (from the shared clause) there is understood from the words above: Whoever says to his brother: Fool, shall be liable to Gehenna. For whoever says: Fool, to one who believes in God equally with him is impious in religion.

If, therefore, you are offering your gift at the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you. He did not say: if you have something against your brother, but if your brother has something against you, so that a harder necessity of reconciliation may be laid upon you. So long, then, as we cannot appease him, I do not know whether we may fittingly offer our gifts to God.

Be consenting to your adversary quickly, while you are with him on the way, lest perhaps the adversary hand you over to the judge, and the judge hand you over to the officer, and you be put into prison. Amen I say to you: You will not go out from there until you pay the last quadrans. Where we have consenting in the Latin codices, in the Greek is written εὐνοῶν, which is interpreted well-disposed or kind. Now from what precedes and what follows the sense is plain: that our Lord and Savior, while we run along the way of this world, exhorts us to peace and concord, according to the Apostle, who says: If it can be done, as far as lies in you, having peace with all men. For in the preceding passage too he had said: If you are offering your gift at the altar and there remember that your brother has something against you, and having finished this he immediately adds: Be well-disposed or kind to your adversary, and the rest. And in what follows he commands: Love your enemies, do good to those who hate you, and pray for your persecutors. Although this understanding is plain and follows from the context, very many think it was said of the flesh and the soul, or of the soul and the spirit, which does not stand at all. For how will the flesh be put into prison if it has not consented to the soul, since both soul and flesh are to be shut up alike, and the flesh can do nothing except what the mind commanded? Or how could the Holy Spirit dwelling in us hand over to the judge either the flesh or the soul resisting him, since he himself is the judge? Others, following the letter of Peter, who says: Our adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion, interpret the adversary as the devil, and hold that the Savior commands that, while it is in our power, we should be well-disposed toward the devil, who is our enemy and avenger, and not make him undergo punishments on our account; for since he himself supplies the incitements to the vices, and we too sin by our own will, if we consent to him when he suggests the vices, he too must be tormented on our account; and they say that each of the saints is well-disposed to his adversary if he does not make him undergo torments on his account. Some argue in a more forced way that in baptism each one enters into a pact with the devil and says: I renounce you, devil, and your pomp and your vices and your world, which lies in the evil one. If, then, we keep the pact, we are well-disposed and consenting to our adversary and are by no means to be shut up in prison. But if we transgress in anything of what we had pledged to the devil, we shall be handed over to the judge and the officer and put into prison, and we shall not go out of it until we pay the last quadrans. The quadrans is a kind of coin that contains two mites. Hence in one Gospel that poor widow woman is said to have put a quadrans into the corban, in another two mites; not that the Gospels are at variance, but because one quadrans contains two mites. This, then, is what he says: You will not come out of prison until you have paid even for the least sins.

Whoever looks at a woman to lust after her has already committed adultery with her in his heart. Between πάθος and προπάθειαν, that is, between passion and propassion, the difference is this: passion is reckoned as a vice; propassion, though it carries the fault of a beginning, is nevertheless not held as a crime. So whoever looks at a woman and his soul is tickled, he has been struck by propassion; but if he consents and turns the thought into an affection, as it is written in David: They have passed into the affection of the heart, he has passed from propassion to passion, and what he lacks is not the will to sin but the opportunity. Whoever, then, looks at a woman to lust after her, that is, if he so looks at her as to lust, as to resolve to do it, he is rightly said to have committed adultery with her in his heart.

But if your right eye causes you to stumble, and the rest. Because he had spoken above of lust for a woman, he now rightly called the thought and the mind flitting in different directions the eye. And by the right hand and the other parts of the body the beginnings of will and affection are shown, so that what we conceive in the mind we may accomplish in deed. We must beware, therefore, lest what is best in us quickly slip into vice. For if the right eye and the right hand cause us to stumble, how much more the things in us that are on the left! If the soul slips, how much more the body, which is more prone to sins! Alternatively: in the right eye and the right hand is shown our affection for brothers and wife and children and kinsmen and relatives; if we see that these are a hindrance to us in contemplating the true light, we ought to cut off such portions, lest, while we wish to gain the others, we ourselves perish forever. Hence it is said also of the high priest, whose soul is dedicated to the worship of God: Over mother and father and children he shall not be defiled, that is, he shall know no affection except for him to whose worship he is dedicated.

It was said: Whoever puts away his wife, let him give her a bill of divorce; but I say to you that everyone who puts away his wife, and the rest. In a later part the Savior explains this passage more fully: that Moses ordered a bill of divorce to be given on account of the hardness of heart of the husbands, not granting separation but taking away murder. For it is much better that discord, however grievous, should come about than that blood should be shed through hatred.

But I say to you not to swear at all, neither by heaven, and the rest. The Jews always had this most evil custom of swearing by the elements, as the word of the prophets frequently rebukes them. He who swears either reveres or loves the one by whom he swears. In the law it is commanded that we swear only by the Lord our God. The Jews, swearing by the angels and by the city of Jerusalem and by the temple and by the elements, venerated creatures and carnal things with the honor and homage due to God. In short, consider that here the Savior did not forbid swearing by God, but by heaven and earth and Jerusalem and by your head. And this had been granted by the law as if to little children: that just as they sacrificed victims to God so as not to sacrifice them to idols, so they were also permitted to swear by God, not that they did this rightly, but that it was better to offer it to God than to demons. But the truth of the Gospel does not admit an oath, since every faithful word stands in place of an oath.

You have heard that it was said: An eye for an eye, a tooth for a tooth; but I say to you not to resist evil. He who says: An eye for an eye, does not wish to take away the other eye too, but to preserve both. Our Lord, taking away requital, cuts off the beginnings of sins. And in the law there is retribution, in the Gospel grace; there the fault is corrected, here the beginnings of sins are taken away.

If anyone strikes you on your right cheek, offer him the other too; and to him who wishes to contend with you in judgment and take your tunic, let him have your cloak as well. The man of the Church is described, the imitator of him who says: Learn from me, for I am meek and humble of heart; and who, struck with a slap, proves his promise: If I have spoken evil, rebuke me; but if well, why do you strike me? David too said something of the kind in the psalm: If I have repaid with evils those who repaid me; and Jeremiah in the Lamentations: It is good for a man when he has sat from his youth; he will give his cheek to the one who strikes him, he will be filled with reproaches. This is against those who think there is one God of the law and another of the Gospel, since meekness is taught both there and here. According to the mystical understanding, when our right cheek is struck we are commanded to offer not the left but the other, that is, the other right one. For the just man has no left. If a heretic strikes us in disputation and wishes to wound a right doctrine, let another testimony from the Scriptures be set against him, and let us offer to the one who strikes us right cheeks succeeding one another, until the anger of the enemy grows weary.

Give to him who asks of you, and do not turn away from him who wishes to borrow from you. If we understand it as said only of almsgiving, in the case of most poor people this cannot stand. And even the rich, if they always give, will not be able always to give. After the good of almsgiving, then, precepts are given to the apostles, that is, to teachers, so that those who have received freely may bestow freely. Money of this kind never runs out, but the more of it is given, the more it is doubled; and though it waters the fields below it, the water of the spring never dries up.

But I say to you: Love your enemies, do good to those who hate you. Many, judging the precepts of God by their own weakness and not by the strength of the saints, think that the things commanded are impossible, and say that it is enough for the virtues not to hate one's enemies; but that to love them is to command more than human nature can bear. It must be known, then, that Christ commands not impossible things but perfect ones, which David did toward Saul and Absalom. Stephen the martyr too prayed for the enemies who were stoning him, and Paul longs to be anathema for his persecutors. And these things Jesus both taught and did, saying: Father, forgive them; for they do not know what they are doing.

That you may be sons of your Father who is in heaven. If by keeping the precepts of God someone is made a son of God, then he is a son not by nature but by his own choice.

Matthew 6

When therefore you give alms, do not sound a trumpet before you, as the hypocrites do in the synagogues and in the streets, that they may be honored by men. He who sounds a trumpet when giving alms is a hypocrite; he who prays in the synagogues and at the corners of the squares so as to be seen by men is a hypocrite; he who when fasting demolishes his face so as to show the emptiness of his belly in his countenance, he too is a hypocrite. From all of which it is gathered that hypocrites are those who do anything at all in order to be glorified by men. To me it seems that he too who says to his brother: Let me take the mote out of your eye, does this for the sake of glory, so that he himself may seem to be just. Hence it is said to him by the Lord: Hypocrite, first cast the beam out of your own eye. Only then is the virtue of an observance acceptable to God, if it is done for God's sake. So it is not the virtue but the cause of the virtue that has a reward with God. And if you turn aside a little from the right path, it makes no difference whether you go to the right or to the left, since you have lost the true way.

But when you give alms, let your left hand not know what your right hand is doing. Not only when we give alms but whenever we do any good work, the left hand ought not to know; for if it knows, the works of the right hand are at once defiled.

Amen I say to you: They have received their reward, not God's reward but their own; for they were praised by men, for whose sake they practiced the virtues.

But you, when you pray, go into your chamber and, having shut your door, pray to your Father in secret. Understood simply, too, it instructs the hearer to flee vain glory in praying. But to me this seems rather to be the command, that with the thought of the breast shut in and with lips pressed together we should pray to the Lord, as we read that Hannah also did in the book of Kings: Only her lips, he says, were moving, and her voice was not heard.

But in praying do not speak much, as the heathen do. If the heathen speaks much in prayer, then he who is a Christian ought to speak little. God indeed is the hearer not of words but of the heart.

For your Father knows what you need before you ask. At this place there rises up a certain heresy, the perverse doctrine also of philosophers, who say: If God knows what we are going to pray for, and knows before we ask what we need, we speak in vain to one who knows. To them we must answer briefly that we are not narrators but petitioners. For it is one thing to narrate to one who does not know, another to ask of one who knows. In the former there is information, in the latter homage; there we faithfully inform, here we piteously beseech.

Our Father, who are in heaven. By saying Father they confess themselves sons.

Hallowed be your name, not in you but in us. For if because of sinners the name of God is blasphemed among the Gentiles, conversely because of the just it is hallowed.

Your kingdom come. He asks either generally for the kingdom of the whole world, that the devil may cease to reign in the world, or that God may reign in each one, and that sin may not reign in the mortal body of men. At the same time this too must be noted, that it belongs to great boldness and a pure conscience to ask for the kingdom of God and not to fear the judgment.

Your will be done, as in heaven, also on earth, that as the angels serve you blamelessly in the heavens, so men may serve you on earth. Let those blush at this sentence who falsely claim that falls take place daily in heaven. For what does likeness to the heavens profit us, if there is sin in heaven too?

Our supersubstantial bread give us today. What we have expressed as supersubstantial is in the Greek ἐπιούσιον, the word which the LXX translators very often rendered περιούσιον (special). We looked, therefore, in the Hebrew, and wherever they had expressed περιούσιον (special), we found sogolla, which Symmachus translated ἐξαίρετον, that is, choice or excellent, although in one place he rendered it peculiar. When therefore we ask God to grant us the peculiar or choice bread, we ask for that bread which says: I am the bread that came down from heaven. In the Gospel that is called according to the Hebrews, for supersubstantial bread I found maar, which means of tomorrow, so that the sense is: Our bread of tomorrow, that is, of the future, give us today. We can also understand the supersubstantial bread in another way, as that which is above all substances and surpasses all creatures. Others think simply, following the word of the Apostle who says: Having food and clothing, with these we are content, that the saints take care only for present food; whence it is also commanded later on: Do not think about tomorrow.

Amen. The seal of the Lord's Prayer, which Aquila translates as faithfully, and which we can render as truly.

For if you forgive men their sins. This which is written: I said, you are gods, and all of you sons of the Most High; but you shall die like men and fall like one of the princes, is said to those who because of their sins deserved to become men instead of gods. Rightly, then, those too whose sins are forgiven have been called men.

For they exterminate their faces, that they may appear to men as fasting. The word exterminate, which has become worn in the Church's Scriptures through the fault of translators, means something far different from what is commonly understood. For it is exiles, who are sent outside the boundaries, that are exterminated. In place of this word, then, we must always understand demolish. And the hypocrite demolishes his face so as to feign sadness and, while his mind is perhaps rejoicing, to wear mourning on his countenance.

But you, when you fast, anoint your head and wash your face. He speaks according to the custom of the province of Palestine, where on feast days they are accustomed to anoint their heads. He commands, therefore, that when we fast we should show ourselves glad and festive. Many, reading that saying of the psalmist: Let not the oil of the sinner fatten my head, hold by contrast that the oil is good of which it is said elsewhere: God, your God, has anointed you with the oil of gladness above your fellows. And he commands this, that while practicing the virtues we should anoint the ruling part of our heart with spiritual oil.

Where your treasure is, there is your heart also. This is to be understood not only of money but of all the passions. The glutton's god is his belly; so he has his heart there where he has his treasure too. The treasure of the luxurious is feasting, of the wanton amusements, of the lover lust. Each serves that by which he is overcome.

If your eye is single, your whole body will be full of light. Those with bleary eyes commonly see many lamps. The single and pure eye looks upon things single and pure. He transfers all this to the understanding. For just as the whole body is in darkness if the eye is lacking, so if the soul has lost its principal brightness, the whole understanding will linger in darkness.

If then the light that is in you is darkness, how great will the darkness itself be? If the understanding, which is the light of the soul, is darkened by vice, with what darkness, do you think, is the gloom itself enveloped?

You cannot serve God and mammon. In the Syriac language riches are called mammona. You cannot serve God and mammon. Let the avaricious man hear this, let him hear who is enrolled under the Christian name, that one cannot serve riches and Christ at the same time. And yet he did not say: he who has riches, but: he who serves riches. For he who is the slave of riches guards his riches as a slave; but he who has shaken off the yoke of slavery distributes them as a master.

Do not be anxious for your soul, what you shall eat, nor for your body, what you shall put on. In some manuscripts there is added: nor what you shall drink. So from care for that which nature has granted to all, and which is common to beasts of burden and wild beasts and men, we are wholly set free. But we are commanded not to be anxious about what we shall eat, because in the sweat of our face we prepare bread for ourselves. Labor must be exercised, anxiety removed. This which is said: Do not be anxious for your soul, what you shall eat, nor for your body, what you shall put on, let us take of fleshly food and clothing. But for spiritual food and clothing we ought always to be anxious.

Is not the soul more than food, and the body more than clothing? What he says is of this kind: He who has granted the greater will surely grant the lesser too.

Look at the birds of the air, that they neither sow nor reap nor gather into their barns, and your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not worth more than they? The Apostle commands that we not be wiser than it behooves us to be wise. This testimony must be kept in the present passage too. For there are some who, while they wish to overstep the boundaries of the fathers and to fly up to the heights, are plunged into the depths, saying that the birds of the air are the angels and the other powers in the ministry of God, which are fed without care of their own by God's providence. If this is so, as they wish it to be understood, how does what is said to men follow: Are you not worth more than they? It is to be taken simply, therefore: if the birds, which are today and tomorrow will not be, whose soul is mortal and which, once they have ceased to be, will never be again, are fed by God's providence without care and toil, how much more will men, to whom eternity is promised, be ruled by God's governance.

Which of you can add one cubit to his stature? And why are you anxious about clothing? As he showed by the comparison of the birds that the soul is more than food, so from the things that follow he shows that the body is more than clothing, saying:

Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow, and the rest. And truly, what silk, what purple of kings, what picture of weavers can be compared with the flowers? What is as red as the rose? What is as white as the lily? But that the purple of the violet is surpassed by no murex dye is a matter for the judgment of the eyes rather than of words.

Do not be anxious about tomorrow. So he who forbids us to think of the future has granted that we ought to be anxious about the present. Hence the Apostle too: Night, he says, and day laboring with our hands, so as not to burden any of you. In the Scriptures tomorrow is understood as the time to come, as Jacob says: And my justice will hear me tomorrow; and in the apparition of Samuel the pythoness says to Saul: Tomorrow you will be with me.

Sufficient for the day is its own evil. Here he has set down evil not as the contrary of virtue, but as the toil and affliction and straits of the world, just as Sarah too afflicted Hagar her maidservant; which is expressively said in Greek ἐκάκωσεν αὐτήν (she did her evil, i.e. ill-treated her). So the thought of the present time is enough for us; let us leave aside the care of things to come, which is uncertain.

Matthew 7

Do not judge, that you may not be judged. If he forbids judging, by what consistency does Paul in Corinth judge the fornicator, and Peter convict Ananias and Sapphira of lying? But from what follows he shows what he forbade, saying: for as you judge, so will it be judged of you. Thus he did not forbid judging but taught it.

But why do you see the mote in your brother's eye and do not see the beam in your own eye, and the rest. He speaks of those who, though they are themselves held liable for a mortal crime, do not pardon lesser sins in their brothers, straining out the gnat and swallowing the camel. Rightly, then, these too, as we said above, are called hypocrites for their pretense of justice, who through the beam in their own eye look at the mote in their brother's eye.

Do not give what is holy to the dogs. What is holy is the children's bread. We ought not, then, to take the children's bread and give it to the dogs.

Nor cast your pearls before swine. The pig, which wallows in mud-holes, does not receive adornment, and according to the proverbs of Solomon: If it has a golden ring, it is found the more foul. Some would have the dogs understood as those who after faith in Christ return to the vomit of their sins, and the swine as those who have not yet believed the Gospel and wallow in the mud of unbelief and vices. It is not fitting, therefore, to entrust the pearl of the Gospel hastily to men of this kind, lest they trample it underfoot and, turning, begin to tear you apart.

Ask and it will be given to you, seek and you will find, knock and it will be opened to you. He who above had forbidden fleshly things to be asked for shows what we ought to seek. If it is given to him who asks, and he who seeks finds, and to him who knocks it will be opened, then of him to whom it is not given, and who does not find, and to whom it will not be opened, it is plain that he has not asked, sought or knocked well. Let us knock, therefore, at the door of Christ, of which it is said: This is the gate of the Lord, the just shall enter by it, so that when we have entered there may be opened to us the hidden and dark treasures in Christ Jesus, in whom is all knowledge.

If you then, though you are evil, know how to give good things to your children. It must be noted that he called the apostles evil, unless perhaps under the person of the apostles the whole human race is condemned, whose heart, in comparison with the divine clemency, is set on evil from infancy. Read Genesis. Nor is it strange that the men of this world are called evil, since the Apostle Paul too recalls: Redeeming the time, because the days are evil.

Enter by the narrow gate; how wide is the gate and broad the way that leads to destruction, and many are those who enter by it; how narrow is the gate and strait the way that leads to life, and few are those who find it. The wide way is the pleasures of the world, which men seek after; the narrow one is that which is opened up through labors and fasts, which the Apostle too entered upon, and he urges Timothy to enter by it. At the same time consider how expressively he has spoken of both ways. Many walk by the wide one; few find the narrow. The wide one we do not seek, nor is there need of finding it: it offers itself of its own accord and is the way of those who go astray; but the narrow one neither do all find, nor do those who have found it enter by it at once. Indeed many, having found the way of truth, are taken captive by the pleasures of the world and turn back from the middle of the journey.

Beware of false prophets, who come to you in sheep's clothing, but inwardly are ravening wolves. This can indeed be understood of all who promise one thing by their dress and speech and show another by their deeds. But it is to be understood especially of the heretics, who seem to wrap themselves in continence, chastity and fasting as in a kind of garment of piety, but, having inwardly a poisoned mind, deceive the hearts of the simpler brothers. So from the fruits of the soul by which they drag innocence to ruin they are compared to ravening wolves.

A good tree cannot bear bad fruit, nor a bad tree bear good fruit. Let us ask the heretics who say that there are two natures contrary to each other: if, according to their understanding, a good tree can never bear bad fruit, how did Moses, a good tree, sin at the water of contradiction, and David, after Uriah had been killed, lie with Bathsheba, and Peter too at the Lord's passion deny him, saying: I do not know the man? Or by what consistency did Jethro, Moses' father-in-law, a bad tree, who certainly did not believe in the God of Israel, give Moses good counsel; and Achior say something useful to Holofernes; and the comic poet say what the Apostle approved as well said: Very evil conversations corrupt good morals; and when they find nothing to answer, we add that Judas too, once a good tree, bore bad fruit after he betrayed the Savior, and that Paul, a bad tree at the time when he was persecuting the Church of Christ, afterwards bore good fruit, when from a persecutor he was changed into a vessel of election. So long, then, does a good tree not bear bad fruit as its zeal for goodness persists, and a bad tree remains in the fruits of sins as long as it is not converted to repentance. For no one, while remaining in what he was, begins to be what he has not yet begun to be.

Not everyone who says to me: Lord, Lord, will enter into the kingdom of heaven, but he who does the will of my Father who is in heaven. As above he had said that those who had the garment of a good life were not to be received because of the wickedness of their doctrines, so now, conversely, he asserts that credence is not to be given even to those who, though strong in the integrity of the faith, live shamefully and destroy the integrity of their teaching by evil works. For both are necessary for the servants of God, that the work be approved by the word and the word by works. That saying may seem contrary to this sentence: No one can say: Lord Jesus, except in the Holy Spirit. But it is the custom of the Scriptures to take sayings for deeds, as is proved at once in what follows, where those are refuted who boast of knowledge of the Lord without works, and they hear from the Savior: Depart from me, workers of iniquity, I do not know you; and the Apostle speaks in this sense: They profess that they know God, but in deeds they deny him.

Many will say to me on that day: Lord, Lord, did we not prophesy in your name, and in your name cast out demons, and in your name do many mighty works? And then I will confess to them: I never knew you; depart from me, you who work iniquity. To prophesy and to do mighty works and to cast out demons is sometimes not a matter of the merit of him who performs them, but either the invocation of the name of Christ does this, or it is granted for the condemnation of those who invoke it, or for the benefit of those who see and hear, so that, though men may despise those who do the signs, they may yet honor God, at whose invocation such great miracles are done. For Saul and Balaam and Caiaphas prophesied not knowing what they said, and Pharaoh and Nebuchadnezzar learn the future through dreams, and in the Acts of the Apostles the sons of Sceva seemed to cast out demons, and Judas the apostle, with the mind of a traitor, is related to have done many signs among the other apostles.

Then I will confess to them. He said expressively: I will confess, because for a long time before he had refrained from saying: I do not know you. The Lord does not know those who perish. But note why he added: I never knew you, if, as some hold, all men have always dwelt among the rational creatures.

Withdraw from me, you who work iniquity. He did not say: you who have worked iniquity, lest he seem to take away repentance, but you who work, that is, you who up to the present hour, when the time of judgment has come, although you no longer have the opportunity of sinning, still have the inclination.

And the rain came down, and the floods came, and the winds blew. That rain which strives to overturn the house is the devil; the floods are all the antichrists who think against Christ; the winds are the spiritual wickednesses in the heavenly places.

And it did not fall; for it was founded upon the rock. Upon this rock the Lord founded the Church; from this rock the apostle Peter too received his name; upon a rock of this kind the traces of the serpent are not found; of this the prophet too speaks with confidence: He has set my feet upon the rock, and in another place: The rock is a refuge for the hares, or for the hedgehogs. For the timid animal takes refuge in the hollows of the rock, and the one whose skin is rough and armed all over with darts keeps itself safe by such protection. Hence it is said to Moses too, at the time when he had fled from Egypt and was the Lord's little hare: Stand in the cleft of the rock, and you will see my back parts.

Who built his house upon the sand. The foundation which the Apostle as architect laid is one, our Lord Jesus Christ. Upon this foundation, stable and firm and grounded on its own strong mass, the Church of Christ is built; but upon the sand, which is fluid and cannot be compacted or brought together into one bond, every discourse of the heretics is built, to this end, that it may fall.

And he was teaching them as one having authority, not as the scribes. For they taught the peoples the things that are written in Moses and the prophets, but Jesus, as God and Lord of Moses himself, by the freedom of his own will either added in the law the things that seemed lacking or, changing them, preached to the peoples, as we also read above: It was said to the ancients; but I say to you.

Matthew 8

And when he had come down from the mountain, great crowds followed him. And behold, a leper came and worshiped him. As the Lord comes down from the mountain the crowds meet him, because they had not been able to ascend to the higher places. And the first to meet him is the leper. For with his leprosy he could not yet hear the Savior's so manifold discourse on the mountain. And it must be noted that this one was the first to be healed individually; second, the centurion's servant; third, Peter's mother-in-law, sick with fever, in Capernaum; in the fourth place, those vexed by a demon who were brought to him, whose spirits he cast out with a word, when he also healed all who were ill.

And behold, a leper came and worshiped him, saying. Rightly, after the preaching and teaching, an occasion for a sign is offered, so that through the miracles of mighty works the discourse just past may be confirmed among the hearers.

Lord, if you will, you can cleanse me. He who asks about the will does not doubt the power.

And Jesus, stretching out his hand, touched him, saying: I will, be cleansed. As the Lord stretches out his hand, at once the leprosy flees. At the same time consider how humble and free of boasting the answer is. The one had said: If you will; the Lord answered: I will; the one had put first: you can cleanse me; the Lord joins it and says: be cleansed. So it is not to be joined and read, as most of the Latins think: I will to cleanse, but separately, so that first he says: I will, and then commands: be cleansed.

And Jesus said to him: See that you tell no one. And truly, what need was there for him to boast in words of what he displayed in his body?

But go, show yourself to the priest and offer the gift that Moses commanded, as a testimony to them. He sends him to the priest for various reasons. First, for humility's sake, that he may be seen to defer to the priests. For it was commanded in the law that those who had been cleansed of leprosy should offer gifts to the priests. Next, so that, seeing the leper cleansed, they might either believe the Savior or not believe; if they believed, they would be saved; if they did not believe, they would be made inexcusable; and at the same time, so that he might not seem — a thing they very often charged against him — to break the law.

A centurion came to him, entreating him and saying: Lord, my servant lies at home paralyzed and is grievously tormented. And Jesus said to him: I will come and heal him and the rest. We ought not to accuse the Lord of boasting because he promises at once that he will go and heal, for he saw the centurion's faith, humility and prudence: his faith, in that, being of the Gentiles, he believed that the leper could be healed by the Savior; his humility, in that he judged himself unworthy that the Lord should enter under his roof; his prudence, in that beyond the covering of the body he saw the hidden divinity, knowing that what would profit him was not what was seen even by unbelievers, but what lay hidden within. Of this prudence he also says this:

Behold, I, though I am a man, say to this one: Go, and he goes, and the rest. He wishes to show that the Lord too could accomplish what he wished not only through the coming of his body but through the ministries of angels.

And Jesus, hearing it, marveled. He marveled because he saw that the centurion understood his majesty. For both the infirmities of bodies and the opposing powers, to which a man is often given over to be disabled, had to be driven out, both by the word of the Lord and by the ministries of angels.

Amen I say to you: I have not found such faith in Israel. He speaks of those present, not of all the patriarchs and prophets of the past, unless perhaps in the centurion the faith of the Gentiles is set above Israel.

Many will come from the east and the west and will recline with Abraham and Isaac and Jacob in the kingdom of heaven. Because the God of Abraham, the maker of heaven, is the Father of Christ, therefore Abraham too is in the kingdom of heaven, and with him the nations that have believed in Christ, the Son of the Creator, are to recline. And that sense too is likewise fulfilled of which we spoke above, that in the centurion's faith the precedence of the Gentiles is established, since the peoples who will believe from east and west are mentioned with reference to his belief.

But the sons of the kingdom. By the sons of the kingdom he means the Jews, over whom God reigned before.

Will be cast out into the outer darkness. Darkness is always inner, not outer. But since he who is driven out of doors by the Lord leaves the light behind, therefore it has been named the outer darkness.

There will be weeping and gnashing of teeth. If weeping belongs to the eyes, and gnashing of teeth points to bones, then the resurrection of bodies, and of the same members that had fallen, is real.
