When Jesus had come into Peter's house, he saw his mother-in-law lying ill with fever, and he touched her hand, and the fever left her, and she rose and served them. The woman's hand is touched, and as her works are healed the infirmity of sins flees. Human nature is such that after a fever bodies are the more wearied, and as health begins they feel the ills of the sickness. But the health that is conferred by the Lord restores everything at once; nor is it enough that she was healed, but so that an ἐπίτασις (intensification) of strength might be shown, it is added: and she rose, served them. That hand served which had been both touched and healed.

When evening had come, they brought to him many who had demons, and he cast out the spirits with a word and healed all who were ill. All are healed not in the morning, not at midday, but towards evening, when the sun is about to set, when the grain of wheat dies in the earth that it may bear much fruit.

And a scribe came up and said to him: Master, I will follow you wherever you go. And Jesus says to him: Foxes have holes and the birds of the air nests, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head. This scribe of the law, who knew only the letter that kills, if he had said: Lord, I will follow you wherever you go, would not have been rejected by the Lord; but because he reckoned him one master among many, and was a man of letters (which is more expressively said in Greek γραμματεύς, and not a spiritual hearer), therefore Jesus has no place where he can lay his head. It is shown to us, moreover, that the scribe was rejected for this reason too, that, seeing the greatness of the signs, he wished to follow the Savior in order to seek gain from the miracles of his works, desiring the very thing that Simon Magus too had wished to buy from Peter. Such faith, then, is justly condemned by the Lord's sentence, and it is said to him: Why do you desire to follow me for riches and the gains of the world, when I am of such poverty that I do not even have a little lodging and do not use a roof of my own?

And another of his disciples said to him: Lord, let me first go and bury my father. What likeness is there between the scribe and the disciple? The one calls him master, the other confesses him Lord; the one, on the occasion of piety, desires to go to bury his father, the other promises that he will follow wherever he goes, seeking not the master but gain from the master.

Jesus said to him: Follow me, and leave the dead to bury their own dead. Whoever does not believe is dead. But if the dead buries the dead, we ought to have care not for the dead but for the living, lest, while we are anxious about the dead, we too be called dead.

And as he went up into the boat his disciples followed him, and the rest. The fifth sign he did when, going up into the ship from Capernaum, he commanded the winds and the sea; the sixth, when in the country of the Gerasenes he gave the demons power over the swine; the seventh, when, entering his own city, he healed the second paralytic on his bed; for the first paralytic is the centurion's servant.

But he was sleeping. And they came and woke him, saying: Lord, save us. The type of this sign we read in Jonah, when, while the others are in peril, he himself is untroubled and sleeps, and is woken, and by the command and mystery of his passion frees those who woke him.

Then rising he rebuked the winds and the sea. And from this place we understand that all creatures perceive the Creator. For the things that are rebuked and commanded perceive the one who commands, not by the error of the heretics, who think all things are animate, but by the majesty of the Maker, to whom things insensible to us are sensible.

Moreover the men marveled, saying: What sort of man is this, that the winds and the sea obey him. It was not the disciples but the sailors and the others who were in the ship who marveled. But if anyone should contentiously maintain that those who marveled were the disciples, we shall answer that they were rightly called men, because they did not yet know the power of the Savior.

What have we to do with you, Son of God? Have you come here before the time to torment us? This confession is not one of will, on which the reward of confessing follows, but an extortion of necessity, which compels the unwilling; just as runaway slaves, if after a long time they see their master, beg for nothing else but to be spared the lash, so too the demons, seeing the Lord suddenly dwelling on earth, believed that he had come to judge them. The presence of the Savior is the torment of the demons. And some ridiculously think that the demons know the Son of God and the devil does not, because these are of less malice than he whose henchmen they are. Whereas all the knowledge of the disciples is to be referred to the master, and both the demons and the devil are to be understood rather to suspect the Son of God than to know him. For no one knows the Father except the Son and him to whom the Son wills to reveal him.

If you cast us out, send us into the herd of swine. And he said to them: Go. The Savior did not say: Go, as granting the demons what they asked, but so that through the killing of the swine an occasion of salvation might be offered to men. For the herdsmen, seeing these things, at once report them to the city. Let the Manichee blush. If the souls of men and of beasts are of the same substance and from the same author, how are two thousand swine drowned for the salvation of one man?

And behold, the whole city went out to meet Jesus, and when they saw him they asked him to pass from their borders. Those who ask him to pass beyond their borders do this not from pride (as some suppose) but from humility, by which they judged themselves unworthy of the Lord's presence, just as Peter too, at the catch of fish, falling at the Savior's knees, says: Go out from me, Lord, for I am a sinful man.

Matthew 9

And he came into his own city. And behold, they were bringing to him a paralytic lying on a bed. And Jesus, seeing their faith, said to the paralytic: Take heart, son, your sins are remitted to you, and the rest. Let us understand his city to be none other than Nazareth; whence he was also called a nazarene. And, as we said above, they brought to him a second paralytic, lying on a little bed, because he himself was not able to walk in. And Jesus, seeing the faith, not of the one who was being brought but of those who were bringing him, said to the paralytic: Take heart, son, your sins are forgiven you. O wonderful humility! He calls son one who was despised and infirm and loosened in all the joints of his limbs, one whom the priests did not deign to touch. Or else he calls him son for this reason, that his sins are forgiven him. According to tropology, sometimes a soul lying in its own body, with all the powers of its limbs loosened, is brought by a perfect teacher to the Lord to be healed; and if it has been healed by his mercy, it receives so much strength that it at once carries its own little bed.

And behold, some of the scribes said within themselves: This man blasphemes. We read in the prophet, God saying: I am he who blots out your iniquities. Consistently, therefore, the scribes, because they thought him a man and understood the words of God, accuse him of blasphemy. But the Lord, seeing their thoughts, shows himself to be God, who can know the hidden things of the heart, and, as it were keeping silent, he speaks: By the same majesty and power by which I look upon your thoughts, I can also forgive men their offenses; understand from yourselves what the paralytic obtains.

Which is easier, to say: Your sins are forgiven you; or to say: Rise and walk? Between saying and doing there is a great distance. Whether the sins had been forgiven the paralytic, he alone knew who was forgiving them. Rise, however, and walk could be verified as much by him who was rising as by those who saw him rise. A bodily sign therefore is wrought so that the spiritual one may be proved, although it belongs to the same power to remit the faults both of body and of soul. And we are given to understand that many infirmities of bodies come about on account of sins, and that perhaps for this reason the sins are forgiven first, so that, once the causes of the infirmity have been taken away, health may be restored.

Take up your bed and go into your house. And the paralytic soul too, if it rises, if it recovers its former strength, carries its bed, on which it previously lay loosened, and carries it into the house of its own virtues.

And as Jesus was passing on from there, he saw a man sitting at the tax office, Matthew by name, and he said to him: Follow me. And he rose and followed him. The other evangelists, out of modesty and regard for Matthew, did not wish to call him by his common name, but said Levi; for he had a twofold name. But Matthew himself, according to what is enjoined by Solomon: The just man is his own accuser at the beginning of his speech; and in another place: Tell your sins, that you may be justified, names himself Matthew and a tax collector, to show his readers that no one ought to despair of salvation if he has turned to better things, since he himself was suddenly changed from a tax collector into an apostle. In this place Porphyry and Julian the Augustus charge either the ignorance of a lying historian or the folly of those who at once followed the Savior, as though they had irrationally followed any man at all who called them, when so many mighty works and so many signs had gone before, which there is no doubt the apostles saw before they believed. Certainly the very brightness and majesty of the hidden divinity, which shone forth even in his human face, could draw to himself at first sight those who saw him. For if the magnet stone and amber are said to have this power, that they join rings and straw and bits of chaff to themselves, how much more could the Lord of all creatures draw to himself those whom he willed.

And it came to pass, as he reclined at table in the house, behold, many tax collectors and sinners came and reclined with Jesus, and the rest. They had seen that a tax collector converted from sins to better things had found a place of repentance, and on that account they too do not despair of salvation; nor indeed do they come to Jesus remaining in their former vices, as the Pharisees and scribes murmur, but repenting, as the Lord's following word signifies, saying:

I desire mercy and not sacrifice. I came not to call the just, but sinners. Now the Lord used to go to the banquets of sinners in order to have an occasion of teaching and to offer spiritual food to those who invited him. In short, though he is frequently described as going to banquets, nothing else is reported except what he did there, what he taught, so that both the humility of the Lord in going to sinners and the power of his teaching in the conversion of the penitent might be shown. And as for what follows: I desire mercy and not sacrifice; and: I came not to call the just, but sinners, bringing forward testimony from the prophet he rebukes the scribes and Pharisees, who, esteeming themselves just, shunned the company of sinners and tax collectors.

Then the disciples of John came to him, saying: Why do we and the Pharisees fast often, but your disciples do not fast? A proud question and full of the haughtiness of the Pharisees. Certainly (not to say anything else) the boasting of their fasting is to be reproved. Nor could the disciples of John but be at fault, since they were slandering him whom they knew to have been proclaimed by their master's voice, and were joining themselves to the Pharisees, whom they knew had been condemned by John: Brood of vipers, who showed you to flee from the wrath to come?

Jesus said to them: Can the sons of the bridegroom mourn as long as the bridegroom is with them? But the days will come when the bridegroom will be taken away from them, and then they will fast. The bridegroom is Christ, the bride is the Church. From this holy and spiritual marriage the apostles were begotten, who cannot mourn as long as they see the bride in the bridal chamber and know that the bridegroom is with the bride. But when the wedding has passed and the time of the passion and resurrection has come, then the sons of the bridegroom will fast. Some think that for this reason fasts ought to be entered upon forty days after the passion, although the day of Pentecost at once, and the Holy Spirit's coming, indicate festivity to us; and taking occasion from this testimony, Montanus, Prisca and Maximilla keep a forty-day fast even after Pentecost, on the ground that, when the bridegroom has been taken away, the sons of the bridegroom ought to fast. But the custom of the Church comes to the passion and resurrection of the Lord through humility of the flesh, so that by the fasting of the body we may be prepared for the spiritual banquet. By the laws of tropology, however, we must know that as long as the bridegroom is with us and we are in gladness, we can neither fast nor mourn; but when on account of our sins he has withdrawn from us and flown away, then a fast must be proclaimed, then mourning must be taken up.

No one puts a patch of unworked cloth on an old garment; for it takes its fullness away from the garment, and a worse tear is made. Nor do they put new wine into old wineskins; otherwise the wineskins will burst, and the wine will be poured out, and the wineskins will perish; but they put new wine into new wineskins, and both are preserved. What he says is this: until someone has been reborn and, having put off the old man, has put on the new man through my passion, he cannot bear the stricter fasts and the precepts of continence, lest through excessive severity he lose even the belief that he now seems to have. He set down two examples, then, of the garment and of the wineskins, old and new. By the old we ought to understand the scribes and Pharisees. By the patch of the new garment and the new wine are to be understood the precepts of the Gospel, which the Jews cannot bear, lest a greater tear be made. Some such thing the Galatians too were eager to do, to mix the precepts of the law with the Gospel and to put new wine into old wineskins. But the Apostle says to them: O foolish Galatians, who has bewitched you not to obey the truth? The word of the Gospel, therefore, is to be poured into the apostles rather than into the scribes and Pharisees, who, corrupted by the traditions of their elders, could not keep the purity of the precepts of Christ. For the purity of a virginal soul, polluted by no contagion of former vice, is one thing, and the filth of one who has lain subject to the lust of many is another.

Behold, a certain ruler came and worshiped him, saying: My daughter has just now died; but come, lay your hand upon her, and she will live. And Jesus rose and followed him, and his disciples. This is the eighth sign, in which the ruler asks that his daughter be raised, not wishing to be shut out from the mystery of the true circumcision; but the woman with a flow of blood slips in and is healed in the eighth place, so that the ruler's daughter, shut out from this number, comes to the ninth, according to what is said in the psalms: Ethiopia shall go before with her hands to God; and: When the fullness of the Gentiles has come in, then all Israel will be saved.

Behold, a woman who had suffered a flow of blood for twelve years came up behind and touched the fringe of his garment. In the Gospel according to Luke it is written that the ruler's daughter was twelve years of age. Note, therefore, that this woman, that is, the people of the Gentiles, began to be sick at the time when the nation of the Jews believed. For a vice is not shown except by comparison with virtues. Now this woman with a flow of blood approaches the Lord not in a house, not in a city, because according to the law she was excluded from cities, but on the way, as the Lord was walking, so that while he was going to one woman, another might be healed. Whence the apostles too say: It was necessary that the word be preached to you, but since you have judged yourselves unworthy of salvation, we turn to the Gentiles.

For she said within herself: If I only touch the fringe of his garment, I shall be well. According to the law, whoever touches a menstruous woman or one with a flow of blood is unclean. She touches the Lord for this reason, that she too may be healed of the fault of her blood.

Take heart, daughter, your faith has made you well. Daughter, for this reason, that your faith has made you well. Nor did he say: Your faith will make you well, but has made you well; for in that you believed, you have already been made well.

And when Jesus had come into the ruler's house and had seen the flute players and the crowd in an uproar. To this day the girl lies dead in the ruler's house, and those who seem to be teachers are flute players singing a mournful song. The crowd of the Jews, too, is not a crowd of believers but a crowd of people in an uproar.

The girl is not dead, but sleeps, because all things live to God.

And when the crowd had been put out, he went in and took her by the hand. For they were not worthy to see the mystery of her rising, who mocked him that was raising her with unworthy insults.

And he took her by the hand, and the girl arose. Unless the hands of the Jews, which are full of blood, are first cleansed, their dead synagogue does not rise.

And as Jesus passed on from there, two blind men followed him, crying out and saying: Have mercy on us, son of David. And when he had come into the house, the blind men came to him. As the Lord Jesus was passing on through the ruler's house and going to his own house, as we read above: Getting into a boat he crossed over and came into his own city, there were crying out two blind men, saying: Have mercy on us, son of David, and yet they are not healed on the way, not in passing as they supposed, but after he has come into his own house they approach him and enter, and first their faith is examined, so that in this way they may receive the light of true faith. To the previous sign that we have expounded, concerning the ruler's daughter and the woman with the hemorrhage, this one is joined in sequence, so that what death and infirmity showed there, blindness might show here. For each people was blind while the Lord was passing through this world and longing to return to his own house. Unless they confess and say: Have mercy on us, son of David, and, when Jesus asks: Do you believe that I can do this, answer him: Yes, Lord, they will not receive their former light. In another evangelist one blind man is written of, with torn garments and sitting in Jericho, who is forbidden by the apostles to cry out, but by his importunity receives healing. That passage pertains properly to the people of the Gentiles and is to be expounded in its own book. Son of David, have mercy on us. Let Marcion and the Manichee and the other heretics who tear apart the Old Testament hear, and learn that the Savior is called son of David. For if he was not born in the flesh, how is he called son of David?

And Jesus sternly charged them, saying: See that no one knows. But they went out and spread his fame through all that land. Both the Lord, out of humility, fleeing the glory of boasting, had given this command, and they, out of remembrance of the favor, cannot keep silent about the benefit. Note, therefore, that things that are just can be contrary to one another. These blind men are healed in the tenth place.

And when they had gone out, behold, they brought to him a mute man who had a demon, and when the demon had been cast out the mute man spoke. In the eleventh place a mute man receives his tongue for speaking. Now what in Greek is called κωφός is more worn in common speech in such a way that a deaf man rather than a mute is understood. But it is the custom of the Scriptures to call κωφόν either mute or deaf without distinction. Spiritually, however, just as the blind receive light, so also the tongue of the mute is loosed for speaking, so that he may confess him whom before he denied.

And the crowds marveled, saying: Never has the like appeared in Israel. But the Pharisees said: By the prince of demons he casts out demons. The crowd acknowledges the works of God and says: Never has the like appeared in Israel. In the crowd is the confession of the nations. But the Pharisees, because they could not deny the mighty work, slander the deeds and say: By the prince of demons he casts out demons, showing by their slander the unbelief of the Jews down to this day.

And Jesus went about all the cities and villages, teaching in their synagogues and preaching the Gospel of the kingdom and healing every sickness and every infirmity. You see that he preached the Gospel equally to country places and cities and villages, that is, both to great and to small, so that he considered not the power of the noble but the salvation of believers. He went about the cities: having this as his work, which the Father had commanded, and this as his hunger, to save the unbelieving by his teaching. And he taught the Gospel of the kingdom in synagogues and in country places, and after the preaching and the teaching he healed every sickness and every infirmity, so that those whom the word had not convinced the works might persuade. It is said properly of the Lord: healing every sickness and every infirmity; for nothing is impossible to him.

But seeing the crowds, he had compassion on them, who were harassed and lying like sheep that have no shepherd. The harassment of the flock, of the sheep and of the crowds, is the fault of the shepherds, the failing of the teachers. Whence there follows:

The harvest indeed is great, but the laborers are few. The great harvest signifies the multitude of the peoples, the few laborers the scarcity of teachers. And he commands them to ask the Lord of the harvest to send out laborers into his harvest. These are the laborers of whom the psalmist speaks: Those who sow in tears shall reap in joy; going they went and wept, carrying their seeds, but coming they shall come with exultation, carrying their sheaves. And to speak more plainly: the great harvest is the whole crowd of believers; the few laborers are the apostles and their imitators, who are sent to the harvest.

Matthew 10

And having called together his twelve disciples, he gave them power over unclean spirits, to cast them out and to heal every sickness and every infirmity. The kind and merciful Lord and Master does not begrudge his servants and disciples his own mighty works, and just as he himself had healed every sickness and every infirmity, so he grants to his apostles too the power to heal every sickness and every infirmity. But there is a great difference between having and granting, between giving and receiving. Whatever he does, he does by the power of the Lord; they, if they do anything, confess their own weakness and the power of the Lord, saying: In the name of Jesus, rise and walk. Note, moreover, that it is in the twelfth place that the power of signs is granted to the apostles.

Now the names of the twelve apostles are these. A catalogue of the apostles is set down so that those who were going to be false apostles, outside these, might be shut out.

First, Simon, who is called Peter, and Andrew his brother. To set out the order of the apostles and the merit of each belonged to him who searches the secrets of the heart. Simon, surnamed Peter, is written first, to distinguish him from the other Simon, who is called the Cananaean, from Cana, the village of Galilee where the Lord turned water into wine. He also calls James the son of Zebedee, because another James follows as well, the son of Alphaeus. And he joins the apostles together in pairs and yokes. He joins Peter and Andrew, brothers not so much in the flesh as in the spirit; James and John, who, leaving the father of their body, followed the true Father; Philip and Bartholomew; Thomas also and Matthew the tax collector. The other evangelists, in pairing the names, put Matthew first and Thomas after, and do not add the name of tax collector, lest by recalling his former way of life they should seem to disparage the evangelist. But this one, as we said above, both places himself after Thomas and calls himself a tax collector, so that where sin abounded there might superabound grace.

Simon the Cananaean. He is the one who in another evangelist is written as zelotes; for Cana is interpreted 'zeal'. The history of the Church relates that the apostle Thaddaeus was sent to Edessa, to Abgar king of Osrhoene; he is called Judas of James by the evangelist Luke, and elsewhere he is named Lebbaeus, which is interpreted 'little heart'; and we must believe that he had three names, just as Simon Peter and the sons of Zebedee, boanerges, were named from the firmness and greatness of their faith. But Judas Scariothes took his name either from the village in which he was born or from the tribe of Issachar, so that by a kind of prophecy he was born to his own condemnation. For Issachar is interpreted 'reward', so that the price of the traitor may be signified.

Do not walk into the way of the Gentiles, and do not enter into the cities of the Samaritans, but go rather to the sheep of the house of Israel that have perished. This passage is not contrary to that command which is given later: Going, teach all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, because this was commanded before the resurrection, that after the resurrection. And it was necessary that the first coming of Christ be announced to the Jews, lest they should have a just excuse, saying that they had rejected the Lord because he sent his apostles to the Gentiles and the Samaritans. But according to tropology we, who are reckoned by the name of Christ, are commanded not to walk in the way of the Gentiles and in the error of the heretics, so that those whose religion is separated may be separated in life as well.

And going, preach, saying that the kingdom of heaven has drawn near; heal the sick, raise the dead, cleanse the lepers, cast out demons; freely you have received, freely give. So that rustic men, without the charm of eloquence, unlearned and unlettered, should not go unbelieved when they promised the kingdoms of heaven, he gives them power: heal the sick, cleanse the lepers, cast out demons, so that the greatness of the signs might prove the greatness of the promises. And because spiritual gifts always become cheaper if payment comes between, a condemnation of avarice is added: Freely you have received, freely give; I, your Master and Lord, have granted this to you without price, and you too give without price, lest the grace of the Gospel be corrupted.

Do not possess gold or silver or money in your belts, no bag for the road, not two tunics, no shoes, nor a staff in the hand; for the laborer is worthy of his food. Consistently he gave these commands to the preachers of the truth, to whom he had said before: Freely you have received, freely give. For if they preach in such a way that they accept no price, the possession of gold and silver and coins is superfluous. For if they had had gold and silver, they would have seemed to preach not for the sake of men's salvation but for the sake of gain. Nor bronze in purses. He who had cut off riches cuts away almost even the necessities of life, so that the apostles, teachers of the true religion, who taught that all things are governed by providence, might show that they themselves took no thought for the morrow. No bag for the road. By this precept he refutes the philosophers who are commonly called bactroperites, because, though despisers of the world who count all things as nothing, they carried their storeroom about with them. Nor two tunics. By the two tunics he seems to me to indicate a double garment; not that in the regions of Scythia, stiff with glacial snow, anyone ought to be content with a single tunic, but that by the tunic we should understand clothing, lest, being clothed in one, we keep another for ourselves out of fear of what is to come. Nor of shoes. Plato too prescribes that the two extremities of the body should not be covered, and that one ought not to grow used to softness of the head and the feet; for when these have firmness, the rest are sturdier. Nor a staff. We who have the help of the Lord, why should we seek the protection of a stick? And because he had sent the apostles to preach as it were naked and unencumbered, and the condition of the teachers seemed to be hard, he tempered the severity of the precept with the sentence that follows, saying: The laborer is worthy of his food. Take only so much, he says, as is necessary for you in food and clothing. Hence the Apostle too repeats: Having food and clothing, with these we are content, and in another place: Let him who is catechized share with him who catechizes him in every good thing, so that disciples may make those whose spiritual things they reap sharers in their own carnal things, not for avarice but for necessity. These things we have said according to history. But according to anagogy, teachers are not permitted to possess the gold and silver and money that is in the belts. We often read gold for the understanding, silver for speech, bronze for the voice. These we may not receive from others, but must possess them as given by the Lord. Nor may we take up the disciplines of heretics and philosophers and of perverse doctrine, nor be weighed down by the weight of the world, nor be double-minded, nor have our feet bound with deadly bonds, but, entering the holy ground, we must go barefoot, and not have a staff that is turned into a serpent, nor lean on any protection of the flesh, because a staff and stick of this kind is of reed, which, if you press on it a little, breaks and pierces the hand of the one leaning on it.

Into whatever city or village you enter, inquire who in it is worthy, and stay there until you leave. Concerning the ordination of a bishop and a deacon Paul says: Moreover they must also have a good testimony from those who are outside. The apostles, entering a new city, could not know who was of what sort. Therefore the host is to be chosen by the report of the people and the judgment of the neighbors, lest the dignity of the preaching be disfigured by the ill repute of the one who receives them. Though they ought to preach to all, one host is chosen, not conferring a benefit on him who is going to stay with him, but receiving one. For the words who in it is worthy are said so that he may know that he is receiving grace rather than giving it.

And entering into the house, greet it; and if the house is worthy, your peace will come upon it; but if it is not worthy, your peace will return to you. He has expressed, in a hidden way, the greeting of the Hebrew and Syriac language. For what in Greek is said χαῖρε and in Latin haue, this in the Hebrew and Syriac language is called salom lach or salama lach, that is, peace be with you. What he commanded, then, is this: entering a house, call down peace upon your host, and, as far as lies in you, calm the wars of discord; but if contradiction arises, you will have the reward of the peace you offered, and they will possess the war they wished to have.

And whoever does not receive you nor hear your words, going outside, out of the house or the city, shake off the dust from your feet. The dust is shaken from the feet as a testimony of their labor, that they entered the city and the apostolic preaching reached even to them; or else the dust is shaken off so that they may accept nothing, not even what is necessary for food, from those who have spurned the Gospel.

Amen I say to you: It will be more tolerable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah in the day of judgment than for that city. If it will be more tolerable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah than for that city which has not received the Gospel, and more tolerable for this reason, that it was not preached to Sodom and Gomorrah, but it has been preached to this city and yet it has not received the Gospel, then there are different punishments even among sinners.

Behold, I send you as sheep in the midst of wolves. He calls the scribes and Pharisees, who are the clergy of the Jews, wolves.

Be therefore prudent as serpents and simple as doves. So that through prudence they may avoid snares, and through simplicity may do no evil. The cunning of the serpent is set forth as an example because it hides its head with its whole body and protects that in which its life is. So we too, at the risk of our whole body, should guard our head, which is Christ. The simplicity of doves is shown from the appearance of the Holy Spirit. Hence the Apostle too says: In malice be little children.

But when they hand you over, do not think how or what you are to speak. He had said above: For they will hand you over in councils, and in their synagogues they will scourge you, and you will be led before governors and kings for my sake. Since, then, we are led before judges for Christ's sake, we ought to offer only our will for Christ. For the rest, Christ himself, who dwells in us, will speak for himself, and the grace of the Holy Spirit will be supplied in answering.

And brother will hand over brother to death, and father son, and sons will rise up against parents. This we see happen frequently in persecutions, and there is no faithful affection among those whose faith is different.

Whoever has persevered to the end, he will be saved. For it belongs to virtue not to have begun but to have finished.

But when they persecute you in this city, flee into another. Amen I say to you: You will not finish the cities of Israel until the Son of Man comes. This is to be referred to that time when the apostles were being sent out to preach, to whom it was also said in particular: Do not go off into the way of the Gentiles, and do not enter into the cities of the Samaritans, that they ought not to fear persecution, and yet ought to avoid it. This indeed we see that the believers did at the beginning, when, a persecution having arisen at Jerusalem, they were scattered through all Judea, so that the occasion of tribulation became the seedbed of the Gospel. But spiritually we can say: when they have persecuted us in one city, that is, in one book or testimony of the Scriptures, let us flee to other cities, that is, to other volumes. However contentious the persecutor may be, the protection of the Savior will come before victory is granted to the adversaries.

If they have called the householder Beelzebub, how much more those of his household. Beelzebub is the idol of Ekron, which in the book of Kings is called the idol of the fly. Beel is the same as Bel or Baal; zebub means fly. Therefore they called the prince of demons by the name of that most filthy idol, who is called fly because of the uncleanness that destroys the sweetness of the oil.

For nothing is covered that will not be revealed, and hidden that will not be known. And how is it that in the present age the vices of many are not known? But this is written of the time to come, when God will judge the hidden things of men and will illumine the hiding places of darkness and will make manifest the counsels of hearts; and the sense is: do not fear the savagery of the persecutors and the rage of the blasphemers, because the day of judgment will come in which both your virtue and their wickedness will be shown.

What I say to you in darkness, speak in the light, and what you hear in the ear, preach upon the housetops. What you have heard in mystery, preach more openly; what you have learned in secret, speak publicly; what I have taught you in a little place of Judea, say boldly in all the cities and in the whole world.

Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul. If those who kill the body cannot kill the soul, then the soul is invisible and incorporeal; I mean, in comparison with the grosser substance of our body. Or at any rate it will be punished and will feel torments at that time when it has received its former body, so that it may be punished together with that with which it sinned.

But fear him who can destroy both soul and body in Gehenna. The name of Gehenna is not found in the old books, but is first used by the Savior. Let us ask, then, what the occasion of this word is. We read more than once that there was an idol of Baal near Jerusalem, at the foot of Mount Moriah, where Siloam flows. This valley and small level stretch of plain was well watered and wooded and full of delights, and in it was a grove consecrated to the idol. But the people of Israel had come to such madness that, forsaking the neighborhood of the temple, they offered victims there, and delights overcame the rigor of religion, and they burned their sons to the demon or initiated them; and the place itself was called gehennon, that is, the valley of the sons of Ennon. The book of Kings and Chronicles and Jeremiah write of this most fully. And God threatens that he will fill that very place with the corpses of the dead, so that it will no longer be called Topeth and Baal but will be called Polyandrum, that is, the burial mound of the dead. Therefore the future torments and everlasting punishments with which sinners are to be tormented are denoted by the name of this place. That there is a twofold Gehenna, of excessive fire and of cold, we read most fully in Job.

Are not two sparrows sold for an as? And one of them will not fall upon the ground without your Father. But even the hairs of your head are numbered. Do not fear, therefore; you are worth more than many sparrows. The Lord's discourse holds together, and what follows hangs upon what went before. Prudent reader, always beware of a superstitious understanding, so that you do not adjust the Scriptures to your own sense, but join your sense to the Scriptures and understand what follows. He had said above: Do not fear those who kill the body but cannot kill the soul, now he says, in consequence: Are not two sparrows sold for an as, and one of them will not fall upon the ground without your Father; and the sense is: if small and cheap animals do not die without God as their author, and there is providence in all things, and those among them that are to perish do not perish without the will of God, you who are eternal ought not to fear that you live without the providence of God. This sense was also stated above: Look at the birds of the sky, for they neither sow nor reap nor gather into their barns, and your heavenly Father feeds them; are you not worth more than they? and then: Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow, and the rest. But if God so clothes the grass of the field, which is today and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, how much more you, O you of very little faith? Some, in a forced way, interpret the two sparrows as the soul and the body; and the five sparrows according to Luke, which are sold for two asses, they refer to the senses. But how that understanding can be fitted to the whole body of the Gospel discourse is a matter of no small difficulty: But even the hairs of your head are all numbered; do not fear, therefore; you are better than many sparrows. More plainly is expressed the earlier sense of our exposition, that they ought not to fear those who can kill the body and cannot kill the soul, since, if even small animals do not die without God's knowledge, how much more a man who is supported by apostolic dignity. And when he says: But even the hairs of your head are all numbered, he shows the immense providence of God toward men and signifies his ineffable affection, in that nothing of ours is hidden from God, and even small and idle words do not escape his knowledge. Those who deny the resurrection of the flesh mock the Church's understanding of this place, as though we said that even the hairs that have been numbered and cut off by the barber all rise again, when the Savior did not say: But even all your hairs are to be saved, but are numbered. Where there is number, knowledge of the number is shown, not the preservation of that same number.

Do not think that I have come to send peace upon the earth; I have not come to send peace but a sword. He had said above: What I say to you in darkness, speak in the light, and what you hear in the ear, preach upon the housetops. Now he adds what follows after the preaching. Over the faith of Christ the whole world was divided against itself; every house had both unbelievers and believers, and therefore a good war was sent so that an evil peace might be broken. Something of the kind God is written in Genesis to have done against the rebellious men who had moved from the east and were hastening to build a tower by which they might penetrate the heights of heaven: he divided their tongues. Hence also in the psalm David prays: Scatter, O Lord, the nations that want wars.

For I have come to separate a man against his father, and a daughter against her mother, and a daughter-in-law against her mother-in-law; and a man's enemies are those of his household. This passage is written in nearly the same words in the prophet Micah. And note, wherever a testimony is set down from the Old Testament, whether the sense alone agrees or the wording as well.

He who loves father or mother more than me is not worthy of me, and the rest. Because he had said before: I have not come to send peace but a sword, and to divide men against father and mother and mother-in-law, lest anyone should put filial devotion before religion, he went on, saying: He who loves father or mother more than me; and in the Song of Songs we read: Set charity in order in me. This order is necessary in every affection. After God love your father, love your mother, love your children; but if a necessity comes such that the love of parents and children is weighed against the love of God, and both cannot be kept, hatred of one's own is devotion toward God. So he did not forbid loving father or mother, but pointedly added: He who loves father or mother more than me.

And he who does not take up his cross and follow me is not worthy of me. In another Gospel it is written: He who has not taken up his cross daily. Lest we think that ardor of faith shown once can suffice, the cross must always be carried, so that we may show that we always love Christ.

Whoever receives you receives me, and whoever receives me receives him who sent me. A most beautiful order. He sends them to preach, teaches that dangers are not to be feared, subordinates affection to religion; he had taken away gold above, he had shaken bronze out of the belt. Hard is the condition of the evangelists. Whence, then, their expenses, whence the necessities of food? He tempers the austerity of the commands with the hope of the promises: Whoever receives, he says, you receives me, and whoever receives me receives him who sent me, so that in receiving the apostles each of the believers might think that he had received Christ.

Whoever receives a prophet in the name of a prophet will receive a prophet's reward, and the rest. He who receives a prophet as a prophet and understands him as speaking of things to come, he will receive a prophet's reward. Therefore the Jews, understanding the prophets carnally, will not receive the reward of the prophets. Alternatively: in every profession darnel is mixed with the wheat. He had said before: Whoever receives you receives me, and whoever receives me receives him who sent me. He had urged the disciples to receive their teachers. The believers could have an unspoken answer: then we must also receive false prophets and Judas the traitor, and supply them with sustenance. Providing against this beforehand, the Lord says that it is not persons who are to be received but names, and that those who receive do not lose the reward, even though the one who was received was unworthy.

And whoever gives one of these least ones a cup of cold water to drink, only in the name of a disciple, amen I say to you, he will not lose his reward. We read in the prophet David: To make excuses in sins, because many put forward, as it were, just pretexts for their sins, so that what they commit by will they may seem to sin by necessity. The Lord, the searcher of heart and reins, beholds the future thoughts in each one. He had said: Whoever receives you receives me. But many false prophets and false preachers could hinder this command. He applied a remedy to this stumbling block too, saying: Whoever receives a just man in the name of a just man will receive a just man's reward. Again, another could plead an excuse and say: I am prevented by poverty; the slenderness of my means holds me back, so that I cannot be hospitable. This excuse too he washed away with a very light command, that we should offer a cup of cold water with our whole heart: cold, he says, water not hot, lest in the case of hot water too poverty and a shortage of firewood should be sought as a pretext. Something of the kind the Apostle too, as we have said already, commands the Galatians: Let him who is catechized share with him who catechizes him in all good things, and he exhorts disciples to the refreshment of their teachers; and since anyone could plead poverty and evade the command, before such a one could put it forward he resolves the impending question, saying: Do not be deceived; God is not mocked; for what a man sows, that he will also reap; and the sense is: in vain do you plead want when your conscience holds something else; you can deceive me as I exhort you, but know that however much you sow, so much you will also reap.

Book Two

Matthew 11:2-30

Now John, when he had heard in chains of the works of Christ, sending two of his disciples, said to him: Are you he who is to come, or do we look for another? He does not ask as one who does not know, for he himself pointed him out to the others who did not know, saying: Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world, and he had heard the voice of the Father thundering: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased, but just as the Savior asks where Lazarus has been laid, so that those who were showing the place of the tomb might at least in this way be prepared for faith and see the dead man rising, so John too, about to be killed by Herod, sends his disciples to Christ, so that through this occasion, seeing the signs and mighty works, they might believe in him and, while their master asked, learn for themselves. That the disciples of John were proud against the Lord and had something of a biting spirit from spite and envy, the earlier question also showed, as the evangelist relates: Then the disciples of John came to him, saying: Why do we and the Pharisees fast often, but your disciples do not fast? and elsewhere: Master, he to whom you bore witness at the Jordan, behold, his disciples baptize, and more come to him, as if they said: We are deserted, here there is scarcity, to him the crowd runs together.

Are you he who is to come, or do we look for another? He does not say: Are you he who has come, but Are you he who is to come; and the sense is: Send me word, since I am about to go down to the lower world, whether I ought to announce you to those below as well, I who have announced you to those above, or whether it is not fitting for the Son of God to taste death, and you will send another for these mysteries.

Jesus answered and said to them: Go and report to John what you have heard and seen: the blind see, the lame walk, lepers are cleansed, the deaf hear, the dead rise. John had asked through his disciples: Are you he who is to come, or do we look for another? Christ points to signs, answering not what he had been asked but the stumbling block of the messengers: Go, he says, and tell John the signs that you see, the blind seeing, the lame walking, and the rest, and what is no less than these: the poor have the Gospel preached to them; either the poor in spirit or at any rate the poor in wealth, so that in the preaching there is no difference between the noble and the lowborn, between the rich and the needy. These things prove the rigor of the master, these the truth of the teacher, since with him everyone who can be saved is equal. But as for what he says:

And blessed is he who has not been scandalized in me, he strikes at the go-betweens, as will be shown in what follows.

When they had gone away, Jesus began to say to the crowds about John: What did you go out into the desert for? To see a reed shaken by the wind? But what did you go out for? To see a man clothed in soft garments? and the rest. If the previous sentence had been uttered against John, as most people think, in his saying: Blessed is he who has not been scandalized in me, how is John now proclaimed with such praises? But it is because the crowd standing around did not know the mystery of the question and thought that John was in doubt about Christ, whom he himself had pointed out: so that they might understand that John had asked not for himself but for his disciples. What, he says, did you go out into the desert for? Was it for this, that you might see a man like a reed, who is carried about by every wind and, from lightness of mind, would be in doubt about the one whom he had earlier proclaimed? Or perhaps he is driven by the goads of envy against me, and his preaching pursues vain glory, so that he may seek gain from it? Why should he desire riches so as to abound in feasts? He feeds on locusts and wild honey. Or was it to be clothed in soft garments? Camels' hair is his covering. Food and clothing of this kind are received into the lodging of a prison, and the preaching of truth has such a dwelling. But those who are flatterers and pursue gain and seek riches and abound in delights and are clothed in soft garments, they are in the houses of kings. From this it is shown that a rigorous life and austere preaching ought to avoid the courts of kings and shun the palaces of soft men.

But what did you go out for? To see a prophet? Yes, I tell you, and more than a prophet. John is greater than the other prophets in this, that the one whom they had proclaimed was to come, he pointed out with his finger as having come, saying: Behold the Lamb of God, behold him who takes away the sins of the world. And because to the prophetic privilege there was added also the Baptist's reward, that he should baptize his own Lord, he therefore brings in an αὔξησιν (increase) of his merits, producing the testimony from Malachi in which he is even proclaimed an angel. But let us not think that John is called an angel here by a sharing of nature, but by the dignity of his office, that is, a messenger, because he announced the Lord who was to come.

Amen I say to you: There has not arisen among those born of women one greater than John the Baptist. Among those born, he says, of women. He is therefore preferred to those men who were born of women and of union with a man, and not to him who was born of a virgin and the Holy Spirit. And yet in saying: There has not arisen among those born of women one greater than John the Baptist, he did not prefer John to the other prophets and patriarchs and to all men, but made the others equal to John. For it does not straightway follow that, if others are not greater than he, he is greater than the others, but rather that he has equality with the other saints.

But he who is less in the kingdom of heaven is greater than he. Many want this to be understood of the Savior: that he who is less in time is greater in dignity. But let us understand simply that every saint who is already with God is greater than he who still stands in the battle. For it is one thing to possess the crown of victory, another still to fight in the battle line. Some want to take the lowest angel ministering to the Lord in heaven as better than any foremost man who lives on earth.

From the days of John the Baptist until now the kingdom of heaven suffers violence. If John, as we said above, was the first to announce repentance to the peoples, saying: Repent, for the kingdom of heaven has drawn near, it follows that from his days the kingdom of heaven suffers violence, and the violent plunder it. For it is great violence that we, born on earth, should seek the seat of heaven and possess by virtue what we did not hold by nature.

For all the prophets and the law prophesied until John. Not that he excludes prophets after John (for we read in the Acts of the Apostles that both Agabus prophesied and the four virgin daughters of Philip), but that the law and the prophets whom we read in writing, whatever they prophesied, foretold of the Lord. When therefore it is said: All the prophets and the law prophesied until John, the time of Christ is shown, in that the one whom they said was to come, John showed had come.

If you are willing to receive it, he is Elijah who is to come. He who has ears to hear, let him hear. That this saying: If you are willing to receive it, he is Elijah, is mystical and needs understanding, the Lord's following word shows, saying: He who has ears to hear, let him hear. For if the sense were plain and the meaning manifest, what need was there for us to be prepared for understanding it? John, then, is called Elijah not according to the foolish philosophers and certain heretics who introduce a μετεμψύχωσιν (transmigration of souls), but because, according to another testimony of the Gospel, he came in the spirit and power of Elijah and had either the same grace or the same measure of the Holy Spirit. Moreover, the austerity of life and the rigor of mind of Elijah and John are equal. The one was in the desert, the other in the desert; the one was girded with a leather belt, and the other had a similar girdle; the one, because he rebuked King Ahab and Jezebel for impiety, was forced to flee, the other, because he rebuked the unlawful marriage of Herod and Herodias, is beheaded. There are those who think that John is called Elijah for this reason: that, as at the second coming of the Savior, according to Malachi, Elijah is to go before and announce the judge who is to come, so John did at the first coming, and each is a messenger, either of the first coming of the Lord or of the second.

But to what shall I liken this generation? It is like children sitting in the forum, who call out to their companions and say: We sang to you, and you did not dance; we lamented to you, and you did not mourn. For John came neither eating nor drinking, and they say: He has a demon. The Son of Man came eating and drinking, and they say: Look, a gluttonous man and a drinker of wine, a friend of tax collectors and sinners. And wisdom has been justified by her children. To children sitting in the forum and calling out and saying to their companions: We sang to you, and you did not dance; we lamented, and you did not mourn, the generation of the Jews is compared, as Scripture says: What shall I reckon this generation to be like? It is like children sitting in the forum, and the rest. We are not, therefore, granted a free understanding and an allegorical interpretation that ranges at will, but whatever we are going to say about the children must be referred to the likeness of the generation. These children who sit in the forum are those of whom Isaiah speaks: Behold, I and my children whom God has given me; and in Psalm 18 (19): The testimony of God is faithful, giving wisdom to little ones; and elsewhere: Out of the mouth of infants and sucklings you have perfected praise. These children, then, sat in the forum, or ἐν ἀγορᾷ, as it is said more expressively in Greek, where many things are for sale. And because the people of the Jews would not listen, they did not merely speak to it but cried out with full throats: We sang to you, and you did not dance: we urged you to do good works to our song and to dance to our pipe, as David too danced before the ark of the Lord, and you would not; we have lamented, and we urged you to repentance, and you would not do even this, spurning both kinds of preaching, both the exhortation to virtues and the repentance after sins. Nor is it surprising if you have despised the twofold way of salvation, when you have spurned fasting and fullness alike. If fasting pleases you, why did John displease you? If fullness, why the Son of Man? Of these you called the one a man with a demon, the other a glutton and a drunkard. Therefore, because you would not receive either discipline, wisdom has been justified by her children, that is, the dispensation and the doctrine of God; and I, who am the power of God and the wisdom of God, have been proved by my apostles, my children, to have acted justly, to whom the Father has revealed what he had hidden from the wise and from those who are prudent in their own eyes. In certain Gospels it reads: Wisdom has been justified by her works. For wisdom does not seek the testimony of the voice but of works.

Then he began to reproach the cities in which very many of his mighty works had been done, because they had not repented. The reproach of the cities of Chorazin and Bethsaida and Capernaum is set out by the title of this chapter: namely, that he reproached them for this reason, because after very many mighty works and signs had been done they did not repent.

Woe to you, Chorazin; woe to you, Bethsaida; for if the mighty works that have been done in you had been done in Tyre and Sidon, they would long ago have repented in sackcloth and ashes. Nevertheless I say to you: it will be more tolerable for Tyre and Sidon on the day of judgment than for you. Chorazin and Bethsaida, cities of Galilee, are lamented by the Savior because after so many signs and mighty works they did not repent, and Tyre and Sidon, cities given over to idolatry and vices, are preferred to them. And they are preferred for this reason, that Tyre and Sidon had trampled only the natural law, whereas these, after transgressing the natural and the written law, also made light of the signs that had been done among them. We ask where it is written that the Lord did signs in Chorazin and Bethsaida. Above we read: And he went about all the cities and villages, healing every infirmity, and the rest. Among the other cities and little villages, therefore, we must suppose that the Lord did signs in Chorazin and Bethsaida too.

And you, Capernaum, will you be exalted even to heaven? You will go down even to hell. In another copy we have found: And you, Capernaum, who have been exalted even to heaven, you will go down even to the lower regions, and the understanding is twofold: either you will go down to the lower regions because you resisted my preaching most proudly, or because, having been exalted even to heaven by my lodging with you and by my signs and mighty works, possessing so great a privilege, you are struck with greater punishments, since you would not believe even these.

For if the mighty works that have been done in you had been done in Sodom, perhaps it would have remained until this day. The prudent reader may ask and say: If Tyre and Sidon and Sodom could have repented at the preaching of the Savior and the miracles of his signs, they are not at fault for not having believed; rather, the fault of silence lies in him who would not preach to those who would have repented. To this the answer is easy and plain: we do not know the judgments of God and are ignorant of the mysteries of each of his dispensations. The Lord had purposed not to go beyond the borders of Judea, so as not to give the Pharisees and the priests a just occasion for persecution. Hence he also commanded the apostles before the passion: Do not go off into the way of the Gentiles, and do not enter the cities of the Samaritans. Chorazin and Bethsaida, therefore, are condemned because they would not believe the Lord when he was present; Tyre and Sidon are justified because they believed his apostles. Do not ask about the times when you behold the salvation of believers. But in Capernaum, whose name is interpreted most beautiful village, unbelieving Jerusalem is condemned, to which it is said through Ezekiel: Sodom has been justified by you.

At that time Jesus answered and said: I confess to you, Father, Lord of heaven and earth. Confession does not always signify repentance but also thanksgiving, as we very often read in the psalms. Let those hear who slanderously claim that the Savior was not born but created, that he calls him his Father, but Lord of heaven and earth. For if he too is a creature, and a creature can call its maker Father, it was foolish not to call him likewise Lord or Father both of himself and of heaven and earth.

Because you have hidden these things from the wise and prudent and have revealed them to little ones. He gives thanks and exults in the Father because he has opened to the apostles the mysteries of his coming, of which the scribes and Pharisees were ignorant, who seem wise to themselves and prudent in their own sight. Wisdom has been justified by her children.

Yes, Father, for so it was pleasing before you. He speaks to the Father with the feeling of one who coaxes, that the kindness begun toward the apostles may be completed.

All things have been handed over to me by my Father. Understand mystically both the Father who hands over and the Son who receives. Otherwise, if we choose to think according to our frailty: when he who received begins to have, he who gave will begin not to have. And by the all things handed over to him are to be understood not heaven and earth and the elements and the other things that he himself made and founded, but those who through the Son have access to the Father and, rebels before, afterwards began to perceive God.

And no one knows the Son except the Father, nor does anyone know the Father except the Son and he to whom the Son wishes to reveal him. Let Eunomius blush, who boasts that he has as much knowledge of the Father and the Son as they have of each other. But suppose he argues from this and consoles his madness because there follows: and he to whom the Son wishes to reveal him. It is one thing to know what one knows by equality of nature, another by the condescension of one who reveals.

Come to me, all you who labor and are burdened, and I will refresh you. That the burdens of sin are heavy, both the prophet Zechariah testifies, saying that iniquity sits upon a talent of lead, and the psalmist laments: My iniquities have grown heavy upon me. Or else he invites to the grace of the Gospel those who were weighed down by the most heavy yoke of the law.

For my yoke is sweet and my burden light. How is the Gospel lighter than the law, when in the law murder, in the Gospel anger is condemned? On what reckoning is the grace of the Gospel easier, when in the law adultery, in the Gospel desire is punished? In the law there are many precepts which the Apostle teaches most fully cannot be fulfilled. In the law works are required, and whoever has done them shall live in them; in the Gospel the will is sought, which, even if it has not had its effect, still does not lose its reward. The Gospel commands what we are able to do, namely that we should not desire: this is within our choice; the law, while it does not punish the will, punishes the effect, that you should not commit adultery. Imagine some virgin prostituted in a persecution. Under the Gospel, because she does not sin in will, she is received as a virgin; in the law she is rejected as defiled.

Matthew 12

At that time Jesus went through the sown fields on the Sabbath, and his disciples, being hungry, began to pluck ears of grain and to eat. As we read also in another evangelist, because of the excessive importunity they did not even have room to eat, and so, being men, they were hungry. But that they rub the ears of the crops in their hands and so relieve their want of food is a sign of a more austere life, of men who seek not prepared banquets but simple foods.

But the Pharisees, seeing it, said to him: Behold, your disciples are doing what it is not lawful for them to do on the Sabbaths. Note that the first apostles of the Savior destroy the letter of the Sabbath, against the Ebionites, who, though they receive the other apostles, reject Paul as a transgressor of the law.

But he said to them: Have you not read what David did when he was hungry, and those who were with him, how he entered the house of God and ate the loaves of the presentation, which it was not lawful for him to eat, nor for those who were with him, but for the priests alone? To refute the false charge of the Pharisees he recalls the old history, when David, fleeing Saul, came to Nob and, received by Ahimelech the priest, asked for food; and he, since he had no common loaves, gave him consecrated ones, which it was not lawful to eat except for the priests and Levites alone, and he asked only this, whether the young men were clean from women; and when David answered, since yesterday and the day before, he did not hesitate to give the loaves, judging it better (as the prophet says: I desire mercy and not sacrifice) to free men from the danger of hunger than to offer sacrifice to God. For the salvation of men is a victim pleasing to God. So the Lord sets this against them and says: If David too is holy, and Ahimelech the high priest is not blamed by you, but both transgressed the command of the law with an acceptable excuse, and hunger is the cause, why do you not approve in the apostles the same hunger that you approve in the others? Although even in this there is a great difference: these rub ears of grain in their hands on the Sabbath; those ate the levitical loaves, and the days of the new moons were added to the solemnity of the Sabbath, on which, being sought at the banquet, he fled from the royal court. Note that neither David nor his young men received the loaves of the presentation before they had answered that they were clean from women.

Or have you not read in the law that on the Sabbaths the priests in the temple violate the Sabbath and are without guilt? You slander my disciples, he says, because they rubbed ears of grain as they passed through the crops, and did this under the compulsion of hunger, when you yourselves violate the Sabbath in the temple, slaughtering victims, killing bulls, burning up holocausts with fire upon a pile of wood; and, on the testimony of another Gospel, circumcising little children on the Sabbath, so that, while you desire to keep another law, you destroy the Sabbath. But the laws of God are never contrary to one another. And prudently, where his disciples could be charged with transgression, he says that they followed the examples of David and Ahimelech; but the real violation of the Sabbath, one without the pretext of necessity, he turns back upon the very men who had made the false charge.

But I say to you that one greater than the temple is here. The word is to be read not as a pronoun but as an adverb of place, meaning that the place which holds the Lord of the temple is greater than the temple.

But if you knew what this is: I desire mercy and not sacrifice, you would never have condemned the innocent. What I desire mercy and not sacrifice, means, we have said above; but what follows, you would never have condemned the innocent, is to be understood of the apostles, and the sense is: If you approve the mercy of Ahimelech, because he refreshed David, who was in peril from hunger, and his young men, why do you condemn my disciples, who have done nothing of the kind?

And when he had passed on from there, he came into their synagogue. And behold, a man having a withered hand. This man, who is healed in the synagogue, is the thirteenth. And it is to be noted that the hand was withered not on the road and out of doors, but in the assembly of the Jews.

And they questioned him, saying: Is it lawful to heal on the Sabbaths? so that they might accuse him. Since he had excused by an acceptable example the destruction of the Sabbath which the Pharisees charged against the disciples, they wish to slander him himself, and they ask whether it is lawful to heal on the Sabbaths, so that, if he did not heal, they might accuse him of cruelty or of weakness, and if he healed, of transgression.

But he said to them: What man of you will there be who has one sheep, and if it falls into a pit on the Sabbaths, will he not take hold of it and lift it out? How much better, then, is a man than a sheep! So it is lawful to do good on the Sabbaths. He so solved the question put to him as to condemn the questioners of avarice. If you, he says, hasten on the Sabbath to pull out a sheep or any other animal that falls into a pit, looking out not for the animal but for your own avarice, how much more ought I to set free a man, who is much better than a sheep!

Then he said to the man: Stretch out your hand. And he stretched it out, and it was restored to health like the other. In the Gospel which the Nazarenes and the Ebionites use, which we recently translated from the Hebrew language into Greek, and which is called by most the authentic Matthew, this man who has the withered hand is described as a mason, begging for help in words of this kind: I was a mason, seeking my living with my hands; I beg you, Jesus, to restore my health to me, so that I may not shamefully beg for food. Until the coming of the Savior the withered hand was in the synagogue of the Jews, and the works of God were not done in it; after he came upon earth, the right hand was given back in the apostles who believed and was restored to its former work.

But the Pharisees went out and took counsel against him, how they might destroy him. That they lay plots against the Lord, envy is the cause. For what had he done to provoke the Pharisees to kill him? Why, that a man had stretched out his hand. For which of the Pharisees does not stretch out his hand on the Sabbath day, carrying food, holding out a cup, and the other things that are necessary for living? If, then, to stretch out the hand and to lift up food or drink on the Sabbath is no crime, why do they charge in another what they themselves are convicted of doing, especially since this mason carried nothing of the kind, but at the Lord's command only stretched out his hand?

But Jesus, knowing it, withdrew from there. Knowing their plots, that they wished to destroy their Savior, he withdrew from there, so as to take away from the Pharisees an occasion for impiety against himself.

Behold my servant whom I have chosen, my beloved, and the rest. This is said through the prophet Isaiah in the person of the Father: I will put my Spirit upon him. The Spirit is put not upon the Word of God and upon the Only-begotten who came forth from the bosom of the Father, but upon him of whom it was said: Behold my servant.

Nor will anyone hear his voice in the streets. Wide indeed and spacious is the way that leads to perdition, and many enter by it. Who are the many? Those who do not hear the voice of the Savior, because they are not on the narrow way but on the spacious one.

A battered reed he will not break, and smoldering flax he will not put out. He who does not hold out a hand to a sinner and does not carry the burden of his brother, he breaks the battered reed. And he who despises the small spark of faith in little ones, he puts out the smoldering flax. Christ did neither of these things; for he had come for this, to save what had perished.

Then there was brought to him one who had a demon, blind and mute, and he healed him, so that he spoke and saw. Three signs were performed at once in one man: the blind man sees, the mute man speaks, the man possessed by a demon is set free. This was then indeed done in the flesh, but it is also fulfilled daily in the lives of believers, so that, once the demon has been driven out, they first behold the light of faith, and then mouths that were formerly silent are loosed in the praises of God.

But Jesus, knowing their thoughts, said to them: Every kingdom divided within itself will be laid waste. The crowds were amazed and confessed that he who did such great signs was the son of David; but the Pharisees ascribed the works of God to the prince of the demons; and the Lord answered them not according to what they said but according to what they thought, so that even thus they might be compelled to believe in the power of him who saw the hidden things of the heart.

If Satan casts out Satan, he is divided against himself; how then will his kingdom stand? A kingdom and a city divided against itself cannot endure; but as small things grow by concord, so the greatest fall apart by discord. If, then, Satan fights against himself, and demon is the enemy of demon, the consummation of the world ought already to have come, and the adverse powers would have no place in it, whose war among themselves is the peace of men. But if you think, O scribes and Pharisees, that the withdrawal of the demons is obedience to their prince, so that by a fraudulent pretense they may delude ignorant men, what can you say of the healings of bodies which the Lord performed? It is another matter if you ascribe to the demons the infirmities of the limbs too, and the signal marks of spiritual mighty works.

If I cast out demons by Beelzebub, by whom do your sons cast them out? Therefore they shall be your judges. By the sons of the Jews he means either, after the custom, the exorcists of that nation, or the apostles, born of their stock. If the exorcists, who used to cast out demons at the invocation of God, he constrains them with a prudent question, so that they confess it to be the work of the Holy Spirit. If, he says, the expulsion of demons by your sons is ascribed to God and not to demons, why should the same work in me not also have the same cause? Therefore they shall be your judges, not by power but by comparison, since they ascribe the expulsion of demons to God, and you to Beelzebub, the prince of the demons. But if it was said of the apostles, which is rather what we ought to understand, they will be their judges because they will sit on twelve thrones, judging the twelve tribes of Israel.

But if I cast out demons by the Spirit of God, then the kingdom of God has come upon you. In Luke we read this passage written thus: But if I cast out demons by the finger of God. This is the finger which even the magicians, who performed signs against Moses and Aaron, confess, saying: This is the finger of God, the finger by which the tablets of stone were written on Mount Sinai. If, then, the hand and the arm of God is the Son, and his finger the Holy Spirit, the substance of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit is one; let not the inequality of the members cause you to stumble, since the unity of the body edifies. The kingdom of God has come upon you: he means either himself, of whom it is written in another place: The kingdom of God is within you, and: In your midst stands one whom you do not know, or else that kingdom which both John and the Lord himself had preached: Repent, for the kingdom of heaven has drawn near. There is also a third kingdom, that of holy Scripture, which is taken away from the Jews and given to a nation that produces its fruits.

Or how can anyone enter the house of the strong man and plunder his vessels, unless he first binds the strong man, and then he will plunder his house? We must not be complacent: our adversary is shown to be strong even by the words of the victor; his house is the world, which is set in the evil one, not by the dignity of the Creator but by the greatness of the offender; his vessels we once were. The strong man has been bound and fastened down in Tartarus and crushed by the foot of the Lord, and, the seats of the tyrant having been plundered, captivity has been led captive.

He who is not with me is against me, and he who does not gather with me scatters. Let no one think that this was said of heretics and schismatics, although it can also be understood so, as something over and above; but from what follows and from the texture of the discourse it is referred to the devil, in that the works of the Savior cannot be compared with the works of Beelzebub: he desires to hold the souls of men captive, the Lord to set them free; he preaches idols, this one the knowledge of the one God; he draws men to vices, this one calls them back to virtues. How then can those whose works are opposite have concord between them?

Whoever speaks a word against the Son of Man, it will be forgiven him; but whoever speaks against the Holy Spirit, it will not be forgiven him, neither in this age nor in the one to come. And how do some of our people receive bishops and presbyters back into their rank after blasphemy of the Holy Spirit, when the Savior says that every sin and blasphemy is forgiven to men, but that those who have blasphemed against the Holy Spirit are not forgiven, neither in the present time nor in the future; unless perhaps we take that example from Mark the evangelist, who expressed more plainly the causes of such great anger, saying: Because they were saying: He has an unclean spirit. Therefore whoever ascribes the works of the Savior to Beelzebub, the prince of the demons, and says that the Son of God has an unclean spirit, to him the blasphemy will at no time be forgiven. Or else this passage is to be understood thus: He who speaks a word against the Son of Man, scandalized by my flesh and thinking me only a man, because I am the son of a carpenter and have brothers James and Joseph and Jude, and because I am a man who is a glutton and a drinker of wine, such an opinion and blasphemy, although it is not without fault, still has the pardon due to error because of the lowliness of the body; but he who, plainly understanding the works of God, since he cannot deny the mighty work, is goaded by the same envy and slanders them, and says that Christ, the Word of God, and the works of the Holy Spirit are Beelzebub, to him it will not be forgiven, neither in the present age nor in the one to come.

Either make the tree good and its fruit good, or make the tree bad and its fruit bad. For the tree is known by its fruits. He binds them with a syllogism which the Greeks call ἄφυκτον, and which we can call inescapable, which, when men are questioned, shuts them in on this side and on that and presses them with either horn. If, he says, the devil is evil, he cannot do good works; but if the things you see done are good, it follows that it is not the devil who does them. For it cannot be that good should arise from evil, or evil from good. But what follows,

Brood of vipers, how can you speak good things, since you are evil, shows that they are a bad tree and bear such fruits, overflowing with blasphemy, as are the seeds of the devil which they have.

The good man out of the good treasure brings forth good things, and the evil man out of the evil treasure brings forth evil things. Either he shows from what kind of treasure the Jews themselves, blaspheming the Lord, bring forth their blasphemies, or else the saying clings to the earlier question: that as a good man cannot bring forth evil things, nor an evil man good things, so Christ cannot do evil works, nor the devil good ones.

But I say to you that every idle word that men speak, they will render account for it on the day of judgment.
