This too clings to what precedes, and the sense is: If an idle word, which in no way builds up the hearers, is not without danger to the one who speaks it, and on the day of judgment each one will have to render account of his speech, how much more will you, who slander the works of the Holy Spirit and say that I cast out demons by Beelzebub, the prince of the demons, have to render account of your slander! An idle word is one that is spoken without profit both to the speaker and to the hearer: if, leaving serious matters aside, we speak of frivolous things and tell old tales. But he who rehearses buffooneries, and loosens his mouth in guffaws, and utters anything shameful, he will be held guilty not of an idle word but of a criminal one.

Then some of the scribes and Pharisees answered him, saying: We want to see a sign from you. They ask for a sign as though the signs they had seen were not signs. But in another evangelist what they ask for is set out more fully: We want to see a sign from you from heaven, they desired either that fire should come down from on high in the manner of Elijah, or that, after the likeness of Samuel, in the summertime thunder should roar, lightning flash and rains pour down; as if they could not slander those things too, and say that they had happened from hidden and various affections of the air. For you who slander the things you see with your eyes, hold in your hand and feel in their benefit, what will you do with things that come from heaven? Surely you will answer that the magicians in Egypt too did many signs from heaven.

He said to them: A perverse and adulterous generation. He did well to say adulterous, because she had left her husband and, according to Ezekiel, had joined herself to many lovers.

Seeks a sign, and a sign will not be given it except the sign of the prophet Jonah. For as Jonah was in the belly of the whale three days and three nights, so will the Son of Man be in the heart of the earth three days and nights. We have discussed this passage more fully in the commentary on the prophet Jonah; to that place, therefore, we refer the diligence of the reader, content to have said this briefly now: that συνεκδοχικῶς (by synecdoche) the whole is understood from the part; not that the Lord spent all three days and three nights in hell, but that in part of the Preparation and of the Lord's day, and the whole day of the Sabbath, three days and as many nights are understood.

The men of Nineveh will rise in the judgment with this generation and will condemn it, not by the power of a sentence but by the example of comparison.

And behold, here is more than Jonah. Here you must take as an adverb of place, not a pronoun. Jonah, according to the LXX translators, preached for three days, I for so long a time; he to the Assyrians, an unbelieving nation, I to the Jews, the people of God; he to foreigners, I to fellow citizens; he spoke with a simple voice, doing no signs, I, doing such great signs, endure the slander of Beelzebub. So more than Jonah is here, that is, at present among you.

The queen of the south will rise in the judgment with this generation and will condemn it, because she came from the ends of the earth to hear the wisdom of Solomon. The queen of the south will condemn the people of the Jews in the same way as the men of Nineveh will condemn unbelieving Israel. Now this is the queen of Sheba, of whom we read in the book of Kings and in Chronicles, who through such great difficulties, leaving her nation and her empire behind, came into Judea to hear the wisdom of Solomon, and brought him many gifts. And in Nineveh and in the queen of Sheba the hidden faith of the nations is preferred to Israel.

But when the unclean spirit has gone out of a man, it walks through dry places seeking rest and does not find it, and the rest. Some think this passage was said of heretics: that the unclean spirit which had formerly dwelt in them when they were Gentiles is cast out at the confession of the true faith, but that afterwards, when they have gone over to heresy and adorned their house with feigned virtues, then the devil, having joined to himself seven other wicked spirits, returns to them and dwells in them, and their last state becomes worse than the first; for heretics are in a much worse condition than Gentiles, because in the latter there is hope of faith, in the former the fight of discord. Although this interpretation displays a certain appeal and color of learning, I do not know whether it has the truth. For from the fact that, once the parable or example is finished, there follows: So shall it be also with this most wicked generation, we are compelled to refer the parable not to heretics and to any men whatever but to the people of the Jews, so that the context of the passage may not drift loosely and vaguely in different directions and be thrown into confusion in the manner of the foolish, but, cohering with itself, may answer either to what precedes or to what follows. The unclean spirit went out from the Jews when they received the law, and it walked through dry places seeking rest for itself. Driven out, that is, from the Jews, it walked through the solitudes of the Gentiles; and when these afterwards believed in the Lord, it, finding no place among the nations, said:

I will return to my former house from which I went out, I go off to the Jews whom I had formerly left.

And coming, it finds the house vacant, swept with brooms. For the temple of the Jews was vacant and did not have Christ as its guest, who says: Rise, let us go from here; and in another place: Your house will be left to you deserted. Since, therefore, they had neither the protection of God nor that of the angels, and were adorned with the superfluous observances of the law and the traditions of the Pharisees, the devil returns to his former seat and, adding to himself the sevenfold number of demons, inhabits his former house, and the later things of that people become worse than the first. For now they are possessed by a much greater number of demons, blaspheming Christ Jesus in their synagogues, than they had been possessed in Egypt before the knowledge of the law; for it is one thing not to believe in one who is to come, and another not to have received him who has come. But understand the sevenfold number joined to the devil either on account of the Sabbath or on account of the number of the Holy Spirit, so that, as in Isaiah the seven spirits of the virtues are told to have descended upon the rod from the root of Jesse and upon the flower that rises up from the root, so, on the contrary, the number of the vices is consecrated in the devil.

While he was still speaking to the crowds, behold, his mother and his brothers were standing outside, seeking to speak to him. And someone said to him: Behold, your mother and your brothers are standing outside seeking you. The Lord was occupied in the work of the word, in the teaching of the peoples, in the duty of preaching; his mother and brothers come and stand outside and desire to speak to him. Then someone announces to the Savior that his mother and brothers are standing outside seeking him. It seems to me that this man who brings the news does not announce it by chance and in simplicity, but is laying a trap, to see whether he would prefer flesh and blood to the spiritual work. Hence the Lord too scorned to go out, not as though he were denying his mother and brothers, but so as to answer the one laying the trap:

Stretching out his hand toward his disciples he said: Behold my mother and my brothers. These are my mother, who daily bring me to birth in the souls of believers; these are my brothers, who do the works of my Father. So he did not, as Marcion and Mani hold, deny his mother, so as to be thought born of a phantasm, but he preferred the apostles to kinship, so that we too, in weighing our love, may prefer the spirit to the flesh. Behold, your mother and your brothers are standing outside seeking you. Some suspect that the brothers of the Lord are sons of Joseph by another wife, following the ravings of the apocrypha and inventing a certain little woman Escha. But we, as is contained in the book which we wrote Against Helvidius, understand the brothers of the Lord to be not sons of Joseph but cousins of the Savior, the children of Mary, the Lord's maternal aunt, who is said to be the mother of James the less, and of Joses and Jude, whom in another place of the Gospel we read called the brothers of the Lord. And all Scripture shows that cousins are called brothers. Let us also speak in another way. The Savior speaks to the crowds; inside he instructs the nations. His mother and brothers, that is, the synagogue and the people of the Jews, stand outside and desire to enter, and become unworthy of his discourse; and when they have asked and sought and sent a messenger, they receive the answer that they are of free will and can enter, if they too were willing to believe; yet they will not be able to enter unless they have asked others.

Matthew 13

On that day Jesus, going out of the house, sat beside the sea, and great crowds were gathered to him. The people could not enter the house of Jesus, nor be where the apostles were hearing the mysteries; therefore the compassionate and merciful Lord goes out of his house and sat beside the sea of this world, so that the crowds might be gathered to him and hear on the shore what they did not deserve to hear within.

So that, going up into a little boat, he sat, and the whole crowd stood on the shore. Jesus is in the midst of the waves; he is beaten by the sea on this side and on that, and, secure in his majesty, he makes his little boat draw near to the land; but the people, in no way enduring danger nor surrounded by temptations which they could not bear, stand on the shore with a fixed step to hear what is said.

And he spoke many things to them in parables, saying. The crowd is not of one mind but of wills differing in each person. Hence he speaks to it in many parables, so that according to their various wills they might receive different teachings. And it should be noted that he did not speak all things to them in parables, but many. For if he had said everything in parables, the people would have gone away without profit. He mixes the plain with the obscure, so that through the things they understand they may be roused to a knowledge of the things they do not understand.

Behold, he who sows went out to sow. He was within, he was staying at home, he was speaking mysteries to the disciples. So he who sows the word of God went out of his house to sow among the crowds. Now it is signified that this sower who sows is the Son of God and of the Father, sowing the word among the peoples. And at the same time note that this is the first parable that has been set down with its own interpretation; and we must beware, wherever the Lord expounds his own sayings and, when asked by the disciples, discourses within, lest we wish to understand anything else, or anything more or less, than has been expounded by him.

Some fell beside the way, and the birds came and ate them; and others fell on rocky places, where they did not have much earth, and the rest. Valentinus takes up this parable to prove his heresy, bringing in the notion that there are three natures, the spiritual, the animal and the earthly, whereas here there are four: one beside the way, another rocky, the third full of thorns, the fourth good ground. We put off its interpretation for a little, wishing with the disciples to hear in secret what is said:

He who has ears to hear, let him hear. We are roused to the understanding of what is said whenever we are admonished by these words.

And the disciples came and said to him. It must be asked how the disciples come to him when Jesus is sitting in the ship, unless perhaps we are given to understand that some time before they had gone up into the ship with him and, standing there, had inquired about the interpretation of the parable.

He who has, it will be given to him, and he will abound; but he who does not have, even what he has will be taken from him. It is not by an equality of judgment that something is added to those who have, and that from those who do not have even what they seem to have is taken away; rather, to the apostles, who have faith in Christ, something is granted, even if they have somewhat fewer virtues, while from the Jews, who did not believe in the Son of God, it is taken away, even if they possess something through the good of nature. For those who do not have the head of wisdom cannot understand anything wisely.

Therefore I speak to them in parables, because seeing they do not see and hearing they do not hear. He says these things of those who stand on the shore and are separated from Jesus, and, with the sound of the waves roaring, do not hear clearly what is said, and in them is fulfilled the prophecy of Isaiah: By hearing you shall hear and shall not understand, and seeing you shall see and shall not see. These things were prophesied of the crowds that stand on the shore and do not deserve to hear the word of God. Let us therefore also come to Jesus with the disciples and ask him for the explanation of the parable, lest with the crowds we seem to have ears and eyes in vain.

For the heart of this people has grown fat, and with their ears they have heard heavily, and they have closed their eyes, and the rest. He gives the reasons why seeing they do not see and hearing they do not hear: because it has grown fat, he says, the heart of this people, and with their ears they have heard heavily; and lest perhaps we should think that fatness of heart and heaviness of ears belong to nature, not to the will, he adds the fault of free choice and says: They have closed their eyes, lest at any time they should see with their eyes and hear with their ears and understand with their heart and be converted, and I should heal them. In parables, therefore, and in an enigma do they hear who, with their eyes closed, do not wish to see the truth.

But blessed are your eyes, because they see, and your ears, because they hear. Had we not read above that the hearers were roused to understanding when the Savior said, He who has ears to hear, let him hear, we would think that the eyes and ears which now receive the beatitude are to be understood as those of the flesh. But to me those eyes seem blessed which can know the mysteries of Christ, and which Jesus commanded to be lifted up on high so that they might behold the white harvest fields; and those ears blessed of which Isaiah speaks: The Lord has given me an ear.

Amen indeed I say to you that many prophets and just men desired to see what you see, and did not see it, and to hear what you hear, and did not hear it. There seems to be, contrary to this passage, what is said elsewhere: Abraham longed to see my day, and he saw it and rejoiced. But he did not say: all prophets and just men desired to see what you see, but many. Among many it may be that some saw and others did not see, although in this too the interpretation is dangerous, since we may seem to make some distinction or other among the merits of the saints. Therefore Abraham saw in an enigma, saw in a form; but you hold and have your Lord in the present, and you question him at your will, and you eat with him.

Everyone who hears the word of the kingdom and does not understand. Putting this first, he urges us to listen more diligently to what is said.

The evil one comes and snatches what has been sown in his heart. The evil one snatches the good seed. And at the same time understand that it was sown in the heart, and that the different kinds of ground are the souls of believers.

But when tribulation and persecution have come because of the word, he is at once scandalized. Pay attention to what is said: he is at once scandalized. There is therefore some difference between the one who is compelled by many tribulations and punishments to deny Christ and the one who at the first persecution is immediately scandalized and falls.

But he who sows among thorns, this is he who hears the word, and the care of this world and the deceitfulness of riches chokes the word, and it is made fruitless. It seems to me that what is said to Adam according to the letter, Among thorns and thistles you shall eat your bread, also signifies mystically that whoever gives himself to the pleasures of the world and the cares of this world eats the heavenly bread and the true food among thorns. And he aptly added: the deceitfulness of riches chokes the word. For riches are alluring, doing one thing and promising another. Their possession is slippery, since they are carried about hither and thither, and with unsteady step they either desert those who have them or stuff those who do not have them. Hence the Lord also asserts that the rich enter the kingdom of heaven with difficulty, since riches choke the word of God and soften the rigor of the virtues.

But he who was sown in good ground, this is he who hears the word and understands and bears fruit. Just as in the bad ground there were three different kinds, the wayside and the rocky and the thorny places, so in the good ground there is a threefold diversity: of the hundredfold, the sixtyfold and the thirtyfold fruit. And in the one as in the other it is not the substance that is changed but the will, and it is the hearts of unbelievers as well as of believers that receive the seed. The evil one comes, he says, and snatches what has been sown in his heart, and in the second and third place: this is he, he says, who hears the word. In the exposition of the good ground too, this is he who hears the word. First, therefore, we must hear, then understand, and after understanding render the fruits of the teachings and produce either the hundredfold fruit or the sixtyfold and thirtyfold; of these we spoke more fully in the book Against Jovinian, and now we touch on them briefly, assigning the hundredfold fruit to virgins, the sixtyfold to widows and the continent, the thirtyfold to holy matrimony. Honorable indeed is marriage, and the bed undefiled. Some of our writers refer the hundredfold fruit to the martyrs; but if that is so, the holy partnerships of marriage are shut out from the good fruit.

Another parable he set before them, saying: The kingdom of heaven has become like a man who sowed good seed in his field; but while men were sleeping, his enemy came and sowed darnel over it in the midst of the wheat, and the rest. This is the second parable set down with its interpretation, not immediately, but explained after other parables have been placed in between. For here it is set forth, and afterwards, the crowds having been dismissed, he comes home, and there come to him his disciples, asking: Explain to us the parable of the darnel of the field, and the rest. For we ought not, through an overhasty desire to understand, to seek the knowledge of it before it is explained by the Lord.

Another parable he set before them, saying. The Lord was sitting in the ship, the crowd was standing on the shore; they heard from afar, the disciples from nearer at hand. He sets before them yet another parable, like a rich householder refreshing his guests with various foods, so that each might take different nourishment according to the nature of his own stomach. Hence also in the earlier parable he did not say a second, but another. For if he had put forward a second, we could not have expected a third; he put forward another so that more might follow.

The kingdom of heaven is like a grain of mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field; which indeed is the least of all seeds, but when it has grown it is greater than all garden herbs and becomes a tree, so that the birds of the sky come and dwell in its branches. Let it not be irksome to the reader if we set out whole parables. For things that are obscure must be discussed more fully, lest by excessive brevity the sense be wrapped up rather than explained. The kingdom of heaven is the preaching of the Gospel and the knowledge of the Scriptures, which leads to life, and of which it is said to the Jews: The kingdom of God shall be taken from you and given to a nation producing its fruits. Like, therefore, in this manner, is the kingdom to a grain of mustard seed, which a man took and sowed in his field. The man who sows in his field is understood by most to be the Savior, because he sows in the souls of believers; by others, a man himself sowing in his own field, that is, in himself and in his heart. Who is this who sows but our mind and our soul, which, receiving the grain of preaching and fostering the seed with the moisture of faith, makes it sprout in the field of its own breast? The preaching of the Gospel is the least of all teachings. For at the first hearing of its doctrine it has no credit of truth, since it preaches a man who is God, Christ dead, and the stumbling block of the cross. Compare a teaching of this kind with the doctrines of the philosophers and with their books, and with the splendor of their eloquence and the composition of their discourses, and you will see how much smaller than the other seeds is the sowing of the Gospel. But those, when they have grown, show nothing pungent, nothing vigorous, nothing vital; the whole of it bubbles up, flabby and withered, into garden herbs and grasses which quickly dry up and collapse. This preaching, however, which seemed small in the beginning, when it has been sown either in the soul of the believer or in the whole world, does not rise up into garden herbs but grows into a tree, so that the birds of the sky, by which we ought to understand either the souls of believers or powers given over to the service of God, come and dwell in its branches. The branches of the Gospel tree that has grown from the grain of mustard seed I take to be the diversities of doctrines, on which each of the birds mentioned above rests. Let us too take up the wings of a dove, so that, flying to higher places, we may dwell in the branches of this tree and make ourselves little nests of teachings, and, fleeing earthly things, hasten toward heavenly ones. Many, reading that the grain of mustard seed is the least of all seeds, and what is said in the Gospel by the disciples, Lord, increase our faith, and the answer given them by the Savior, Amen I say to you: If you had faith like a grain of mustard seed, you would say to this mountain: Move from this place, and it would move, think either that the apostles are asking for a small faith or that the Lord is in doubt about a small faith, whereas the apostle Paul judges faith compared to a grain of mustard seed to be the greatest. For what does he say? If I have such faith that I move mountains, but do not have love, it profits me nothing. Therefore what the Lord said is done by a faith that is compared to a grain of mustard seed, this the Apostle teaches can be done by faith entire.

Another parable he spoke to them: The kingdom of heaven is like leaven, which a woman took and hid in three measures of meal until the whole was leavened. The stomach of men varies: some delight in bitter foods, others in sweet, these in harsher, those in milder. So the Lord, as we have already said above, sets forth different parables, so that according to the variety of the wounds the medicine too may vary. This woman who took the leaven and hid it in three measures of meal until the whole is leavened seems to me to be either the apostolic preaching or the Church, which has been gathered from different nations. She takes the leaven, namely the knowledge and understanding of the Scriptures, and hides it in three measures of meal, so that spirit, soul and body, brought into one, may not be at variance with one another, but, when they have agreed with two and three, may obtain from the Father whatever they have asked. This passage is also explained in another way. We read in Plato, and it is a widespread doctrine of the philosophers, that there are three passions in the human soul: τὸ λογιστικόν, which we can render as the rational; τὸ θυμικόν, which we call full of anger, or irascible; τὸ ἐπιθυμητικόν, which we name concupiscible; and that philosopher thinks that our rational part dwells in the brain, anger in the gall, desire in the liver. And so we too, if we have received the Gospel leaven of the holy Scriptures of which we spoke above, shall have the three passions of the human soul brought into one, so that in reason we possess prudence, in anger hatred against vices, in desire a longing for virtues; and all this will come about through the Gospel teaching which mother Church has furnished to us. I will also give a third understanding, that of certain people, so that the curious reader may choose from several whichever pleases him. They too interpret this woman as the Church, which has mixed the faith of man in three measures of meal, namely belief in the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, and, when it has been leavened into one, leads us not to a threefold God but to the knowledge of one divinity. The three measures of meal also, since there is no different nature in each, draw toward the unity of the substance. A pious sense indeed; but parables and the doubtful understanding of enigmas can never avail for the authority of doctrines. Now the satum is a kind of measure, according to the custom of the province of Palestine, holding one and a half modii. Other things too are said about this parable, but it is not for the present matter to say everything about everything.

All these things Jesus spoke in parables to the crowds, and without parables he did not speak to them. He spoke through parables not to the disciples but to the crowds; and to this day the crowds hear in parables, while the disciples question the Savior at home.

That there might be fulfilled what was spoken through the prophet, saying: I will open my mouth in parables; I will pour forth things hidden from the foundation of the world. This testimony is taken from Psalm 77 (78). I have read in some codices, and the studious reader may perhaps find the same thing, that in the place where we have set down, and where the common edition has, That there might be fulfilled what was spoken through the prophet, saying, there is written: through Isaiah the prophet, saying. Because this was nowhere found in Isaiah, I think it was afterwards removed by prudent men. But it seems to me that originally it was published thus, namely that it was written: through Asaph the prophet, saying (for Psalm 77 (78), from which this testimony is taken, is inscribed with the title of Asaph the prophet), and that the first copyist did not understand Asaph, thought it a copyist's fault, and corrected it to the name of Isaiah, whose name was better known. It should be known, therefore, that in the psalms and hymns and songs of God not only David but also the others whose names stand at their head are to be called prophets, namely Asaph and Jeduthun and Heman the Ezrahite and Ethan and the sons of Korah and the rest whom Scripture mentions. And as for what is said in the person of the Lord, I will open my mouth in parables; I will pour forth things hidden from the foundation of the world, one must consider it more attentively, and it will be found that the going out of Israel from Egypt is described there, and all the signs are narrated that are contained in the history of Exodus. From this we understand that all those things that are written are to be taken parabolically, and that it is not so great a letter that sounds out plainly, but hidden mysteries too. For this is what the Savior promises he will speak, opening his mouth in parables and pouring forth things hidden from the foundation of the world.

Then, having dismissed the crowds, he came into the house. And his disciples came to him, saying: Explain to us the parable of the darnel of the field. Jesus dismisses the crowds and returns home, so that the disciples may come to him and ask in secret what the people neither deserved to hear nor were able to. Explain to us the parable of the darnel.

He answered and said: He who sows the good seed is the Son of Man. He has plainly explained that the field is the world, the sower the Son of Man, the good seed the sons of the kingdom, the darnel the sons of the most wicked one, the sower of the darnel the devil, the harvest the consummation of the world, the reapers the angels; all stumbling blocks are referred to the darnel, the just are reckoned among the sons of the kingdom. Therefore, as I said above, to the things that have been expounded by the Lord we must give our faith, while those that are passed over in silence and left to our understanding are to be touched on briefly. By the men who sleep understand the teachers of the churches; by the servants of the householder, do not take any others than the angels who daily see the face of the Father; the devil, moreover, is called an enemy man because he ceased to be a god, and in Psalm 9 it is written of him: Arise, O Lord, let not man prevail. Therefore let him who is set over the Church not sleep, lest through his negligence the enemy man oversow darnel, that is, the doctrines of heretics. As for what is said, lest perhaps, in gathering the darnel, you root up the wheat along with it, room is given for repentance, and we are warned not to cut off a brother quickly, because it may happen that he who today has been corrupted by a harmful doctrine comes to his senses tomorrow and begins to defend the truth. That too which follows, Let both grow until the harvest, seems to be contrary to that precept, Remove the evil one from your midst, and to the rule that no fellowship is to be had with those who are called brothers and are adulterers and fornicators. For if uprooting is forbidden and patience is to be kept until the harvest, how are some to be cast out from our midst? Between the wheat and the darnel, which we call lolium, as long as it is still in the blade and the stalk has not yet come to the ear, there is great similarity, and in telling them apart the difference is either none or very hard to see. Therefore the Lord warns us beforehand not to pronounce sentence quickly where anything is doubtful, but to reserve the end for God the judge, so that, when the day of judgment has come, he may cast out of the company of the saints not a suspicion of crime but manifest guilt. As for his saying that the bundles of darnel are given over to the fires and the wheat gathered into barns, it is plain that all heretics and hypocrites of the faith are to be burned in the fires of Gehenna, while the saints, who are called wheat, are received into barns, that is, into heavenly mansions.

Then the just will shine like the sun in the kingdom of their Father. In the present world the light of the saints shines before men, but after the consummation of the world they themselves, the just, will shine like the sun in the kingdom of their Father.

The kingdom of heaven is like a treasure hidden in a field, which the man who finds it hides, and for joy over it he goes and sells all that he has and buys that field. Held back by the frequent obscurities of the parables, we go beyond the brief, verse-by-verse (commatic) interpretation, so that we seem almost to have passed from one kind of interpretation to another. This treasure, in which are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge, is either the Word of God, who is seen hidden in the flesh of Christ, or the holy Scriptures, in which is stored the knowledge of the Savior; and when anyone finds him in them, he ought to despise all the gains of this world, so that he may possess him whom he has found. As for what follows, which, when a man has found it, he hides, it is said not because he does this out of envy, but because, with the fear of one who keeps it and does not want to lose it, he hides in his heart the one he has preferred to his former possessions.

Again, the kingdom of heaven is like a merchant man seeking good pearls; and having found one most precious pearl, he went away and sold all that he had and bought it. The same thing as is said above, in other words. The good pearls that the instructor seeks are the Law and the Prophets. Hear, Marcion; hear, Manichee: the good pearls are the Law and the Prophets and the knowledge of the Old Testament, but the one most precious pearl is the knowledge of the Savior and the mystery of his passion and the secret of his resurrection. When the merchant man, like the apostle Paul, has found it, he despises all the mysteries of the Law and the Prophets and the former observances in which he had lived blamelessly, as refuse and rubbish, so that he may gain Christ. Not that the finding of a new pearl is the condemnation of the old pearls, but that in comparison with it every other gem is of less worth.

Again, the kingdom of heaven is like a net cast into the sea and gathering of every kind of fish; and when it was filled, drawing it out and sitting beside the shore, they chose the good into their vessels, but threw the bad out; so it will be at the consummation of the world, and the rest. When the prophecy of Jeremiah was fulfilled, who says, Behold, I send to you many fishers, after Peter and Andrew, James and John the sons of Zebedee had heard, Follow me, and I will make you fishers of men, they wove for themselves out of the Old and New Testament a net of Gospel doctrines and cast it into the sea of this world; and to this day it is spread out in the midst of the waves, catching from the salt and bitter whirlpools whatever falls into it, that is, both good men and bad, both the best fish and the worst. But when the consummation and end of the world has come, as he himself explains more plainly below, then the net will be drawn out to the shore, then the true judgment of the fish to be sorted will be shown, and, as in a most quiet harbor, the good will be put into the vessels of the heavenly mansions, while the flame of Gehenna will receive the bad, to be roasted and dried up.

Have you understood all these things? They say to him: Yes. The word is addressed properly to the apostles, and to them it is said, Have you understood these things, since he wishes them not merely to hear, like the people, but to understand, as future teachers.

Therefore every scribe learned in the kingdom of heaven is like a man who is a householder, who brings out of his treasure things new and old. The apostles, the scribes and notaries of the Savior, who marked down his words and precepts on the fleshly tablets of the heart, were instructed in the mysteries of the heavenly kingdoms; and they were strong in the wealth of the householder, bringing out of the treasure of their teachings things new and old, so that whatever they preached in the Gospel they might prove by the voices of the Law and the Prophets. Hence the bride too says in the Song of Songs: New things with old, my kinsman, I have kept for you.

And it came to pass, when Jesus had finished these parables, he passed over from there, and coming into his own land he taught them in their synagogue. After the parables which he spoke to the people, and which the apostles alone understand, he passed over into his own country, so as to teach more openly there.

Whence has this man this wisdom and these mighty works? The amazing folly of the Nazarenes! They wonder whence Wisdom has wisdom and Power mighty works; but their error lies plain to see, for they supposed him to be the son of a carpenter.

Is not this the carpenter's son? Is not his mother called Mary, and his brothers James and Joseph, Simon and Judas, and his sisters, all of them with us? The error of the Jews is our salvation and the condemnation of the heretics. For they so far saw Jesus Christ as a man that they thought him the son of a carpenter: Is not this the carpenter's son? Do you wonder that they err about the brothers when they err about the father? This passage is set out more fully in the little book Against Helvidius mentioned above.

But Jesus said to them: A prophet is not without honor except in his own country and in his own house. It is almost natural for citizens always to envy their fellow citizens; for they do not consider the man's present works but remember his frail infancy, as though they themselves had not come to mature age through the same stages of life.

And therefore he did not do many mighty works there, because of their unbelief. Not that he could not have done many mighty works even among them in their unbelief, but lest, by doing many, he should condemn his unbelieving fellow citizens. It can also be understood in another way: that Jesus is despised in his house and in his own country, that is, among the people of the Jews, and therefore did few signs there, lest they be made wholly without excuse. But greater signs he does daily among the Gentiles through the apostles, not so much in the healing of bodies as in the salvation of souls.

Matthew 14

At that time Herod the tetrarch heard the report of Jesus and said to his servants: This is John the Baptist; he has risen from the dead, and therefore mighty works are at work in him. A certain one of the Church's interpreters asks the reasons why Herod suspected this, so as to think that John had risen from the dead and that therefore mighty works were at work in him; as though we had to render an account of another's error, or as though the sect of μετεμψυχώσεως (transmigration of souls) could take occasion from these words, when in any case, at the time when John was beheaded, the Lord was thirty years old, whereas μετεμψύχωσις (transmigration of souls) says that souls are introduced into different bodies after many cycles of years.

But Herod had seized John and bound him and put him in prison because of Herodias, his brother's wife. For John said to him: It is not lawful for you to have her. An old history relates that Philip, the son of Herod the elder, under whom the Lord fled into Egypt, and the brother of this Herod, under whom Christ suffered, married Herodias, the daughter of King Phetras; but that afterwards her father, his father-in-law, when certain quarrels had arisen against his son-in-law, took back his daughter and, to the grief of her former husband, joined her in marriage to Herod, his enemy. Who this Philip is, the evangelist Luke teaches more fully: In the fifteenth year of the reign of Tiberius Caesar, when Pontius Pilate was governing Judea, and Herod was tetrarch of Galilee, and Philip his brother was tetrarch of Iturea and the region of Trachonitis. So John the Baptist, who had come in the spirit and power of Elijah, with the same authority with which Elijah had rebuked Ahab and Jezebel, reproves Herod and Herodias because they had made an unlawful marriage, and because it is not lawful, while one's own brother is living, to take his wife; for he preferred to be in danger with the king rather than to be forgetful of the commandments of God for the sake of flattery.

And wishing to kill him, he feared the people, because they held him as a prophet. He dreaded indeed an uprising of the people on account of John, by whom he knew that very great crowds had been baptized in the Jordan; but he was overcome by love of his wife, out of burning desire for whom he had neglected even the commandments of God.

But on Herod's birthday the daughter of Herodias danced in the middle of the dining hall and pleased Herod. We find no one else who kept his birthday except Herod and Pharaoh, so that those whose impiety was equal might have a single festival.

With an oath he promised to give her whatever she should ask of him. I do not excuse Herod on the ground that he committed murder against his will and unwillingly because of the oath; he perhaps swore for this very purpose, to prepare his engines for an occasion to come. Otherwise, if he says that he did it because of the oath, had she asked for the death of his father, or of his mother, would he have done it or not? What, therefore, he would have refused in his own case, he ought to have disregarded in the case of the prophet as well.

Give me, she says, here on a dish the head of John the Baptist. Herodias, fearing that Herod might at some time come to his senses, or become a friend to his brother Philip, and that the unlawful marriage might be dissolved by divorce, advises her daughter to ask at once, at the very banquet, for the head of John: a fitting reward of blood for a fitting work of dancing.

And the king was saddened. It is the custom of the Scriptures that the historian narrates the opinion of many in the way it was believed by all at that time. Just as Joseph is called the father of Jesus even by Mary herself, so now too Herod is said to have been saddened, because those reclining at table thought so. For this dissembler of his own mind and skilled murderer displayed sadness on his face while he had joy in his mind.

But because of the oath and of those who were reclining with him, he ordered it to be given. He excuses the crime by the oath, so that under the pretext of piety he might become impious. And as for what he added, and because of those who were reclining with him, he wants all to be partners in his crime, so that bloody dishes might be served at a luxurious and impure banquet.

And his head was brought on a dish and given to the girl, and she brought it to her mother. We read in Roman history that Flaminius, a Roman general, was expelled from the senate by the censors because, when a little harlot reclining beside him said that she had never seen a man beheaded, he had agreed that a certain man guilty of a capital crime should be beheaded at the banquet: expelled because he had mixed feasting with blood and had offered up the death of a man, guilty though he was, for the delight of another, so that lust and murder were mingled together. How much more wicked are Herod and Herodias, and the girl who danced and asks for the head of the prophet as the price of blood, so as to have in her power the tongue that rebuked the unlawful marriage. Let this have been done according to the letter. But we see to this day, in the head of John the prophet, that the Jews have lost Christ, who is the head of the prophets.

And his disciples came up and took his body and buried it, and they came and told Jesus. Josephus reports that John was beheaded in a certain town of Arabia. And what follows, His disciples came up and took the body, we can understand of the disciples both of John himself and of the Savior. And they came and told Jesus.

When Jesus had heard this, he withdrew from there in a boat to a desert place apart. They announce the Baptist's death to the Savior; having heard of it, he withdraws to a desert place; not, as some think, out of fear of death, but sparing his enemies, lest they add murder to murder; or else putting off his own death to the day of the Passover, on which, because of the mystery, the lamb is to be sacrificed and the doorposts of the believers are to be sprinkled with blood. Or else he withdrew for this reason, to give us an example of avoiding the rashness of those who hand themselves over of their own accord, because not all persevere in torments with the same constancy with which they offer themselves to be tortured. For this reason he also commands in another place: When they persecute you in this city, flee into another. Elegantly too the evangelist does not say, he fled to a desert place, but he withdrew, so that he avoided his persecutors rather than feared them. Alternatively. After the head of the prophet had been cut off by the Jews and by the king of the Jews, and prophecy had lost its tongue and voice among them, Jesus passed over to the desert place of the Church, which before had not had a husband.

And when the crowds had heard, they followed him on foot from the cities. He may also, for another reason, having heard of John's death, have withdrawn into a desert place: to test the faith of the believers. In short, the crowds followed him on foot, not on beasts of burden, not in various vehicles, but by the labor of their own feet, to show the ardor of their minds. If we wish to lay open the reasons of every single word, we exceed the brevity of the work we have undertaken. Yet it must be said in passing that after the Lord had come into the desert, very great crowds followed him; for before he came into the solitudes of the Gentiles, he was worshiped by one people only.

And going out he saw a great crowd. In the words of the Gospel the spirit is always joined to the letter, and whatever seems cold at first sight is warm if you touch it. The Lord was in a desert place; the crowds followed him, leaving their cities, that is, their former ways of life and the variety of doctrines. But Jesus' coming out signifies that the crowds had indeed the will to go, but did not have the strength to arrive; therefore the Savior comes out from his place and goes to meet them, as also in another parable the penitent son; and seeing the crowd he has pity and heals their sicknesses, so that full faith may at once obtain its reward.

But when evening had come, his disciples came to him, saying, and the rest. All things are full of mysteries. He withdraws from Judea, he comes into a desert place, the crowds follow him, having left their cities, Jesus comes out to them, he has pity on the crowd, he heals their sick; and he does this not in the morning, not as the day grows, not at midday, but in the evening, when the sun of justice had set.

But Jesus said to them: They have no need to go; you give them to eat. They have no need to seek various foods and to buy themselves unknown loaves, when they have the heavenly bread with them. You give them to eat. He challenges the apostles to the breaking of bread, so that, when they testify that they have none, the greatness of the sign may become better known.

They answered him: We have nothing here but five loaves and two fishes. In another evangelist we read: There is a certain boy here who has five loaves, and he seems to me to signify Moses; the two fishes we understand either as both Testaments, or because an even number refers to the law. So the apostles, before the passion of the Savior and the flashing forth of the lightning-bright Gospel, had nothing but five loaves and two little fishes, which moved about in salt waters and in bitter waves.

He said to them: Bring them here to me. Hear, Marcion; hear, Manichee: Jesus orders the five loaves and the two little fishes to be brought to him, so that he may sanctify and multiply them.

And when he had ordered the crowd to recline on the grass. According to the letter the sense is plain; let us lay open the mysteries of the spiritual interpretation. They are ordered to recline on the grass, and according to another evangelist on the ground, by fifties or by hundreds, so that, after they have trodden down their flesh and have put under themselves all its flowers and the pleasures of the world like withered grass, they may then, through the repentance of the number fifty, ascend to the perfect summit of the number one hundred.

Taking the five loaves and the two fishes, looking up to heaven, he blessed and broke and gave the loaves to the disciples. He looks up to heaven to teach that our eyes must be directed there. He took the five loaves and the two little fishes into his hands, and broke them, and handed them to the disciples. As the Lord breaks them, a seedbed of food comes into being. For if they had stayed whole, and had not been torn into pieces nor divided into a manifold harvest, they could not have fed the crowds, and the children, and the women, and so great a multitude. So the law together with the prophets is broken and torn into pieces, and its mysteries are brought out into the open, so that what did not nourish while it was whole and remained in its former state might, divided into parts, nourish the multitude of the Gentiles.

And he gave the loaves to the disciples, and the disciples to the crowds. The crowds receive nourishment from the Lord through the apostles.

And they took up of the remnants twelve baskets full of fragments. Each of the apostles fills his basket from the Savior's remnants, either so that he may have from it something with which afterwards to offer food to the Gentiles, or so that he may teach from the remnants that those were real loaves which were afterwards multiplied. And at the same time ask how, in the desert and in so vast a solitude, no loaves are found but five only, and two little fishes, and yet twelve baskets are found so easily.

Now the number of those who ate was five thousand men, besides women and little children. In keeping with the number of the five loaves, the multitude of the men who ate is also five thousand. For it had not yet come, as in the narrative of another passage, to the number seven, of which those who eat are four thousand, a number close to that of the Gospels. Now five thousand men eat, men who had grown to the perfect man and were following him of whom Zechariah says: Behold a man, the Rising is his name. But women and little children, the frail sex and the lesser age, are unworthy of being numbered. Hence too in the book of Numbers, whenever the priests and the Levites and the army or the bands of fighting men are registered, slaves and women and little children and the undistinguished common people are passed over without number.

And he compelled his disciples to get into the boat and to go ahead of him across the water, until he dismissed the crowds. He commanded the disciples to cross the water, and compels them to get into the boat. By this word it is shown that they withdrew from the Lord unwillingly, since out of love for their teacher they do not want to be separated from him even for a moment of time.

And having dismissed the crowd, he went up the mountain alone to pray. If the disciples Peter and James and John, who had seen the glory of him transfigured, had been with him, perhaps they would have gone up the mountain with him; but the crowd cannot follow to the heights unless he has taught it by the sea on the shore and fed it in the desert. But that he went up alone to pray, do not refer to him who with five loaves satisfied five thousand men, besides little children and women, but to him who, having heard of John's death, withdrew into solitude; not that we separate the person of the Lord, but that his works are divided between God and man.

But the boat in the middle of the sea was tossed by the waves. Rightly had the apostles withdrawn from the Lord as though unwilling and reluctant, lest in his absence they should suffer shipwreck. In short, while the Lord was lingering on the summit of the mountain, at once a contrary wind arises and troubles the sea, and the apostles are in danger, and the impending shipwreck lasts until Jesus comes.

But in the fourth watch of the night he came to them walking upon the sea. Military guard-posts and watches are divided into spaces of three hours each. So when he says that the Lord came to them in the fourth watch of the night, he shows that they were in danger the whole night, and that at the very end of the night and at the consummation of the world he will bring them help.

And seeing him walking upon the sea, they were troubled, saying: It is a phantom. If, according to Marcion and Mani, our Lord was not born of a virgin but was seen as a phantom, how is it that the apostles now fear that they are seeing a phantom?

And for fear they cried out. A confused cry and an uncertain voice are the mark of great fear.

And at once Jesus spoke to them, saying: Have confidence, it is I. What was at issue first, that he heals first, and to those who are afraid he gives a command, saying: Have confidence, do not be afraid. And as to what follows, it is I, he does not add who he is: either they could recognize him from a voice known to them, as he spoke through the dark shadows of the night, or they recalled that it was he himself whom they knew to have spoken to Moses: This is what you say to the sons of Israel: He who is has sent me to you.

But Peter answered and said: Lord, if it is you, bid me come to you over the waters. In every place Peter is found to be of the most ardent faith. When the disciples are asked whom men said Jesus was, he confesses him the Son of God; when Jesus wishes to go to his passion, though he errs in the matter, yet he does not err in his affection, not wishing him to die whom he had shortly before confessed to be the Son of God; on the mountain he goes up with the Savior, first among the first; and in the passion he alone follows; the sin of denial, which had come upon him from sudden fear, he at once washes away with bitter tears; after the passion, when they were on the lake of Gennesaret and were fishing, and the Lord was standing on the shore, while the others were sailing in little by little, he does not endure delay, but, girding himself with his outer garment, at once throws himself headlong into the waves. So with the same ardor of faith as always, now too, while the others are silent, he believes that he can do by the will of the master what the master could do by nature. Bid me come to you over the waters. You command, and at once the waves will become solid, and the body that is heavy of itself will become light.

And Peter, getting down from the boat, walked upon the water. Let those who think that the Lord's body was not real because, soft and airy, it walked over the soft waters, answer how Peter walked, whom they will certainly not deny to be a real man.

But seeing the strong wind he was afraid, and when he began to sink he cried out, saying: Lord, save me. The faith of his spirit was burning, but human frailty was dragging him into the deep. So he is left to temptation for a little, so that his faith may be increased and he may understand that he was preserved not by the readiness of his request but by the power of the Lord.

And at once Jesus, stretching out his hand, took hold of him and said to him: You of little faith, why did you doubt? If to the apostle Peter, of whose faith and ardor of mind we spoke above, who had confidently asked the Savior, saying: Lord, if it is you, bid me come to you over the waters, it is said, because he was a little afraid: You of little faith, why did you doubt, what must be said to us, who do not have even the smallest little portion of this little faith?

Those who were in the boat came and worshiped him, saying: Truly you are the Son of God. At a single sign, when calm was restored to the sea, which after excessive storms sometimes also happens by chance, the sailors and passengers confess him truly the Son of God; and Arius preaches in the Church that he is a creature.

And when they had crossed the water, they came into the land of Gennesaret. If we knew what Gennesaret sounds like in our language, we would understand how Jesus, through the type of the apostles and of the ship, brings the Church, delivered from the shipwreck of persecution, across to the shore, and makes it rest in a most tranquil harbor.

And when the men of that place had recognized him, they sent into all that region. They recognized him by report, not by face; or at any rate, because of the greatness of the signs that he was performing among the peoples, he was also known by face to very many. And see how great is the faith of the men of the land of Gennesaret, that they are not content with the health of those present only, but send to other cities round about, so that all may run to the physician.

And they brought to him all who were ill, and they begged him that they might touch even the fringe of his garment; and whoever touched it were made whole. Let those who are ill touch, not the body of Jesus, nor his whole garment, but the outermost fringe; and whoever touch it will be healed. Understand the fringe of his garment as the least commandment, for whoever transgresses it shall be called least in the kingdom of heaven; or as the assumption of the body, through which we come to the Word of God and afterwards enjoy his majesty.

Matthew 15

Why do your disciples transgress the tradition of the elders? Astonishing is the folly of the Pharisees and scribes. They accuse the Son of God of not keeping the traditions and precepts of men.

For they do not wash their hands when they eat bread. The hands, that is, the works, not of the body but of the soul, are what must be washed, so that the word of God may come to be in them.

But he answered and said to them: Why do you also transgress the commandment of God for the sake of your tradition? He refutes a false charge with a true answer. Since, he says, you neglect the precepts of the Lord for the sake of the tradition of men, why do you think my disciples are to be reproved for making light of the commands of the elders in order to keep the decrees of God?

For God said: Honor father and mother, and whoever curses father or mother, let him die the death. But you say: Whoever says to father or mother: Whatever gift is from me will profit you; and he has not honored his father or his mother. Honor in the Scriptures is understood not so much in greetings and the paying of courtesies as in alms and the offering of gifts. Honor, says the Apostle, widows who are truly widows, and here honor is understood as a gift; and in another place: Presbyters are to be honored with double honor, especially those who labor in the word and teaching of God. And by this commandment we are bidden not to close the mouth of the ox that treads out the grain, and that the laborer be worthy of his wages. The Lord, considering either the infirmities or the ages or the poverty of parents, had commanded that sons should honor their parents also by supplying them with the necessities of life. Wishing to overturn this most provident law of God, so as to bring in impiety under the name of piety, the scribes and Pharisees taught the worst of sons that if anyone wished to vow to God, who is the true Father, the things that were to be offered to his parents, the offering to the Lord should take precedence over gifts to parents. Or else the parents themselves, shunning what they saw consecrated to God so as not to incur the charge of sacrilege, were worn down by want; and so it came about that the offering of the children, under pretext of the temple and of God, went to the profit of the priests. This most wicked tradition of the Pharisees came from another occasion. Many who held men bound to them by debt, men unwilling to repay what had been lent them, assigned these debts to the priests, so that the money, once exacted, might serve the ministries of the temple and the priests' own uses. But it can also, briefly, have this sense: The gift that is from me will profit you. You compel sons, he says, to say to their parents: Whatever gift I was going to offer to God I spend on your food, and it profits you, O father and mother; so that they, afraid to accept what they see made over to God, would rather lead a life of want than eat of consecrated things.

Not what enters the mouth makes a man common, but what comes out of the mouth, this makes a man common. The word makes common is proper to the Scriptures and is not worn in public speech. The Jewish people, boasting that they are God's portion, call common the foods that all men use, for example pork, oysters, hares and animals of that kind that do not divide the hoof nor chew the cud, nor, among fish, have scales. Hence it is also written in the Acts of the Apostles: What God has sanctified, do not call common. Common, then, that which lies open to other men and is, as it were, not of God's portion, is used for unclean. Not what enters the mouth defiles a man, but what comes out of the mouth, this defiles a man. The prudent reader may object and say: If what enters the mouth does not defile a man, why do we not eat things sacrificed to idols? And the Apostle writes: You cannot drink the cup of the Lord and the cup of demons. It must be understood, therefore, that the foods themselves, and every creature of God, are clean in themselves, but the invocation of idols and demons makes them unclean.

Then his disciples came and said to him: Do you know that the Pharisees were scandalized when they heard this word? By a single saying the whole superstition of the Jewish observances had been struck down, for they think their religion lies in the eating and abhorring of foods. And since the word scandalum is often used in the writings of the Church, we must say briefly what it signifies. Scolon and scandalum we can call an obstacle, or a fall and a striking of the foot. So when we read: Whoever causes any one of these least ones to stumble, we understand this: he who by word or deed has given anyone an occasion of falling.

But he answered and said: Every planting that my heavenly Father has not planted will be uprooted. Even the things that seem plain in the Scriptures are full of questions. Every planting, he says, that my heavenly Father has not planted will be uprooted. Then that planting too will be uprooted of which the Apostle says: I planted, Apollos watered. But the question is solved by what follows: But God gave the increase. He himself also says: You are God's tillage, God's building; and in another place: We are God's fellow workers. But if fellow workers, then, when Paul plants and Apollos waters, God plants and waters together with his workers. Those who bring in different natures misuse this passage, saying: If the planting that the Father has not planted will be uprooted, then the one he has planted cannot be uprooted. But let them hear that word of Jeremiah: I planted you a true vine; how you have turned into the bitterness of a strange vine. God did indeed plant, and no one can uproot his planting. But since this planting rests in the will of its own free choice, no one else will be able to uproot it unless it gives its own assent.

Let them alone; they are blind, leaders of the blind. This is what the Apostle had commanded: Avoid a heretical man after a first and a second correction, knowing that such a one is perverted and condemned by himself. In this sense the Savior too commands that the worst teachers be left to their own choice, knowing that they can hardly be drawn to the truth, that they are blind and drag a blind people into error.

And Peter answered and said to him: Explain this parable to us. And he said: Are you also still without understanding? What had been said openly and lay plain to the hearing, the apostle Peter thinks was spoken in a parable, and in a plain matter he seeks a mystical understanding; and he is rebuked by the Lord for thinking that something was said in parable which he had spoken plainly. From this we observe that a hearer is at fault who wishes to understand either obscure things plainly or things plainly said obscurely.

Do you not understand that everything that enters the mouth goes into the belly and is cast out into the privy? All the passages of the Gospels are full of stumbling blocks for heretics and the perverse. And from this little saying some slander the Lord as ignorant of natural science, as though he thought that all foods go into the belly and are passed into the privy, when in fact food, as soon as it is taken in, is diffused through the limbs and veins and marrow and sinews; whence too, they say, many who through a disorder of the stomach suffer constant vomiting throw up what they have taken in immediately after dinners and luncheons, and yet are fleshy, because at the first touch the more liquid food and drink are diffused through the members. But men of this kind, while they wish to censure another's ignorance, display their own. For however thin the moisture and however liquid the food, once it has been concocted and digested in the veins and limbs, it slips down through the hidden passages of the body, which the Greeks call πόρους, to the lower parts, and goes into the privy.

For out of the heart come evil thoughts, murders, adulteries, fornications, and the rest. Out of the heart, he says, come evil thoughts. Therefore the ruling part of the soul is not, as Plato holds, in the brain, but, as Christ teaches, in the heart; and this saying refutes those who think that thoughts are sent in by the devil and do not arise from one's own will. The devil can be a helper and an inciter of evil thoughts; their author he cannot be; but if, ever lying in ambush, he has fanned the slight spark of our thoughts into flame with his kindling. We ought not to suppose that he also searches out the hidden things of the heart, but rather that he judges from the bearing and gestures of the body what we are turning over within: for example, if he has seen us often looking back at a beautiful woman, he understands that the heart has been wounded by the dart of love.

And Jesus went out from there and withdrew into the parts of Tyre and Sidon. Leaving behind the scribes and Pharisees, his slanderers, he passes over into the parts of Tyre and Sidon in order to heal the Tyrians and Sidonians. And the Canaanite woman goes out from her former territory in order to obtain, by crying out, healing for her daughter. Note that it is in the fifteenth place that the Canaanite daughter is healed.

Have mercy on me, Lord, son of David; my daughter is badly vexed by a demon. She knew to call him son of David because she had already gone out from her own territory and, by a change of place and of faith, had left behind the error of the Tyrians and Sidonians. My daughter is badly vexed by a demon. I think that the daughter of the Church is the souls of believers, which were badly vexed by a demon, not knowing the Creator and worshiping stone.

Who did not answer her a word. Not from Pharisaic pride nor from the haughtiness of the scribes, but so that he might not seem to contradict his own pronouncement, in which he had commanded: Do not go into the way of the Gentiles, and do not enter the cities of the Samaritans. For he did not wish to give an occasion to the slanderers, and he was reserving the full salvation of the Gentiles for the time of his passion and resurrection.

And his disciples came and asked him, saying: Send her away, for she is crying out after us. The disciples, not yet knowing at that time the mysteries of the Lord, were asking on behalf of the Canaanite woman (whom the other evangelist calls a Syrophoenician), either moved by mercy or wishing to be rid of her importunity, because she kept crying out to him, not as to a merciful physician but as to a harsh one.

I was not sent except to the lost sheep of the house of Israel. Not that he was not sent to the Gentiles too, but that he was sent first to Israel, so that, when they did not receive the Gospel, the passing over to the Gentiles might be just. And he said pointedly: to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, so that from this place we may also understand the one straying sheep of the other parable.

But she came and worshiped him, saying. Wonderful, under the person of the Canaanite woman, are the faith, the patience and the humility of the Church: faith, by which she believed that her daughter could be healed; patience, by which, though so often scorned, she perseveres in her prayers; humility, by which she compares herself not to dogs but to whelps. Now the heathen are called dogs because of idolatry, for, given over to the eating of blood and to the corpses of the dead, they are carried away into madness. Note that this Canaanite woman, persevering, calls him first son of David, then Lord, and at the last worships him as God.

But she said: Yes, Lord, for even the little dogs eat of the crumbs that fall from their masters' table. I know, she says, that I do not deserve the children's bread, and that I cannot take whole food or sit at table with the father; but I am content with the whelps' leavings, so that through the humility of crumbs I may come to the greatness of the whole loaf. O wonderful reversal of things! Israel once a son, we dogs. As faith differs, the order of the names is changed. Of them it is later said: Many dogs have surrounded me; and: Look at the dogs, look at the evil workers, look at the concision. We hear, with the Syrophoenician woman and with the woman who had suffered a flow of blood: Great is your faith, let it be done according to your faith; and: Daughter, your faith has made you whole.

And when Jesus had passed on from there, he came beside the sea of Galilee, and going up on the mountain he sat there. And great crowds came to him, having with them the mute, the lame, the blind, the maimed and many others, and they cast them down at his feet. In the place where the Latin translator rendered maimed the Greek has κυλλούς, which is the name not of disability in general but of one particular infirmity: just as he is called lame who limps on one foot, so he is called κυλλός who has one disabled hand. We have no word with the exact meaning of this one. Hence too, in what follows, the evangelist set out the healings of the other disabled, but was silent about these. For what follows?

And he healed them, so that the crowds marveled, seeing the mute speaking, the lame walking, the blind seeing; and they magnified the God of Israel. About the κυλλούς he was silent, because he had no word to say for their opposite. So much for one word. But let us observe that, once the daughter of the Canaanite woman has been healed, he returns to Judea and to the sea of Galilee, and goes up on the mountain, and, like a bird calling its tender young to fly, sits there; and the crowds run together to him, leading or carrying with them those weighed down by various infirmities; and after he had healed them he gave them food, and when this work was completed he went up into the boat and came into the territory of Magadan. And going up on the mountain he sat there, and crowds came to him. Note that many lame and blind are led to the mountain so as to be healed there by the Lord.

But Jesus called his disciples together and said: I have pity on the crowd, because they have now been staying with me three days and have nothing to eat; and I am unwilling to send them away fasting, lest they faint on the way. He wishes to feed those whom he has healed: first he takes away their disabilities, then he offers food to the healthy. He also calls his disciples together and tells them what he is about to do, either to give them a master's example that counsels are to be shared with the lesser and with disciples, or so that from the conversation they might understand the greatness of the sign, when they answer that they have no loaves in the desert. I have pity, he says, on the crowd, because they have now been staying with me three days. He has pity on the crowd because, in the number of three days, they believed in the Father, the Son and the Holy Spirit. And they have nothing to eat. The crowd is always hungry and in need of food unless it is filled by the Lord. And I am unwilling to send them away fasting, lest they faint on the way. They were hungry after their great disabilities, and in patience they were awaiting the food to come. Jesus does not wish to send them away fasting, lest they faint on the way. He is in peril, then, who hastens to reach the longed-for dwelling without the heavenly bread. Hence too the angel says to Elijah: Arise and eat, for you are to walk a great way.

And the disciples say to him: Where then are we to get so many loaves in the desert as to fill so great a crowd? And Jesus said to them: How many loaves have you? And they said: Seven, and a few little fishes, and the rest. Of this sign we have already spoken above, and to repeat the same things is the mark of an idle man; let us dwell only on the points in which they differ. Above we read: When evening had come, the disciples came to him, saying: The place is a desert, and the rest. Here, having called the disciples together, the Lord himself speaks: I have pity on the crowd, because they have now been staying with me three days. There, there were five loaves and two fishes; here, seven loaves and a few little fishes. There they recline upon the grass, here upon the ground. There those who eat are five thousand, according to the number of the loaves they eat; here, four thousand. There twelve baskets are filled with what is left of the fragments; here, seven hampers. In the former sign, then, because they were near and neighbors to the five senses, it is not the Lord himself who is mindful of them but the disciples, and they are mindful of them at evening, with night near and the sun already sinking. Here, however, the Lord himself is mindful of them, and says that he has pity, and sets out the causes of his pity: Because they have now been staying with me three days, and he does not wish to send them away fasting lest they faint on the way. These who are fed from seven loaves, that is, by a sacred and perfect number, are not five thousand but four thousand, a number that is always set down in praise; and a four-cornered stone does not totter, is not unstable; and for this reason the Gospels too are consecrated in that number.

Matthew 16:1-12

But he answered and said to them: When evening has come you say: It will be fair, for the sky is red; and in the morning: Today there will be a storm, for the sky is red and lowering. You know, then, how to judge the face of the sky, but the signs of the times you cannot. This is not found in most manuscripts, and the sense is plain: from the order and constancy of the elements days both of fair weather and of rain can be known beforehand; but the scribes and Pharisees, who seemed to be teachers of the law, could not understand from the prophecy of the prophets the coming of the Savior.

And leaving them he went away; and when his disciples had come across the water, they had forgotten to take bread. Leaving the scribes and Pharisees, to whom he had said: An evil and adulterous generation seeks a sign, and no sign will be given it except the sign of Jonah, he rightly went away across the water, and the people of the Gentiles followed him. What the sign of Jonah means has already been said above.

Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees. He who bewares of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees does not keep the precepts of the law and of the letter; he neglects the traditions of men in order to do the commandment of God.

Why are you reasoning among yourselves, you of little faith, that you have no bread? Do you not yet understand, nor remember the five loaves of the five thousand men, and how many baskets you took up, and the rest. On the occasion of the precept that the Savior had given, saying: Beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees, he teaches them what the five loaves and the seven mean, and the five thousand men and the four thousand who were fed in the desert: that, although the greatness of the signs is evident, yet something else as well is shown by spiritual understanding. For if the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees signifies not bodily bread but perverse traditions and heretical doctrines, why should not the foods too with which the people of God was nourished signify true and whole doctrine? Someone may ask and say: How did they have no bread, who, right after filling seven hampers, went up into the boat and came into the territory of Magadan, and there, while sailing, hear that they must beware of the leaven of the Pharisees and Sadducees? But Scripture testifies that they had forgotten to take them along. This is the leaven of which the Apostle too speaks: A little leaven corrupts the whole lump. Leaven of this kind, which must be shunned by every means, Marcion had, and Valentinus, and all the heretics. Leaven has this power: if it is mixed with flour, what seemed small grows into something greater and draws the whole mass of dough to its own taste. So too heretical doctrine: if it has cast even a tiny spark into your breast, in a short time it grows up into a huge flame and draws the whole of a man's possession to itself. Finally there follows: Then they understood that he had not said they should beware of the leaven of bread, but of the teaching of the Pharisees and Sadducees.

Book Three

Matthew 16:13-28

But Jesus came into the parts of Caesarea Philippi. This Philip is the brother of Herod of whom we spoke above, the tetrarch of the regions of Iturea and Trachonitis, who in honor of Tiberius Caesar named Caesarea Philippi (which is now called Paneas), and it is in the province of Phoenicia; he imitated his father Herod, who in honor of Augustus Caesar named Caesarea, which was formerly called Strato's Tower, and who, from the name of his daughter, built Libias beyond the Jordan. This place is Caesarea Philippi, where the Jordan rises at the roots of Lebanon; and it has two springs, one named Ior and the other Dan, which, mingled together, make the name Jordan.

And he asked his disciples, saying: Whom do men say that the Son of Man is? He did not say: Whom do men say that I am, but the Son of Man, lest he seem to inquire boastfully about himself. And note that wherever son of man is written in the Old Testament, in the Hebrew son of Adam is set down; and that which we read in the psalm: Sons of men, how long will you be heavy of heart? in the Hebrew is said sons of Adam. Beautifully, moreover, he asks: Whom do men say that the Son of Man is, because those who speak of the Son of Man are men; but those who understand his divinity are called not men but gods.

And they said: Some John the Baptist, others Elijah, but others Jeremiah or one of the prophets. I wonder at certain interpreters who inquire into the causes of each one's errors and weave a very long discussion of why some thought our Lord Jesus Christ to be John, others Elijah, but others Jeremiah or one of the prophets, when they could have erred thus about Elijah and Jeremiah in the same way that Herod erred about John, saying: John, whom I beheaded, has himself risen from the dead, and mighty works are at work in him.

But whom do you say that I am? Simon Peter answered: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. Prudent reader, observe that from what follows and from the texture of the discourse the apostles are by no means called men but gods. For when he had said: Whom do men say that the Son of Man is, he added: But whom do you say that I am? Since they, being men, think human things, you who are gods, whom do you consider me to be? Peter, in the person of all the apostles, professes: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. He calls him the living God by comparison with those gods who are thought to be gods but are dead, meaning Saturn, Jupiter, Ceres, Liber, Hercules and the other monstrosities of idols.

Jesus answered and said to him: Blessed are you, Simon Bar Iona, because flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, but my Father who is in heaven. He repays in kind the apostle's testimony about him. Peter had said: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God; the true confession received its reward: Blessed are you, Simon Bar Iona. Why? Because flesh and blood did not reveal it to you, but the Father revealed it. What flesh and blood could not reveal was revealed by the grace of the Holy Spirit.
