Therefore from his confession he obtains a name signifying that he has his revelation from the Holy Spirit, whose son he is also to be called. For Bar Iona in our language means son of a dove. Others take it simply, that Simon, that is, Peter, is the son of John, according to the question in another place: Simon of John, do you love me? and he answered: Lord, you know, and they hold that it was corrupted by the fault of the copyists, so that Bar Iona was written in place of Bar Iohanna, that is, son of John, one syllable being taken away. And Iohanna is interpreted the grace of the Lord. But both names can be understood mystically, in that the dove signifies the Holy Spirit, and the grace of God the spiritual gift. That also which he says: Because flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, compare with the Apostle's narrative, in which he says: Immediately I did not acquiesce to flesh and blood, there signifying the Jews by flesh and blood; so that here too, under another sense, it is shown that Christ the Son of God was revealed to him not through the teaching of the Pharisees but through the grace of God.

And I say to you. What is it that he says: And I say to you? Because you said to me: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God, and I say to you, not with an empty word that has no effect, but I say to you because for me to have said is to have done.

That you are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church. Just as he himself gave light to the apostles, so that they were called the light of the world, and the other names which they obtained from the Lord; so too on Simon, who believed in the rock Christ, he bestowed the name of Peter, and according to the metaphor of the rock it is rightly said to him: I will build my Church upon you.

And the gates of hell shall not prevail against it. I think the gates of hell are vices and sins, or at any rate the doctrines of heretics, by which men are enticed and led to Tartarus. So let no one think it is said of death, as if the apostles were not subject to the condition of death, when he sees their martyrdoms shining.

And to you I will give the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind upon earth shall be bound also in heaven, and whatever you loose upon earth shall be loosed also in heaven. Not understanding this place, bishops and presbyters assume to themselves something of the haughtiness of the Pharisees, so as either to condemn the innocent or to think that they loose the guilty, when with God it is not the sentence of the priests but the life of the accused that is examined. We read in Leviticus about lepers, where they are commanded to show themselves to the priests, and, if they have leprosy, then they are made unclean by the priest; not that the priests make them lepers and unclean, but that they have knowledge of the leper and the non-leper and can discern who is clean or who unclean. Just as, then, there the priest makes the leper unclean, so here too the bishop and presbyter binds or looses, not those who are innocent or guilty, but, according to his office, when he has heard the varieties of sins, he knows who is to be bound, who loosed.

Then he commanded his disciples that they should tell no one that he himself was Jesus Christ. Above, when sending the disciples to preach, he had ordered them to announce his coming; here he commands them not to say that he is Jesus Christ. It seems to me that it is one thing to preach Christ, another to preach Jesus Christ. Christ is a common name of dignity; Jesus is the proper name of the Savior. But it may be that he did not wish to be preached before the passion and resurrection for this reason, that, when the mystery of the blood had afterward been completed, he might more fittingly say to the apostles: Go, teach all nations, and the rest. And lest anyone think that this is only our own understanding and not the sense of the Gospel, what follows sets out the causes of the preaching forbidden at that time.

From then on Jesus began to show his disciples that he must go to Jerusalem and suffer many things from the elders and scribes and chief priests, and be killed, and on the third day rise again. Now the sense is: Preach me then, when I have suffered these things, because it does no good to preach Christ publicly and to spread abroad among the peoples the majesty of him whom a little later they are to see scourged and crucified, suffering many things from the elders and scribes and chief priests. And now Jesus suffers many things from those who crucify the Son of God for themselves again, and who, though they are regarded in the Church as elders and chief priests, following the simple letter, kill the Son of God, who is perceived wholly in the spirit.

And Peter, taking him aside, began to rebuke him, saying: Far be it from you, Lord; this shall not happen to you. He turned and said to Peter: Go behind me, Satan; you are a stumbling block to me, because you do not savor the things that are of God but those that are of men. We have often said that Peter had an excessive ardor and the very greatest love toward the Lord Savior. Because, then, after his confession in which he had said: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God, and the Savior's reward which he had heard: Blessed are you, Simon Bar Iona, because flesh and blood has not revealed it to you, but my Father who is in heaven, he suddenly hears from the Lord that he must go to Jerusalem and there suffer many things from the elders and scribes and chief priests and be killed and on the third day rise again, he does not want his confession destroyed, nor does he think it can happen that the Son of God should be killed; and he takes him into his affection, or leads him apart, lest he seem to rebuke his master in the presence of the other fellow disciples, and began to rebuke him with the affection of one who loves, and to say, wishing it: Far be it from you, Lord, or, as it is better had in the Greek, Ἵλεώς σοι κύριε, that is: Be propitious, Lord; this shall not happen, it cannot be, nor do my ears accept it, that the Son of God is to be killed. To him the Lord, turning, says: Get back behind me, Satan; you are a stumbling block to me. Satan is interpreted adversary or opponent. Because, he says, you speak things contrary to my will, you ought to be called an adversary. Many think that it was not Peter who was rebuked, but the adversary spirit who was suggesting to the apostle to say these things. But to me the apostle's error, coming from an affection of piety, will never seem an incitement of the devil. Go behind me, Satan, is said to the devil. Get back, Peter hears, get back behind me, that is, follow my judgment, because you do not savor the things that are of God but those of men. It is my will and the Father's, whose will I came to do, that I should die for the salvation of men; you, considering only your own will, do not want the grain of wheat to fall into the earth so that it may bear many fruits. Let the prudent reader inquire how, after such great blessedness: Blessed are you, Simon Bar Iona, and: You are Peter, and upon this rock I will build my Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it, and to you I will give the keys of the kingdom of heaven, and whatever you bind or loose upon earth shall be bound or loosed in heaven, he now hears: Get back behind me, Satan; you are a stumbling block to me, or what so sudden a turnabout this is, that after such great rewards he is called Satan. But let him who asks this consider that that blessing and blessedness and power and the building of the Church upon him were promised to Peter in the future, not given in the present. I will build, he says, my Church upon you, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it, and I will give you the keys of the kingdom of heaven. All of it is in the future; and if he had given it to him at once, the error of a wrong confession would never have found a place in him.

Then Jesus said to his disciples: If anyone wishes to come after me, let him deny himself and take up his cross and follow me, and the rest. He who puts off the old man with his deeds denies himself, saying: But I live, now not I, but Christ lives in me, and he takes up his cross and is crucified to the world. And he to whom the world is crucified follows the crucified Lord.

Or what exchange shall a man give for his soul? For Israel, Egypt and Ethiopia and Soene are given as an exchange; for the human soul the only recompense is that which the psalmist sings: What shall I render to the Lord for all that he has rendered to me? I will take the cup of salvation, and I will call upon the name of the Lord.

For the Son of Man is to come in the glory of his Father with his angels, and then he will render to each according to his work. Peter, scandalized at the announcement of the Lord's death, had been rebuked by the Lord's judgment; the disciples were challenged to deny themselves and take up their cross and follow their master in the spirit of men going to their death. Great was the terror of the hearers, and such as could, with the chief of the apostles terrified, cast fear into the others too. Therefore glad things succeed the sad, and he says: The Son of Man is to come in the glory of his Father with his angels. You fear death: hear of the glory of the one triumphing; you dread the cross: listen to the ministries of the angels. And then, he says, he will render to each according to his works. There is no distinction of Jew and heathen, of man and woman, of poor and rich, where not persons but works are considered.

Amen I say to you: There are some of those standing here who shall not taste death until they see the Son of Man coming in his kingdom. He had wished to heal the terror of the apostles with the hope of what was promised, saying: The Son of Man is to come in the glory of his Father with his angels; adding besides the authority of the judge: And he will render to each according to his works. The silent thought of the apostles could have suffered a stumbling block of this sort: slaughter and death you say are coming now; but what you promise, that you will be present in the glory of the Father with the ministries of the angels and the power of a judge, this will be for days to come and is put off to long ages. Foreseeing, then, what they could object, the present fear, the knower of hidden things compensates it with a present reward. For what does he say? There are some of those standing here who shall not taste death until they see the Son of Man coming in his kingdom, so that what he is to be like when he comes afterward may, because of your unbelief, be shown in the present time.

Matthew 17

And after six days Jesus took Peter and James and John his brother. Why Peter and James and John are separated from the rest in certain places of the Gospels, or what privilege they have beyond the other apostles, we have often said. Now it is asked how he took them after six days and led them up a high mountain apart, when Luke the evangelist puts the number eight. But the answer is easy: here the middle days are given; there the first and the last are added. For it is not said: After eight days Jesus takes Peter and James and John, but on the eighth day.

And he leads them up a high mountain apart. To lead the disciples up to the mountains is part of the kingdom. They are led apart because many are called, few chosen.

And he was transfigured before them. Such as he is to be at the time of judging, such he appeared to the apostles. But as to his saying: he was transfigured before them, let no one think that he lost his former form and face, or gave up the reality of his body and took on a body either spiritual or airy; but how he was transformed the evangelist shows, saying:

And his face shone like the sun, and his garments became white as snow. Where the splendor of the face is shown and the whiteness of the garments is described, the substance is not taken away, but the glory is changed. His face shone like the sun. Certainly the Lord was transformed into that glory with which he is to come afterward in his kingdom. The transformation added splendor; it did not take away the face. Grant that the body was spiritual: were the garments also changed, which were so white that another evangelist said: Such as no fuller on earth can make? But what a fuller on earth can make is bodily and subject to touch, not spiritual and airy, a thing that deceives the eyes and is seen only as a phantasm.

And behold, there appeared to them Moses and Elijah, talking with him. When the scribes and Pharisees were testing him and demanding signs from heaven, he would not give them, but refuted the perverse demand with a prudent answer. Here, however, to increase the faith of the apostles, he gives a sign from heaven, Elijah coming down from the place to which he had ascended, and Moses rising from the underworld; as Ahaz too is instructed through Isaiah to ask for himself a sign from on high or from hell. For as to what was said: There appeared to them Moses and Elijah, talking with him, it is also reported in another Gospel that they told him what he was to suffer at Jerusalem. The Law and the Prophets are shown, which in frequent utterances announced both the passion and the resurrection of the Lord.

But Peter answered and said to Jesus: Lord, it is good for us to be here. Because he had climbed up to the mountains, he does not want to go down to earthly things, but to remain always on the heights.

If you wish, I will make here three tabernacles, one for you, and one for Moses, and one for Elijah. You err, Peter, as another evangelist also testifies: you do not know what you are saying. Do not seek three tabernacles, when there is one tabernacle of the Gospel, in which the Law and the Prophets are to be summed up. But if you seek three tabernacles, by no means compare the servants with the Lord. Rather, make three tabernacles, or rather one, for the Father and the Son and the Holy Spirit, so that those whose divinity is one may also have one tabernacle in your breast.

While he was still speaking, behold, a bright cloud overshadowed them, and a voice from the cloud, saying: This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased; hear him. Because he had asked imprudently, he therefore does not merit the Lord's answer, but the Father answered for the Son, so that the word of the Lord might be fulfilled: I do not bear witness for myself, but the Father who sent me, he bears witness for me. And the cloud is seen to be bright and overshadows them, so that those who were seeking a fleshly tabernacle of boughs or tents might be covered by the shelter of the bright cloud. The voice too of the Father speaking from heaven is heard, to bear witness to the Son and, removing Peter's error, to teach him the truth, or rather, through Peter, the other apostles. This is, he says, my beloved Son: for him a tabernacle is to be made, him you must obey; this is my Son; they are servants; Moses and Elijah ought themselves, with you, to prepare a tabernacle for the Lord in the inner recesses of their heart.

And the disciples, hearing it, fell on their face and were exceedingly afraid. They are struck with dread for a threefold cause: either because they had recognized that they had erred, or because the bright cloud had covered them, or because they had heard the voice of God the Father speaking; human frailty does not endure to bear the sight of a greater glory, and, trembling in its whole soul and body, it falls to the earth. The more anyone seeks greater things, the more he collapses to the lower, if he is ignorant of his own measure.

And Jesus came and touched them. Because they lay there and could not rise, he himself mercifully approaches and touches them, so that by his touch he may put their fear to flight and their weakened limbs may be made firm.

And he said to them: Rise, and do not be afraid. Those whom he had healed with his hand he heals with his command. Do not be afraid. First fear is driven out, so that afterward teaching may be imparted.

And lifting up their eyes they saw no one but Jesus alone. Reasonably, after they had risen they saw no one but Jesus alone, lest, if Moses and Elijah had remained with the Lord, the Father's voice might seem uncertain as to whom above all it bore witness. They see, then, Jesus standing, the cloud taken away, and Moses and Elijah vanished; because after the shadow of the Law and the Prophets, which had covered the apostles with its veil, has departed, both are found in the Gospel.

And as they were coming down from the mountain, Jesus commanded them, saying: Tell the vision to no one until the Son of Man rises from the dead. A foretaste of the kingdom to come and the glory of the one triumphing had been shown on the mountain. He does not wish, therefore, that this be preached among the peoples, lest it both be incredible because of the greatness of the thing and, after such great glory, the cross that followed should cause a stumbling block among untaught minds.

And the disciples asked him, saying: Why then do the scribes say that Elijah must come first? Unless we know the causes why the disciples asked about the name of Elijah, their question seems foolish and out of place. For what has it to do with the things written above, to ask about the coming of Elijah? It is a tradition of the Pharisees, according to the prophet Malachi, who is the last of the twelve, that Elijah will come before the coming of the Savior and turn back the heart of the fathers to the sons and of the sons to the fathers, and restore all things to their ancient state. The disciples therefore suppose that this transformation of glory is the one they had seen on the mountain, and they say: If you have already come in glory, how is it that your forerunner does not appear? especially since they had seen Elijah withdraw. But when they add: The scribes say that Elijah must come first, by saying first they show that, unless Elijah has come, it is not the coming of the Savior according to the Scriptures.

But he answered and said to them: Elijah indeed is to come and will restore all things; but I say to you that Elijah has already come, and the rest. He who is to come at the second coming of the Savior in the truth of the body now comes through John in power and spirit; but they did not know him and did to him whatever they wished, that is, they despised and beheaded him.

So also the Son of Man is to suffer by them. Now it is asked, since Herod and Herodias killed John, how they themselves are said to have crucified Jesus as well, when we read that he was killed by the scribes and Pharisees; and the short answer is that both in John's murder the faction of the Pharisees consented, and in the slaying of the Lord Herod joined his will, he who, after mocking and despising him, sent him back to Pilate to crucify him.

Lord, have mercy on my son, for he is a lunatic and suffers badly; for he often falls into the fire, and frequently into the water; and I brought him to your disciples, and they could not cure him. For what reason a demon, watching the course of the moon, seizes men and strives through the creatures to defame the Creator, we have said above. But it seems to me that according to tropology the lunatic is he who is changed toward vices from moment to moment of the hours and does not persist in what he has begun, but waxes and wanes, and is now carried into the fire with which the hearts of adulterers are kindled, now into the waters, which cannot extinguish charity. But as to his saying: I brought him to your disciples, and they could not cure him, he covertly accuses the apostles, although the impossibility of curing is sometimes to be referred not to the weakness of those who cure but to the faith of those who are to be cured, as the Lord says: Be it done to you according to your faith.

Jesus answered and said: O unbelieving and perverse generation, how long shall I be with you? How long shall I bear with you? Not that he was overcome by weariness, and that he who is meek and gentle, who like a lamb before its shearer did not open his mouth, broke out into words of fury; but that, in the likeness of a physician who sees a sick man conducting himself against his orders, he might say: How long shall I come to your house, how long shall I waste the diligence of my art, when I order one thing and you do another? So little, moreover, is he angry with the man rather than with the vice, and through one man he convicts the Jews of unbelief, that he immediately added: Bring him here to me.

And Jesus rebuked him, and the demon went out of him. Not he who was suffering but the demon ought to have been rebuked. Or else he rebuked the boy, and the demon went out of him, because he had been oppressed by the demon on account of his own sins.

And they said: Why could we not cast him out? He said to them: Because of your unbelief. This is what he says in another place: Whatever you ask in my name, believing, you will receive. So whenever we do not receive, it is not the inability of the giver but the fault of those who pray.

If you have faith like a grain of mustard seed, you will say to this mountain: Move from here, and it will move. Some think that faith compared to a grain of mustard seed is said to be small, though the kingdom of heaven is likened to a grain of mustard seed; and since the Apostle says: And if I have all faith, so that I move mountains, great, then, is the faith that is made equal to a grain of mustard seed. The moving of a mountain signifies not that one which we see with the eyes of the flesh, but the one which had been moved by the Lord out of the lunatic. For what does he say? You will say to this mountain: Move from here, and it will move. By this those are to be convicted of folly who contend that the apostles and all believers did not have even a small faith, because none of them moved mountains. For the moving of a mountain from one place to another, and a vain display of signs to be sought, does not profit so much as the moving, for the benefit of all, of that mountain which, through the prophet, is said to corrupt the whole earth.

But this kind is not cast out except through prayer and fasting. While he teaches how a most wicked demon can be expelled, he instructs all for life.

And while they were staying in Galilee, Jesus said to them: The Son of Man is to be betrayed into the hands of men, and they will kill him, and on the third day he will rise again. And they were exceedingly saddened. He always mingles sadness with prosperous things, so that when these come suddenly they may not terrify the apostles, but may be borne by minds that have prepared for them beforehand. For if it saddens them that he is to be killed, what is said ought to gladden them: on the third day he will rise again. Moreover, that they are saddened, and saddened exceedingly, does not come from unbelief, for otherwise they also knew that Peter had been rebuked because he did not savor the things that are of God but those of men; but because, out of love for their master, they cannot bear to hear anything adverse and lowly about him.

And when they had come to Capernaum, those who collected the didrachma came to Peter and said: Does your master not pay the didrachma? He said to them: Yes. After Augustus Caesar, Judea was made tributary, and all were registered by a head tax. Hence Joseph too, with Mary his kinswoman, made his declaration in Bethlehem. Again, since he had been brought up in Nazareth, which is a town of Galilee lying near the city of Capernaum, tribute is demanded of him; and because of the greatness of his signs those who were collecting it do not dare to ask it of him himself, but approach the disciple; or else they ask maliciously whether he pays tribute or opposes the will of Caesar, according to what we also read in another place: Is it lawful to pay tribute to Caesar or not?

And when he had entered the house, Jesus forestalled him, saying. Those who collected the didrachma had approached Peter apart; and when he had entered the house, before Peter can bring it up, the Lord asks, lest the disciples be scandalized at the demand for the tribute, when they see that he knows what was done in his absence.

What do you think, Simon? The kings of the earth, from whom do they take tribute or tax, from their own sons or from strangers? And he said: From strangers. Jesus said to him: Then the sons are free. Our Lord was the son of a king both according to the flesh and according to the spirit, either as begotten of the stock of David or as the Word of the almighty Father. Therefore, as the son of kings, he did not owe tribute; but he who had taken on the humility of the flesh had to fulfill all justice. And we unhappy ones, who are reckoned by the name of Christ and do nothing worthy of so great a majesty: he for our sake both endured the cross and paid tribute; we, for his honor, do not pay tribute, and as though sons of the king we are exempt from taxes.

Go to the sea and cast a hook, and take the fish that comes up first, and when you have opened its mouth you will find a stater; take it and give it to them for me and for you. What I should wonder at first in this place I do not know, whether the foreknowledge or the greatness of the Savior: the foreknowledge, in that he knew the fish had a stater in its mouth and that it would be the first to be caught; the greatness and power, if at his word the stater was at once created in the fish's mouth, and he himself made what was to be by speaking it. But it seems to me, according to the mystical understandings, that this fish which was caught first is the one which was in the depth of the sea and dwelt in the salt and bitter whirlpools, so that through the second Adam the first Adam might be freed, and that which had been found in his mouth, that is, in confession, might be paid for Peter and the Lord. And beautifully the very same price is given, but it is divided, because for Peter, as for a sinner, a price was being paid, whereas our Lord had committed no sin, nor was deceit found in his mouth. That is called a stater which holds two didrachmas, so that the likeness of the flesh might be shown, when both lord and servant are freed by the same price. But understood simply too it edifies the hearer, since the Lord was of such poverty that he did not have the means to pay the tribute for himself and the apostle. But if anyone wishes to object: And how did Judas carry money in his purse? we shall answer: he thought it wrong to turn the property of the poor to his own uses, and he gave us the same example.

Matthew 18

At that hour the disciples came to Jesus, saying: Who, do you think, is greater in the kingdom of heaven? What I have often advised must be observed now too: the causes of each of the Lord's sayings and deeds are to be sought. After the stater was found, after the tribute was paid, what does this sudden question of the apostles mean? At that hour the disciples came to Jesus, saying: Who, do you think, is greater in the kingdom of heaven? Because they had seen the same tribute paid for Peter and for the Lord, they judged from the equality of the price that Peter had been preferred to all the apostles, since in the payment of the tribute he had been put on a level with the Lord; therefore they ask who is greater in the kingdom of heaven. And Jesus, seeing their thoughts and understanding the causes of their error, wishes to heal the desire for glory by a contest of humility.

And Jesus, calling a little child, set him in their midst. Either simply any little child, so that he might seek out that age and show the likeness of innocence; or else he set himself in their midst as the little child, he who had come not to be served but to serve, so that he might give them an example of humility. Others interpret the little child as the Holy Spirit, whom he placed in the hearts of the disciples so that they might exchange pride for humility.

Amen I say to you: Unless you are turned and become like little children, you will not enter the kingdom of heaven. The apostles are not commanded to have the age of little children but their innocence, and that what those possess by their years, these should possess by effort, so that they may be little children in malice, not in wisdom.

Whoever therefore humbles himself like this little child, he is greater in the kingdom of heaven. Just as this little child, whose example I give you, does not persist in anger, does not remember when he has been hurt, is not delighted at the sight of a beautiful woman, does not think one thing and say another, so you too, unless you have such innocence and purity of mind, will not be able to enter the kingdom of heaven. Or alternatively: Whoever humbles himself like this little child, he is greater in the kingdom of heaven, that is, whoever imitates me and humbles himself after my example, so as to cast himself down as far as I cast myself down when I took the form of a servant, he will enter the kingdom of heaven.

And whoever receives one such little child in my name receives me. Whoever is such that he imitates the humility and innocence of Christ, in him Christ is received. And prudently, lest the apostles, when honor has been paid to them, should think themselves the ones honored, he added that they are to be received not for their own merit but in honor of their master.

But whoever causes one of these little ones to stumble. Note that the one who is scandalized is a little child; for the greater do not admit stumbling blocks.

It is better for him that an ass's millstone be hung on his neck and that he be sunk in the depth of the sea. Although this may be a general sentence against all who cause anyone to stumble, yet according to the sequence of the discourse it can also be understood as spoken against the apostles, who, by asking who was greater in the kingdom of heaven, seemed to be contending among themselves about rank, and, had they persisted in this fault, could have destroyed through their own stumbling block those whom they were calling to the faith, when these saw the apostles fighting among themselves over honor. But when he said: It is better for him that an ass's millstone be hung on his neck, he speaks according to the custom of the province, by which this was the penalty for the greater crimes among the Jews of old, that they were sunk in the deep with a stone tied to them. And it is better for him, because it is much better to receive a brief punishment for a fault than to be kept for eternal torments. For the Lord will not punish twice for the same thing.

Woe to the world because of stumbling blocks. It is necessary that stumbling blocks come, but woe to the man through whom the stumbling block comes. Not that it is necessary for stumbling blocks to come (otherwise those who cause the stumbling block would be without fault), but, since it is necessary that stumbling blocks occur in this world, each one lies open to stumbling blocks by his own fault. And at the same time, by a general sentence, Judas is struck, who had prepared his mind for the betrayal.

But if your hand or your foot causes you to stumble, cut it off and throw it from you, and the rest. It is indeed necessary that stumbling blocks come, yet woe to that man who, by his own fault, brings it about that what must necessarily happen in the world happens through him. Therefore every affection is cut off and all kinship is amputated, lest under the pretext of dutiful affection each of the believers lie open to stumbling blocks. If, he says, there is someone so joined to you as hand, foot, eye, and he is useful and attentive and sharp at seeing, but he makes a stumbling block for you and through the discord of his ways drags you into Gehenna, it is better for you to go without his kinship and his fleshly advantages than, while you wish to gain your relatives and close friends, to have a cause of ruin. So let not brother, not wife, not children, not friends, not any affection that can shut us out from the kingdom of heaven be preferred to the love of the Lord. Each of the believers knows what harms him, or in what his mind is solicited and often tempted. It is better to lead a solitary life than to lose eternal life for the necessities of the present life.

See that you do not despise one of these little ones. For I tell you that their angels always see the face of my Father who is in heaven. Above he had said that through the hand and the foot and the eye all kinships and close ties that could cause a stumbling block were to be amputated; so he tempers the severity of the sentence by the precept that follows, saying: See that you do not despise one of these little ones. I so enjoin severity, he says, as to teach clemency mingled with it. As far as lies in you, do not despise them, but through your own salvation seek their healing as well. But if you see them persevering in sins and enslaved to vices, it is better to be saved alone than to perish with many. For their angels in heaven always see the face of the Father. Great is the dignity of souls, that each one has, from the moment of its birth, an angel assigned to its keeping. Hence we read in the Apocalypse of John: To the angel of Ephesus, of Thyatira, and to the angel of Philadelphia, and to the angels of the four remaining churches, write these things. The Apostle too commanded that the heads of women be veiled in the churches because of the angels.

What do you think? If someone has a hundred sheep, and one of them goes astray, does he not leave the ninety-nine on the mountains and go to seek the one that went astray? Following on, he who had said before: See that you do not despise one of these little ones, calls us to clemency, and adds the parable of the ninety-nine sheep left on the mountains and of the one that strayed, which the good shepherd, because it could not walk for its great weakness, carried back on his shoulders to the rest of the flock. Some think that this is the shepherd who, though he was in the form of God, did not think it robbery to be equal with God, but emptied himself, taking the form of a servant, becoming obedient to the Father unto death, even the death of the cross, and that for this reason he came down to earthly things, to save the one little sheep that had perished, that is, the human race. Others, however, think that in the ninety-nine sheep the number of the righteous is understood, and in the one little sheep that of sinners, according to what he said in another place: I did not come to call the righteous but sinners; for those who are well have no need of a physician, but those who are ill. This parable is written in the Gospel according to Luke together with two other parables, of the ten drachmas and of the two sons.

So it is not the will before your Father who is in heaven that one of these little ones should perish. He refers back to the proposition above, about which he had said: See that you do not despise one of these little ones, and teaches that the parable was set down for this reason, that the little ones should not be despised. And in saying: It is not the will before your Father that one of these little ones should perish, it is shown that whenever one of the little ones has perished, he has not perished by the will of the Father.

But if your brother sins against you, go and rebuke him between yourself and him, and the rest. If our brother sins against us and has injured us in any matter, we have the power to forgive, nay rather the necessity, since we are commanded to forgive our debtors their debts; but if anyone sins against God, it is not within our discretion. For the divine Scripture says: If a man sins against a man, the priest will pray for him; but if he sins against God, who will pray for him? We, on the contrary, are kindly when the injury is done to God, and over insults to ourselves we give rein to hatreds. A brother is to be rebuked privately, lest, if he has once lost his shame and modesty, he remain in sin; and if indeed he listens, we gain his soul, and through the salvation of another, salvation is gained for us too; but if he will not listen, let a brother be brought in; and if he does not listen to him either, let a third be brought in as well, either with the aim of correcting him or of summoning him before witnesses. Further, if he will not listen even to them, then it must be told to many, so that they may hold him in detestation, and he who could not be saved by shame may be saved by reproaches. But when it is said: Let him be to you as a heathen and a tax collector, it is shown that he who under the name of a believer does the works of unbelievers deserves greater detestation than those who are openly Gentiles. For according to tropology those are called tax collectors who pursue the gains of the world and exact tolls through trade and frauds and thefts, crimes and perjuries.

Amen I say to you: Whatever you bind on earth shall be bound also in heaven, and whatever you loose on earth shall be loosed also in heaven. Because he had said: But if he does not listen to the Church, let him be to you as a heathen and a tax collector, and the contemptuous brother's hidden reply or silent thought could be this: If you despise me, I too despise you; if you condemn me, you too will be condemned by my sentence, he gave power to the apostles, so that those who are condemned by such men may know that the human sentence is strengthened by the divine sentence, and that whatever has been bound on earth is bound equally in heaven.

Again I say to you that if two of you agree on earth about anything whatever that they ask, it will be done for them by my Father who is in heaven. For where two or three are gathered in my name, there I am in their midst. All the discourse above had called us to concord. Therefore he also promises a reward, so that we may hasten more eagerly to peace, since he says that he will be in the midst between two and three, after the example of the tyrant who wished to test two friends he had taken captive in this way, holding the one and letting the other go, when one had gone back to visit his mother and had given his friend as surety for himself; and when he returned on the appointed day, the tyrant, admiring the good faith of both, asked them to take him as a third. We can also understand this spiritually: that where spirit and soul and body agree and do not have among themselves a war of divergent wills, the flesh lusting against the spirit and the spirit against the flesh, they obtain from the Father whatever they ask; and no one can doubt that the request is for good things where the body wishes to have what the spirit wishes.

Then Peter, coming to him, said: Lord, how often shall my brother sin against me and I forgive him? Up to seven times? and the rest. The Lord's discourse holds together and, like a threefold cord, cannot be broken. Above he had said: See that you do not despise one of these little ones; and he had added: If your brother sins against you, go and rebuke him between yourself and him; and he had promised a reward, saying: If two of you agree on earth, about anything they will obtain what they ask, and I will be in their midst. Roused by this, the apostle Peter asks how often he ought to forgive a brother who sins against him, and with his question he puts forward a verdict: up to seven times, to whom Jesus answered: Not up to seven times but seventy times seven, that is, four hundred and ninety times, so that he should forgive a brother who sins as many times in a day as the brother could not sin.

Therefore the kingdom of heaven is likened to a man, a king, who wished to settle accounts with his servants. It is customary for the Syrians, and especially for the Palestinians, to join parables to all their speech, so that what cannot be held by the hearers through a simple precept may be held through a likeness and examples. So he commanded Peter, under the comparison of the king and Lord and of the servant who, owing ten thousand talents, had obtained pardon by asking it of the Lord, that he too should forgive his fellow servants who sin less. For if that king and Lord so easily forgave the servant who owed ten thousand talents, how much more ought servants to forgive their fellow servants lesser things? To make this clearer, let us put it by an example: if any of us has committed adultery, murder, sacrilege, greater crimes worth ten thousand talents, they are forgiven to those who ask, if they too forgive those who sin less; but if we are implacable because of an insult done to us, and keep up lasting quarrels because of a rather bitter word, do we not see that we are rightly to be thrown into prison, and that by the example of our own deed we bring it about that pardon for our greater offenses is not granted us?

There was brought to him one who owed ten thousand talents. I know that some interpret this man who owed ten thousand talents as the devil; by his wife and children, who are to be sold while he persists in malice, they would have folly and evil thoughts understood; for just as the wife of the righteous man is said to be wisdom, so, they say, the wife of the unrighteous man and sinner is called folly. But how the Lord should forgive him ten thousand talents and he not forgive us, his fellow servants, a hundred denarii, is neither the interpretation of the Church nor one to be accepted by prudent men.

So also my heavenly Father will do to you, if you do not forgive, each of you, his brother from your hearts. A fearful sentence, if the sentence of God is bent and changed according to our mind. If we do not forgive our brothers small things, great things will not be forgiven us by God. And because anyone can say: I have nothing against him, he knows his own affair, he has God for his judge, it is no concern of mine, whatever he wishes to do, I have forgiven him, he confirms his sentence and overturns every pretense of feigned peace, saying: If you do not forgive, each of you, his brother from your hearts.

Matthew 19

And the Pharisees came to him, testing him and saying: Is it lawful for a man to put away his wife for any cause? He had come from Galilee to Judea; for that reason the faction of the Pharisees and scribes asks him whether it is lawful for a man to put away his wife for whatever cause, so as to hold him as in a horned syllogism, and whatever he answered would lie open to capture: if he said that a wife is to be put away for any cause whatever and others married, the preacher of purity would seem to teach things contrary to himself; but if he answered that she ought not to be put away for every cause, he would be held as guilty of sacrilege, as acting against the teaching of Moses, and through Moses, of God. So the Lord tempers his answer in such a way as to pass by the snare, bringing in holy Scripture as testimony and opposing the natural law and God's first sentence to the second, which was granted not by the will of God but by the necessity of sinners.

Have you not read that he who made them from the beginning made them male and female? This is written at the beginning of Genesis. But by saying male and female, he shows that second marriages are to be avoided. For he does not say: male and females, which is what was sought by the repudiation of the former wives, but male and female, so that the partnership of a single marriage might be bound together.

For this reason a man leaves father and mother and will cleave to his wife. Likewise he says: will cleave to his wife, not to wives.

And the two shall be in one flesh. The reward of marriage is that from two there comes to be one flesh. Chastity joined to the spirit becomes one spirit.

What therefore God has joined, let man not separate. God has joined them by making one flesh of man and woman; this man cannot separate, unless perhaps God alone. Man separates when, out of desire for a second wife, we put away the first; God, who also had joined, separates when by consent, for the service of God, because the time is short, we so have wives as though we did not have them.

They say to him: Why then did Moses command to give a bill of divorce and to put her away? They lay open the false charge they had prepared. And yet the Lord had not put forward an opinion of his own, but had recalled the ancient history and the commandments of God.

He says to them: Because Moses, in view of the hardness of your heart, permitted you to put away your wives; but from the beginning it was not so. What he says is of this kind: Can God be contrary to himself, so as to have commanded one thing before and then break his own sentence by a new command? That is not to be thought; but Moses, when he saw that, because of the desire for second wives who were richer or younger or more beautiful, the first wives were being killed or leading a wretched life, preferred to allow discord rather than that hatreds and murders should go on. And at the same time consider that he did not say: God permitted you because of the hardness of your heart, but Moses, so that, according to the Apostle, it is the counsel of a man, not the command of God.

But I say to you that whoever puts away his wife, except for fornication, and marries another commits adultery; and he who marries her who was put away commits adultery. Fornication alone is what overcomes the affection owed to a wife; indeed, when she has divided the one flesh into another and by fornication has separated herself from her husband, she ought not to be kept, lest she bring her husband too under a curse, as Scripture says: He who keeps an adulteress is foolish and impious. Wherever, therefore, there is fornication and the suspicion of fornication, the wife is freely put away. And because it could happen that someone might bring a false charge against an innocent woman and, for the sake of a second marriage union, fasten a crime on the former wife, he is commanded to put away the first wife on these terms: that he not have a second while the first is living. For what he says is this: If you put away your wife not out of lust but because of an injury, why, having experienced an unhappy first marriage, do you thrust yourself into the danger of a new one? Moreover, because it could happen that by the same law the wife too might give her husband a bill of divorce, the same caution is enjoined, that she not take a second husband. And because a harlot, and one who had once been an adulteress, did not fear disgrace, it is enjoined on the second husband that if he marries such a woman he is under the charge of adultery.

The disciples say to him: If such is the case of a man with his wife, it is not expedient to marry. Heavy is the burden of wives, if it is not lawful to put them away except for the cause of fornication. For what if she is a drunkard, if irascible, if of bad morals, if wanton, if gluttonous, if a gadabout, if quarrelsome and abusive, must such a one be kept? Willing or unwilling, she must be borne. For when we were free, we subjected ourselves to servitude of our own will. The apostles, then, seeing the heavy yoke of wives, utter the feeling of their mind and say: If such is the case of a man with his wife, it is not expedient to marry.

He said: Not all accept this word, but those to whom it is given. Let no one think that by this word either fate or fortune is brought in, as if those are virgins to whom it has been given by God, or whom some chance has brought to it; rather, it is given to those who have asked, who have willed, who have labored to receive it. For to everyone who asks it will be given, and he who seeks will find, and to him who knocks it will be opened.

For there are eunuchs who were born so from their mother's womb, and there are eunuchs who were made so by men, and there are eunuchs who castrated themselves for the kingdom of heaven. He who can accept it, let him accept it. There is a threefold kind of eunuchs, two carnal and the third spiritual. Some are those who are born so from their mother's womb; others, those whom captivity makes, or the luxuries of matrons. The third are those who castrated themselves for the kingdom of heaven, who, though they could be men, become eunuchs for Christ. To these the reward is promised; but to the former, for whom chastity is a necessity and not a choice, nothing at all is owed. We can also put it another way: those are eunuchs from their mother's womb who are of a colder nature and do not seek after lust; and others are those made by men, whom either the philosophers make, or who are softened into women for the worship of idols, or who, by heretical persuasion, feign chastity so as to counterfeit the truth of religion. But none of them attains the kingdom of heaven except him who has castrated himself for Christ. Hence he adds: He who can accept it, let him accept it, so that each may consider his own strength, whether he can fulfill the precepts of virginity and purity. For chastity in itself is alluring and draws anyone to itself. But one's strength must be considered, so that he who can accept it, let him accept it. It is the voice of the Lord as of one exhorting and urging his soldiers on to the reward of purity: He who can accept it, let him accept it; he who can fight, let him fight, conquer and triumph.

Then little children were brought to him, that he might lay his hands on them and pray; but the disciples rebuked them. Not that they were unwilling for them to be blessed by both the hand and the voice of the Savior, but because, not yet having the fullest faith, they thought that he, after the manner of men, was being wearied by the importunity of those who brought them.

Let the little children be, and do not forbid them to come to me; for of such is the kingdom of heaven. He said significantly of such, not of these, to show that it is not age that reigns but character, and that the reward is promised to those who have a like innocence and simplicity, the Apostle too agreeing with the same sentence: Brothers, do not become children in your understanding, but be little children in malice; but in understanding, be perfect.

Behold, one came up and said to him: Good Teacher, what good shall I do that I may have eternal life? This man who asks how he may attain eternal life is young and rich and proud, and, according to another evangelist, he asks not with the desire of a learner but as one testing.

Why do you ask me about the good? One is good, God. Because he had called him a good teacher and had not confessed him God or the Son of God, he learns that a man, however holy, is not good in comparison with God, of whom it is said: Confess to the Lord, for he is good. But lest anyone think that, in God's being called good, the Son of God is excluded from goodness, let us read in another place: The good shepherd lays down his life for his sheep; and in the prophet, a good spirit and a good land. So the Savior too did not refuse the testimony to his goodness, but excluded the error of a teacher apart from God.

If you wish to enter into life, keep the commandments. He says to him: Which? And Jesus said: You shall not commit murder, you shall not commit adultery, and the rest, and you shall love your neighbor as yourself. That this young man is a tempter we can prove from this too, that when the Lord said to him: If you wish to come to life, keep the commandments, he again asks deceitfully which those commandments are, as if he had not read them himself, or as if the Lord could command things contrary to God.

The young man says to him: All these I have kept; what do I still lack? The young man lies. For if he had fulfilled in deed what is set down in the commandments: You shall love your neighbor as yourself, how is it that afterward, on hearing: Go, sell what you have and give to the poor, he went away sad, because he had many possessions?

Jesus said to him: If you wish to be perfect, go, sell what you have and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me. It is in our power whether we wish to be perfect. Yet whoever wishes to be perfect ought to sell what he has, and not to sell in part, as Ananias and Sapphira did, but to sell all, and when he has sold it, to give everything to the poor, and so to prepare for himself a treasure in the kingdom of heaven. Nor is this enough for perfection unless, after despising riches, he follows the Savior, that is, leaving evil things behind, does good. For a purse is more easily despised than the will. Many who leave riches behind do not follow the Lord. But he follows the Lord who is his imitator and walks in his footsteps. For he who says he believes in Christ ought himself also to walk as he walked.

He went away sad, for he had many possessions. This is the sadness that leads to death, and the cause of the sadness is given: that he had many possessions, that is, thorns and thistles, which choked the Lord's seed.

And Jesus said to his disciples: Amen I say to you that a rich man will enter the kingdom of heaven with difficulty. And how did Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, rich men, enter the kingdom of heaven, and how are Matthew and Zacchaeus in the Gospel proclaimed by the Lord's testimony, once they had left their riches? But it must be considered that at the time they entered they had ceased to be rich. So long, then, as they are rich, they will not enter. And yet, because riches are despised with difficulty, he did not say: It is impossible for the rich to enter the kingdom of heaven, but with difficulty. Where difficulty is stated, impossibility is not alleged, but rarity is shown.

And again I say to you: It is easier for a camel to pass through the eye of a needle than for a rich man to enter the kingdom of heaven. By this saying it is shown that it is not difficult but impossible. For if, as a camel cannot enter through the eye of a needle, so a rich man cannot enter the kingdom of heaven, none of the rich will be saved. But if we read in Isaiah how the camels of Midian and Ephah come to Jerusalem with gifts and offerings, and how those who were formerly bent and twisted by the perversity of vices enter the gates of Jerusalem, we shall see how these camels too, to which the rich are compared, when they have laid down the heavy burden of sins and the crookedness of the whole body, can enter by the narrow and strait way that leads to life. But when the disciples asked, marveling at the severity of the saying: Who then will be saved? he tempered the severity of the sentence by his clemency, saying: What with men is impossible is possible with God.

Then Peter answered and said to him: Behold, we have left all things and have followed you; what then will there be for us? Great confidence! Peter was a fisherman, he had not been rich, he sought his food by hand and craft, and yet he speaks confidently: we have left all things. And because merely leaving is not enough, he joins what is perfect: and have followed you. We have done what you commanded; what reward, then, will you give us?

And Jesus said to them: Amen I say to you that you who have followed me, in the regeneration, when the Son of Man sits on the seat of his majesty, will sit on twelve seats, judging the twelve tribes of Israel. He did not say: who have left all things, for this Crates the philosopher also did, and many others have despised riches; but who have followed me, which belongs properly to the apostles and to believers. In the regeneration, when the Son of Man sits on the seat of his majesty, when from the dead they rise incorrupt out of corruption, you too will sit on the thrones of judges, condemning the twelve tribes of Israel, because when you believed they refused to believe.

And everyone who has left house or brothers or sisters or father or mother or wife or children or fields for my name's sake will receive a hundredfold and will possess eternal life. But many who are first will be last, and the last first. This passage agrees with that sentence in which the Savior says: I came not to send peace but a sword; for I came to separate a man from his father, and the mother from the daughter, and the daughter-in-law from the mother-in-law; and a man's enemies are those of his own household. Those, therefore, who for the faith of Christ and the preaching of the Gospel have despised all affections, and riches, and the pleasures of the world, these will receive a hundredfold and will possess eternal life. On the occasion of this sentence some bring in a thousand years after the resurrection, saying that then a hundredfold of all the things we have given up, and eternal life, will be rendered to us, not understanding that, even if in other things the promise is fitting, in the case of wives the baseness is plain: that he who has given up one for the Lord should receive a hundred in the future. This, then, is the sense: He who has given up carnal things for the Savior will receive spiritual things, which in comparison and by their worth will be as if the number one hundred were compared with a small number. Hence the Apostle too, who had given up only one house and the small fields of one province, says: As having nothing and possessing all things.

Matthew 20

The kingdom of heaven is like a householder who went out early in the morning to hire labourers for his vineyard. And having made an agreement with the labourers for a denarius a day, he sent them into his vineyard. This parable, or likeness of the kingdom of heaven, is understood from what has gone before it. For it is written before it: Many who are first will be last, and the last first, the Lord giving precedence not to time but to faith; and it says that the householder went out early in the morning to hire labourers for his vineyard and fixed a denarius as the price of the work. Then, having gone out about the third hour, he saw others standing idle in the marketplace and promised them by no means a denarius but what is just; at the sixth hour too and at the ninth he did likewise; and at the eleventh he found others standing who had been idle the whole day, and sent them into the vineyard; and when evening had come, he ordered his steward to begin paying from the last, that is, from the labourers of the eleventh hour down to the labourers of the first hour; and all alike, roused to envy against the last, charged the householder with injustice, not because they received less than had been agreed, but because they wanted to receive more than those upon whom the clemency of the hirer had poured itself out. It seems to me that the labourers of the first hour are Samuel and Jeremiah and John the Baptist, who can say with the psalmist: From my mother's womb you are my God. The labourers of the third hour are those who began to serve God from puberty; of the sixth hour, those who took up the yoke of Christ at a mature age; of the ninth, those who did so when already declining toward old age; and of the eleventh, those who did so in extreme old age; and yet all alike receive the reward, though the labour is different. There are those who explain this parable otherwise. They hold that at the first hour Adam and the rest of the patriarchs down to Noah were sent into the vineyard; at the third, Noah himself down to Abraham and the circumcision given to him; at the sixth, from Abraham down to Moses, when the law was given; at the ninth, Moses himself and the prophets; at the eleventh, the apostles and the people of the Gentiles, whom all envy. Hence, understanding this very thing, when it was already past the eleventh hour, near sunset and toward evening, John the evangelist says: My little children, it is the last hour. And consider at the same time that the injustice of the householder, which all alike accuse in the case of the labourers of the eleventh hour, they do not perceive in their own case. For if the householder is unjust, he is unjust not toward one but toward all, since the labourer of the third hour did not labour as much as he who was sent into the vineyard from the first hour; likewise the labourer of the sixth hour laboured less than the labourer of the third hour, and the labourer of the ninth less than the labourer of the sixth hour. So every earlier calling envies the Gentiles and is tormented at the grace of the Gospel. Hence the Savior too, concluding the parable: The first, he says, will be last, and the last first, because the Jews are turned from the head into the tail, and we are changed from the tail into the head.

Friend, I do you no wrong. I have read in someone's book that this friend who is rebuked by the householder, the labourer of the first hour, is to be understood as the first-formed man and those who believed in that time.

Did you not agree with me for a denarius? The denarius bears the figure of the king. So you have received the wage that I had promised you, that is, my image and likeness; what more do you seek? And you desire not so much to receive more yourself as that another should receive nothing, as though the merit of the reward were lessened by being shared with another.

Take what is yours and go. The Jew under the law is saved not by grace but by work. For he who does it shall live in it. Hence it is said to him:

Or is your eye evil because I am good? The same thing is meant by that parable of Luke as well, where the elder son envies the younger and does not want him received back as a penitent, and accuses the father of injustice. And so that we may know that this is the sense we have stated, the title of this parable and its end agree: So, he says, the last will be first and the first last; for many are called but few are chosen.

And Jesus, going up to Jerusalem, took his twelve disciples and said to them: Behold, we are going up to Jerusalem, and the Son of Man will be handed over to the chiefs of the elders and to the scribes, and they will condemn him to death and hand him over to the Gentiles to be mocked and scourged and crucified, and on the third day he will rise again. He had often told the disciples this very thing, but because, with many matters discussed in between, what they had heard could slip from their memory, being about to go to Jerusalem and to take the apostles with him, he prepares them for the trial, so that when persecution and the ignominy of the cross come they may not be scandalized.

Then the mother of the sons of Zebedee came to him with her sons, worshipping him and asking something of him. He said to her: What do you want? She said to him: Say that these two sons of mine may sit, one at your right hand and one at your left, in your kingdom. Where does the mother of the sons of Zebedee get the notion of a kingdom, that, when the Lord had said: The Son of Man will be handed over to the chief priests and the scribes, and they will condemn him to death and hand him over to the Gentiles to be mocked and scourged and crucified, and had announced the ignominy of the passion to his fearful disciples, she should ask for the glory of one triumphing? For this reason, as I think: because after everything else the Lord had said: and on the third day he will rise again, the woman thought that he would reign immediately after the resurrection, and that what is promised for the second coming was to be fulfilled in the first; and with a woman's eagerness she longs for present things, unmindful of things to come. But that the Lord asks, and answered her request: What do you want? does not come from ignorance; rather it is said in the person of him who was to be scourged and crucified, just as also in the case of the woman with the flow of blood: Who touched me? and of Lazarus: Where have you laid him? and in the Old Testament too: Adam, where are you? And I will go down and see whether they are doing according to the cry that has come to me; and if it is not so, that I may know. The mother of the sons of Zebedee, then, makes her request out of a woman's error and the affection of love, not knowing what she was asking. Nor is it surprising if she is convicted of inexperience, when it is said of Peter, when he wants to make three tabernacles, not knowing what he was saying.

But Jesus answered and said: You do not know what you are asking. The mother makes the request, and the Lord speaks to the disciples, understanding that her prayers came down from the will of her sons.

Can you drink the cup that I am to drink? By the cup in the divine Scriptures we understand the passion: Father, if it is possible, let this cup pass from me; and in the psalm: What shall I render to the Lord for all that he has rendered to me? I will take the cup of salvation and call upon the name of the Lord; and at once he adds what this cup is: Precious in the sight of the Lord is the death of his saints.

He said to them: My cup you will indeed drink, but to sit at my right hand and at my left is not mine to give you, but to those for whom it has been prepared by my Father. It is asked how the sons of Zebedee, namely James and John, drank the cup of martyrdom, when Scripture relates that only James the apostle was beheaded by Herod, while John ended his life by a death of his own. But if we read the histories of the Church, in which it is reported that he too, for the sake of martyrdom, was put into a jar of boiling oil, and came out from there as an athlete of Christ to receive the crown, and was at once banished to the island of Patmos, we shall see that his spirit did not fall short of martyrdom, and that John drank the cup of confession, which the three young men in the furnace of fire also drank, although the persecutor did not shed their blood. But what he says: to sit at my right hand and at my left is not mine to give you, but to those for whom it has been prepared by my Father, is to be understood thus: The kingdom of heaven belongs not to the one who gives but to the one who receives; for there is no respect of persons with God, but whoever shows himself such that he becomes worthy of the kingdom of heaven, he will receive what has been prepared not for a person but for a life; if then you are such as to attain the kingdom of heaven which my Father has prepared for the triumphant and the victors, you too will receive it. Others want it to have been said of Moses and Elijah, whom a little before they had seen on the mountain talking with him, but to me it by no means seems so. For the names of those who sit in the kingdom of heaven are not stated for this reason: lest, if a few were named, the rest should be thought excluded.

And the ten, hearing it, were indignant at the two brothers. The ten apostles are not indignant with the mother of the sons of Zebedee, nor do they lay the boldness of the request to the woman, but to the sons, because, ignorant of their own measure, they had blazed up with immoderate desire; to them the Lord too had said: You do not know what you are asking. But it is understood, either from the Lord's answer or from the indignation of the apostles, that the sons had sent their mother in to ask for great things.

But Jesus called them to him and said: You know that the princes of the Gentiles lord it over them, and the rest. The humble and gentle master neither rebukes the two who asked for their immoderate desire nor reproves the other ten for indignation and envy, but sets forth such an example as to teach that he is greater who has been lesser, and that he becomes lord who is the servant of all. In vain therefore did those seek immoderate things, or do these grieve over the desire for greater things, since one comes to the summit of the virtues not by power but by humility. In short, he sets forth his own example, so that, if they held his words cheap, they might be put to shame by his deeds, and he says:

Even as the Son of Man did not come to be served but to serve. Note what we have often said: that the one who serves is called the Son of Man. And to give his life as a redemption for many, when he took the form of a servant in order to shed his blood for the world. And he did not say to give his life as a redemption for all, but for many, that is, for those who would be willing to believe.

And as they were going out from Jericho, a great crowd followed him. And behold, two blind men sitting by the way heard that Jesus was passing by, and they cried out, saying: Lord, have mercy on us, son of David. But the crowd rebuked them, that they should be silent. But they cried out the more, saying: Lord, have mercy on us, son of David. There were many robbers in Jericho who were accustomed to kill and wound those going out and going down from Jerusalem; therefore the Lord came to Jericho with his disciples, to free the wounded and to draw a great crowd along with him. And indeed, when he wished to go out from Jericho, a great crowd followed him. If he had stayed in Jerusalem and had never come down to lowly places, the crowd would to this day be sitting in darkness and in the shadow of death. But there were also two blind men by the way. He calls them blind who could not yet say: In your light we shall see light; by the way, because they seemed indeed to have knowledge of the law, but did not know the way, which is Christ. Most take them to be the Pharisees and the Sadducees, but others both peoples, of the Old Testament and of the New, because the one following the written law, the other the natural law, was blind without Christ. These, because they could not see by themselves, heard the proclamations of the Savior and confessed him the son of David. But if both blind men are referred to the people of the Jews, what follows: the crowd rebuked them, is to be understood of the heathen, whom the Apostle warns not to boast and be proud against their root, but, since they themselves, through the error of the former, have been grafted from the wild olive into the good olive, they ought by no means to envy the salvation of the former.

Have mercy on us, son of David. They are rebuked by the crowds and nonetheless are not silent, but repeat the very same thing all the more often, to show a desire full of the true light.

Jesus stood still and called them and said. They were blind; they did not know where to go; they could not follow the Savior. There are many pits in Jericho, many crags and precipices sloping down into the depths; therefore the Lord stands still so that they may be able to come, and orders them to be called so that the crowds may not hinder them, and asks, as though not knowing, what they want, so that from the blind men's answer the infirmity may be made plain and the power may be recognized from the remedy.

And Jesus, having pity on them, touched their eyes, and at once they saw and followed him. He touches their eyes, and the craftsman supplies what nature had not given, or at any rate what infirmity had taken away; mercy bestows it, and at once they saw and followed him. Those who before sat huddled in Jericho and knew only how to cry out afterward follow Jesus not so much with their feet as with their virtues.

Matthew 21

And when they drew near to Jerusalem and came to Bethphage, to the Mount of Olives, then Jesus sent two disciples, saying to them: Go into the village that is opposite you, and at once you will find an ass tied and a colt with her; loose them and bring them to me; and if anyone says anything to you, say: The Lord has need of them, and at once he will let them go. He goes out from Jericho, having led out from there very many crowds, and, having restored health to the blind, he draws near to Jerusalem, enriched with great wares; having restored salvation to those who believe, he desires to enter the city of peace and the place of the vision of God and the citadel of the watchman. And when he had drawn near to Jerusalem and had come to Bethphage, to the house of jaws (which was a little village of the priests and bore the type of confession and was situated on the Mount of Olives, where is the light of knowledge, where is rest from labors and sorrows), he sent his two disciples, θεωρητικὴν καὶ πρακτικήν, that is, knowledge and works, to enter the village, and he said to them: Go into the village that is opposite you. For it was opposite the apostles and would not accept the yoke of teachings. And at once, he says, you will find an ass tied and a colt with her; loose them and bring them to me. The ass was tied with many chains of sins; the colt too, frisky and impatient of the bridle, was with its mother; according to the Gospel of Luke it had many masters, being subject not to one error and doctrine; and yet the many masters who claimed for themselves an unlawful power, seeing that the true Lord had come and his servants who had been sent to loose them, do not dare to resist. But what the ass and the foal of the ass are, we say below.

Now this was done that what was spoken through the prophet might be fulfilled, who says: Say to the daughter of Zion: Behold, your king comes to you, meek, sitting upon an ass and a colt, the foal of a beast of burden. This is written in the prophet Zechariah, of whom it will be spoken more fully in its own place, if span of life is granted. For now we must touch briefly on this: that according to the letter, in the short stretch of the journey, he did not rest sitting upon both animals. For either he sat on the ass and the colt was without a rider, or, if he made use of the colt for sitting (which is more fitting), the ass was led along free. Therefore, since the history involves either impossibility or unseemliness, we are carried over to higher things, so that this ass, which was a beast of burden and tamed and had drawn the yoke of the law, may be understood as the synagogue, and the foal of the ass, frisky and free, as the people of the Gentiles, upon whom Jesus sat, his two disciples having been sent to them, one to the circumcision and the other to the Gentiles.

And the disciples went and did as Jesus had commanded them, and they brought the ass and the colt and put their garments upon them and made him sit on top. This colt and this ass, on which the apostles spread their garments so that Jesus may sit more softly, were naked before the coming of the Savior, and, with many claiming dominion over them, they shivered without covering; but after they had received the apostolic garments and had been made more beautiful, they had the Lord as their rider. Now the apostolic garment can be understood either as the teaching of the virtues or as the exposition of the Scriptures, or as the varieties of the doctrines of the Church; unless the soul has been instructed and adorned with these, it does not deserve to have the Lord as its rider.

And a very great crowd spread their garments on the road. See the difference of each person. The apostles put their garments upon the ass; the crowd, which is of lower rank, spreads them beneath the ass's feet, so that it may nowhere strike against a stone, may not tread on a thorn, may not slip into a pit.

And others cut branches from the trees and spread them on the road. From the fruit-bearing trees with which the Mount of Olives is planted they cut branches and spread them on the road, to make the crooked straight and to level the uneven, so that Christ, the conqueror of demons and of vices, might advance more straightly and more safely in the heart of believers.

And the crowds that went before and that followed cried out, saying: Hosanna to the son of David; blessed is he who is to come in the name of the Lord; hosanna in the highest. Since the history is plain, let us follow the spiritual order of discussion. The crowds that had gone out from Jericho and had followed the Savior and his disciples, after they saw the foal of the ass loosed, which before had been tied, and adorned with the garments of the apostles, and the Lord Jesus sitting upon it, laid their garments beneath and spread the road with branches of trees; and when they had done all this in deed, they also bestow the testimony of the voice, and those going before and those following resound not with a brief and silent confession but with the fullest shout: Hosanna to the son of David; blessed is he who is to come in the name of the Lord. And in saying: The crowds that went before and that followed, he shows that both peoples, those who believed in the Lord before the Gospel and those who believed after the Gospel, praise Jesus with the harmonious voice of confession, and that, after the example of the parable above, the workers of different hours receive the one reward of faith. Moreover, what the following words mean: Hosanna to the son of David, I remember that I said many years ago, in a short letter to Damasus, then bishop of the city of Rome, and now I will touch on it briefly. In Psalm 117 (118), which was plainly written about the coming of the Savior, among other things we read this too: The stone which the builders rejected, this has become the head of the corner; this was done by the Lord, this is wonderful in our eyes; this is the day which the Lord has made; let us exult and rejoice in it; and at once there is joined: O Lord, save me; O Lord, prosper well; blessed is he who is to come in the name of the Lord; we have blessed you from the house of the Lord, and the rest. Where the LXX translators have: O Lord, save me, in the Hebrew we read: Anna adonai osi anna, which Symmachus translated more plainly, saying: I beseech you, Lord, save me, I beseech you. Let no one, then, think that the word is composed of two words, namely a Greek and a Hebrew one, but that it is wholly Hebrew and signifies that the coming of Christ is the salvation of the world. Hence there also follows: Blessed is he who is to come in the name of the Lord, the Savior too confirming this very thing in the Gospel: I have come in the name of my Father, and you have not received me; another will come in his own name, and you will receive him. Further, by what is joined: Hosanna (that is, salvation) in the highest, it is clearly shown that the coming of Christ is the salvation not only of men but of the whole world, joining earthly things to heavenly, that every knee should bow to him, of those in heaven and on earth and in the lower world.

And when he had entered Jerusalem, the whole city was stirred, saying: Who is this? As Jesus enters with the crowd, the whole city of Jerusalem is stirred, marveling at the throng, not knowing the truth, and saying: Who is this? We read this in another place too, where the angels say: Who is this king of glory? But while others are either in doubt or asking, the common little crowd confesses, beginning from lesser things so as to arrive at greater, and says:

This is Jesus, the prophet from Nazareth of Galilee. The prophet whom Moses too had said would come like himself, and who among the Greeks is properly written with the article; and from Nazareth of Galilee, because he had been brought up there, so that the flower of the field might be nourished in the flower of the virtues.

And Jesus entered the temple of God and cast out all who sold and bought in the temple, and he overturned the tables of the money-changers and the chairs of those who sold doves, and he says to them: It is written: My house shall be called a house of prayer, but you have made it a den of robbers.
