Accompanied by the crowd of believers, which had spread its garments so that the colt might go with unhurt foot, Jesus enters the temple and casts out all who sold and bought in the temple, and he overturned the tables of the money-changers and scattered the chairs of those who sold doves, and he said to them, bringing forward testimony from the holy Scriptures, that his Father's house ought to be a house of prayer, not a den of robbers or a house of trade, as is written in another evangelist. And this must first be known: that, in accordance with the commands of the law, since the temple of the Lord was the most august in the whole world, and the people of the Jews flocked there from almost all regions, countless victims were sacrificed, especially on feast days, of bulls, rams and goats, while the poor, so as not to be without a sacrifice, offered young doves and turtledoves; and it often happened that those who had come from far away did not have victims. So the priests devised how they might make plunder of the people, and they sold all the animals that were needed for the sacrifices, so that they might both sell to those who did not have them and themselves receive back again what had been bought. This scheme of theirs was frequently defeated by the poverty of those who came, who lacked the means and not only had no victims but had not even the wherewithal to buy birds and cheap little gifts. So they also set up money-changers to lend money on security; but because it was commanded in the law that no one should take interest, and money lent at interest could be of no use when it brought no profit and sometimes lost the principal, they devised yet another trick: in place of money-changers to make collybists, a word whose proper sense the Latin language does not express. Among them collyba is the name for what we call sweetmeats or cheap little gifts, for example roasted chickpeas and raisins and fruits of various kinds. So, because the collybists who had lent the money could not take interest, in place of interest they took various kinds of goods, so as to exact in these things, which are bought with coins, what was not lawful in coin; as if Ezekiel had not guarded against this very thing, saying: You shall not take interest and increase. The Lord, seeing such trade, or robbery, in his Father's house, roused by the ardor of the spirit, according to what is written in Psalm 68 (69): The zeal of your house has eaten me up, made himself a whip of cords and cast so great a multitude of men out of the temple, saying: It is written: My house shall be called a house of prayer, but you have made it a den of robbers. For he is a robber, and turns the temple of God into a den of robbers, who pursues gain from religion, and whose worship is not so much the worship of God as an occasion for trade. So much according to the history. But according to the mystical senses Jesus daily enters his Father's temple and casts out all, bishops and presbyters and deacons as well as laymen and the whole crowd, from his Church, and holds those who sell and those who buy alike guilty of one crime; for it is written: Freely you have received, freely give. He also overturns the tables of the money-changers. Note that because of the avarice of the priests the altars of God are called the tables of money-changers. And he overturns the chairs of those who sell doves, who sell the grace of the Holy Spirit and do everything to devour the peoples subject to them, of whom it is said: Who devour my people like food of bread. According to the simple understanding, the doves were not on chairs but in cages, unless perhaps the dealers in doves sat on chairs, which is utterly absurd, because in chairs it is rather the dignity of teachers that is indicated, a dignity that is brought to nothing when it has been mixed with gain. What we have said of the churches, let each one understand of himself as well. For the Apostle Paul says: You are the temple of God, and the Holy Spirit dwells in you. Let there be no trade in the house of our heart, no commerce of selling and buying, no greed for gifts, lest Jesus enter angry and stern and cleanse his temple in no other way than by applying the whip, so that out of a den of robbers and a house of trade he may make a house of prayer.

And the blind and the lame came to him in the temple, and he healed them. Had he not overturned the tables of the money-changers and the chairs of those who sold doves, the blind and the lame would not have deserved to receive back their former light and their quick step.

But the chief priests and the scribes, seeing the wonderful things that he did and the children crying out in the temple and saying: Hosanna to the son of David, were indignant and said to him: Do you hear what these are saying? Most people think that the greatest of the signs is that Lazarus was raised, that the man blind from the womb received light, that at the Jordan the voice of the Father was heard, that, transfigured on the mountain, he showed the glory of one triumphing. To me, among all the signs he did, this seems more wonderful: that one man, at that time despised and so lowly that he was afterward crucified, while the scribes and Pharisees raged against him and saw their gains being destroyed, was able, at the blows of a single whip, to cast out so great a multitude, to overturn the tables and break the chairs, and to do other things which an endless army would not have done. For something fiery and starlike shone from his eyes, and the majesty of the divinity gleamed in his face. And though the priests do not dare to lay a hand on him, they nevertheless slander his works, and the testimony of the people and of the children who cried out: Hosanna to the son of David, they turn into slander, namely that this is not said except to the Son of God alone. Let bishops, then, and men however holy, see with how great danger they allow these things to be said to them, if to the Lord, to whom this was truly said, it is charged as a crime because the faith of believers was not yet firm.

But Jesus says to them: Certainly; have you never read: Out of the mouth of infants and sucklings you have perfected praise? How moderately tempered the statement, and the reply leaning both ways and not open to slander! He did not say what the scribes wanted to hear: The children do well to bear witness to me; nor again: They are wrong, they are children, you ought to make allowance for their age; but he brings forward an example from Psalm 8, so that, while the Lord was silent, the testimony of the Scriptures might confirm the words of the children.

And leaving them, he went out outside the city to Bethany and stayed there. He left the unbelieving and, going out of the city of those who contradicted him, went to Bethany, which is interpreted house of obedience, already then prefiguring the calling of the Gentiles; and he stayed there because he could not remain in Israel. This too must be understood: that he was of such poverty and flattered no one to such a degree that in the greatest city he found no host, no lodging, but lived in a tiny field with Lazarus and his sisters; for Bethany is their village.

Now in the morning, returning to the city, he was hungry, and seeing a single fig tree beside the road, he came to it and found nothing on it but leaves only. And he said to it: Let fruit never grow from you forever. And the fig tree withered at once. And the disciples, seeing it, marveled, saying: How did it wither at once? When the darkness of night had been dispelled and the morning light was shining, and midday was near, in which the Lord was to illumine the world with his passion, as he was returning to the city he was hungry, either showing the reality of human flesh, or hungering for the salvation of believers and burning at the unbelief of Israel. And when he had seen a single tree, which we understand as the synagogue and the assembly of the Jews, beside the road (for it had the law, and it was beside the road because it did not believe in the Way), he came to it, standing as it was and immovable and not having the feet of the Gospel, and he found nothing on it but leaves only: the rustling of promises, the traditions of the Pharisees, boasting of the law, ornaments of words, without any fruits of truth. Hence another evangelist also says: For it was not yet the time, either because the time of Israel's salvation had not yet come, since the people of the Gentiles had not yet come in, or because the time of faith had passed, since, having come to Israel first and been spurned, he had passed over to the nations. And he said to it: Let fruit never grow from you, either forever or to the age; for the Greek word αἰών signifies both. And the fig tree withered, because, when the Lord was hungry, it did not have the food he had desired. But the leaves withered in such a way that the trunk itself remained, and, though the branches were broken, the root stayed green, so that in the last time, if they are willing to believe, it may sprout shoots of faith, and the Scripture may be fulfilled which says: There is hope for a tree. But according to the letter, the Lord, being about to suffer among the peoples and to bear the stumbling block of the cross, had to strengthen the minds of the disciples by anticipating it with a sign. Hence the disciples too marvel, saying: How did it wither at once? The Savior, then, could have withered his enemies too by the same power, had he not been awaiting their salvation through repentance.

And Jesus answered and said to them: Amen I say to you: If you have faith and do not hesitate, you will do not only this of the fig tree, but even if you say to a mountain: Lift yourself up and cast yourself into the sea, it will be done. The dogs of the Gentiles bark against us in their volumes, which they have left behind as a memorial of their own impiety, asserting that the apostles did not have faith because they could not move mountains. To them we shall answer that many signs were done by the Lord, according to the testimony of John the evangelist, which, if they had been written, the world could not contain; not that the world could not have contained the volumes, which, however many they may be, one little bookcase or one book-chest can contain, but that it could not bear the greatness of the signs, on account of their wonder and its own unbelief. Therefore we believe that the apostles did these things too, but that they were not written for this reason, lest a greater occasion for contradicting be given to unbelievers. Otherwise, let us ask them whether they believe these signs which are narrated in writing or not; and when we have seen that they are unbelieving, we shall prove as a consequence that those who did not believe the lesser would not have believed the greater either. So much against them. But we, as we have already said before, should understand the mountain as the devil, who is proud and vaunts himself against his creator, who is called by the prophet the corrupt mountain; and when he has possessed a man's soul and has become rooted in it, he can be moved by the apostles, and by those who are like the apostles, into the sea, that is, into places salty and tossing and bitter, which have none of the sweetness of God. This same thing is read in the psalms too: We will not fear while the earth is troubled and the mountains are moved into the heart of the sea.

And when he had come into the temple, the chief priests and the elders of the people came to him as he was teaching, saying: By what power do you do these things, and who gave you this power? In different words they build up the same slander as above, when they said: By Beelzebub, the prince of demons, this man casts out demons. For when they say: By what power do you do these things, they doubt the power of God and want it to be understood that what he does is the devil's; and adding further: who gave you this power, they most plainly deny the Son of God, whom they think does signs not by his own powers but by another's.

Jesus answered and said to them: I also will ask you one word, and if you tell it to me, I also will tell you by what power I do these things. The baptism of John, where was it from, from heaven or from men? and the rest. This is what is commonly said: Into a bad knot in a tree a bad nail or wedge must be driven. The Lord could have refuted the slander of those tempting him by an open answer, but he prudently asks a question, so that they may be condemned either by their own silence or by their own statement. For if they had answered that the baptism of John was from heaven, as they, wise in malice, had worked it out among themselves, the reply followed: Why then were you not baptized by John? If they had chosen to say that it was put together by human deceit and had had nothing divine, they feared a riot of the people. For all the assembled multitudes had received the baptism of John and held him thus, as a prophet. So the most impious faction answered and used a word of humility, by which it said that it did not know, to fit together its snares.

He too said to them: Neither do I tell you by what power I do these things. They lied in answering that they did not know; it followed, then, according to their answer, that the Lord too should say: Neither do I know; but the truth cannot lie; and he said: Neither do I tell you. By this he shows both that they knew but would not answer and that he knew and for this reason would not tell, because they kept silent about what they knew; and at once he brings in a parable by which he both convicts them of impiety and teaches that the kingdom of God is to be transferred to the Gentiles.

But what do you think? A man had two sons, and coming to the first he said: Go today, work in my vineyard. But he answered and said: I will not; but afterward, moved by repentance, he went. And coming to the other he spoke likewise. And he answered and said: I go, lord, and he did not go. These are the two sons who are also described in Luke's parable, the thrifty one and the prodigal, and of whom the prophet Zechariah speaks: I took for myself two rods; one I called Beauty and the other I called Cord, and I fed the flock. First it is said to the people of the Gentiles, through the knowledge of the natural law: Go and work in my vineyard, that is: what you do not want done to you, do not do to another. It answered proudly: I will not; but afterward, at the coming of the Savior, having repented, it worked in the vineyard of God and corrected the stubbornness of its word by labor. But the second son is the people of the Jews, which answered Moses: All things whatsoever the Lord says we will do, and did not go into the vineyard, because, having killed the householder's son, it thought itself the heir. Others, however, think that the parable is not about the Gentiles and the Jews but simply about sinners and the just, the Lord himself too afterward expounding his proposition: Amen I say to you that tax collectors and harlots go before you in the kingdom of God; because those who by evil works had refused to serve God afterward received the baptism of repentance from John, while the Pharisees, who displayed righteousness and boasted that they did the law of God, having despised the baptism of John, did not do the commandments of God. Hence he says: For John came to you in the way of righteousness, and you did not believe him, but the tax collectors and harlots believed. Moreover, as to what follows: Which of the two did the father's will? and they say: the last, it must be known that the true copies have not the last but the first, so that they are condemned by their own judgment. But if we choose to read the last, the interpretation is plain: we should say that the Jews indeed understand the truth but evade it and will not say what they think, just as they would not say that the baptism of John was from heaven, though they knew it.

Hear another parable: There was a man, a householder, who planted a vineyard and set a hedge around it and dug a winepress in it and built a tower, and he let it out to farmers and went abroad. This is what the Lord signified with a saying taken from a proverb: It is hard to kick against the goad. The chief priests and the elders of the people, who had asked the Lord: By what power do you do these things, and who gave you this power, and had wished to catch Wisdom in a word, are overcome by their own art and hear in parables what they did not deserve to hear openly. This man, the householder, is the same who had two sons, and who in another parable hired workers for his vineyard; who planted the vineyard of which Isaiah too speaks most fully in a song, bringing in at the end: The vineyard of the Lord of hosts is the house of Israel; and in the psalm: A vineyard, he says, you transplanted from Egypt; you cast out the nations and planted it. And he set a hedge around it, either the wall of the city or the help of the angels. And dug a winepress in it, either the altar or those winepresses with which three psalms are marked in their title, the eighth, the eightieth (81st) and the eighty-third (84th). And built a tower, no doubt the temple, of which it is said through Micah: And you, cloudy tower of the daughter of Zion. And he let it out to farmers, whom elsewhere he called the workers of the vineyard, who had been hired at the first, third, sixth and ninth and eleventh hour. And went abroad, not by change of place, for from where can God be absent, by whom all things are filled, and who says through Jeremiah: I am a God who draws near, and not from afar, says the Lord? But he seems to go away from the vineyard so as to leave the vinedressers free choice in their working. And when the time of fruits drew near, he sent his servants to the farmers to receive its fruits; and the farmers, seizing his servants, beat one, killed another, and stoned another. He had given them the law and had ordered them to work in this vineyard, so that they might show the fruit of the law in works. Afterward he sent servants to them, whom they seized and either beat, like Jeremiah, or killed, like Isaiah, or stoned, like Naboth and Zechariah, whom they slew between the temple and the altar. Let us read Paul's letter to the Hebrews, and from it we learn most fully which of the Lord's servants suffered how much.

But last of all he sent his son to them, saying: They will revere my son. In what we read above: Again he sent other servants, more than the first, and they did the same to them, he shows the patience of the householder, in that he sent more often, to provoke the wicked tenants to repentance; but they stored up for themselves wrath on the day of wrath. Moreover, what is joined: They will revere my son, does not come from ignorance. For what could the householder not know, who in this place is understood as God the Father? But God is always said to be in doubt, so that free will may be kept for man. Let us question Arius and Eunomius. See, the Father is said not to know, and he tempers his statement, and, as far as lies in you, he is proved to have lied. Whatever they answer on behalf of the Father, they will understand this of the Son, who says that he does not know the day of the consummation.

Seizing him, they cast him out of the vineyard and killed him. The Apostle too says that the Lord was crucified outside the gate. But we can also understand it otherwise: that he was cast out of the vineyard and killed there so that, with the Gentiles receiving him, the vineyard might be let out to others.

When therefore the lord of the vineyard comes, what will he do to those tenants, and the rest. The Lord asks them, not because he does not know what they will answer, but so that they may be condemned by their own answer. Now the vineyard has been let out to us, and let out on this condition: that we render the Lord the fruit in its seasons and know at each time what we ought either to say or to do.

Jesus says to them: Have you never read in the Scriptures: The stone which the builders rejected, this has become the head of the corner; this was done by the Lord, this is wonderful in our eyes? By various parables and different discourses the same things are woven together. For those whom above he had called workers and vinedressers and farmers, he now calls builders, that is, masons. Hence the Apostle too says: You are God's tillage, God's building. These masons, then, just as the vinedressers receive the vineyard, so received the stone, to place it either in the foundations, according to the architect Paul, or in the corner, so that it might join together two walls, that is, both peoples; and this stone, rejected by them, has become the head of the corner; and this was done by the Lord, not by human powers but by the might of God. Of this stone of help Peter too speaks confidently: This is the stone which was rejected by you builders, which has become the head of the corner; and Isaiah: Behold, he says, I will lay in the foundations of Zion a stone, chosen, precious, a cornerstone, and whoever believes in it will not be confounded.

Therefore I say to you that the kingdom of God will be taken from you and given to a nation producing its fruits. I have said several times that the kingdom of God is understood as the holy Scriptures, which the Lord took away from the Jews and handed over to us, so that we may produce their fruits. This is the vineyard that is handed over to farmers and vinedressers; those in it who have not worked, having only the name of the Scriptures, will lose the fruits of the vineyard.

Whoever falls on this stone will be broken, but on whomever it falls it will crush him. It is one thing to offend Christ by evil works, another to deny him. He who is a sinner and yet believes in him falls indeed on the stone and is broken, but is not utterly crushed; for he is kept through patience for salvation. But on whomever it falls, that is, on whom the stone itself rushes down, and who has wholly denied Christ, it will so crush him that not even a potsherd remains with which a little water might be drawn.

And when the chief priests and the Pharisees had heard his parables, they knew that he was speaking about them; and seeking to seize him, they feared the crowds, since they held him as a prophet. Although they were hard of heart and dulled by unbelief and impiety toward the Son of God, still they could not deny plain propositions, and they understood that all the Lord's sayings were directed against them. Hence they wished indeed to kill him, but they feared the crowds, because they held him as a prophet. The crowd is always fickle and does not persist in its purposed will, and, like the waves and the various winds, it is drawn this way and that. The one whom they now venerate and honor as a prophet, afterward they shout against: Crucify, crucify such a man.

Matthew 22:1-40

And answering, Jesus spoke to them in parables, saying: The kingdom of heaven has become like a man, a king, who made a wedding for his son, and sent his servant to call those invited to the wedding, and they would not come. The Pharisees, understanding that the parables were spoken about them, sought to seize him and kill him. Knowing this will of theirs, the Lord nonetheless rebukes them in their raging, and he is not so overcome by fear as to refrain from reproving sinners. This king who made a wedding for his son is God almighty. And he makes a wedding for our Lord Jesus Christ and the Church, which has been gathered as much from the Jews as from the Gentiles; and he sends his servant to call those invited to the wedding, no doubt Moses, through whom he gave the law to those invited. But if we read 'servants', as most copies have it, it is to be referred to the prophets, because those invited through them disdained to come.

Again he sent other servants, saying: Tell those invited: Behold, I have prepared my dinner, my bulls and fatlings are killed and all things are ready; come to the wedding. The servants who were sent the second time are better understood as the prophets than as the apostles, provided, however, that 'servant' was written above; but if you read 'servants' there, then here the second servants are to be understood as the apostles. By the dinner prepared and the bulls and fatlings killed, either the king's riches are described by metaphor, so that from carnal things spiritual things may be understood, or else the greatness of the doctrines and the teaching of God, most full of the law, can be perceived.

And they went off, one to his farm, another to his business; but others seized his servants and, having treated them with insult, killed them. Among those who do not receive the truth of the Gospel there is great diversity. For those who, occupied with other things, would not come are of lesser guilt than those who, scorning the affection of the one inviting, turned kindness into cruelty and seized the king's servants and either treated them with insults or killed them. In this parable the killing of the bridegroom is passed over in silence, and through the deaths of the servants the contempt for the wedding is shown.

But when the king heard, he was angry. Of him he had said above: The kingdom of heaven has become like a man, a king, when he was inviting to the wedding and doing works of clemency, the name of man was set beside him; now, when he comes to vengeance, 'man' is passed over in silence and he is called only 'king'.

And having sent his armies, he destroyed those murderers and burned their city. By the armies let us understand either the avenging angels, of whom it is written in the psalms: An infliction by most evil angels, or the Romans under the leadership of Vespasian and Titus, who, having slain the peoples of Judea, burned the transgressing city.

Then he said to his servants: The wedding indeed is ready, but those who had been invited were not worthy; go therefore to the exits of the roads, and whomever you find, call to the wedding. The people of the Gentiles was not on the roads but at the exits of the roads. But it is asked how, among these who were outside among the wicked, some good men were also found. The Apostle treats this passage more fully to the Romans: that the Gentiles, doing by nature the things that are of the law, condemn the Jews, who did not do the written law. Among the heathen themselves, too, there is infinite diversity, since we know that some are prone to vices and rush headlong into evils, while others, on account of the uprightness of their morals, are devoted to virtues.

But the king went in to see those reclining at table, and he saw there a man not clothed in a wedding garment, and he said to him: Friend, how did you come in here not having a wedding garment? But he fell silent. Those who had been invited to the wedding from the hedges and corners and streets and various places had filled the king's supper. But afterward, when the king had come to see those reclining at his banquet, that is, resting, as it were, in his faith, so that on the day of judgment he might visit the guests and discern the merits of each, he found one who was not clothed in a wedding garment. This one man is understood as all who are allied in malice. And the wedding garments are the precepts of the Lord and the works that are fulfilled from the law and the Gospel and make up the clothing of the new man. If anyone, then, at the time of judgment is found, under the Christian name, not to have the wedding garment, that is, the garment of the supercelestial man, but a polluted garment, that is, the cast-off skins of the old man, he is at once rebuked, and it is said to him: Friend, how did you come in here? He calls him friend because he was invited to the wedding; he convicts him of impudence because with a filthy garment he polluted the cleanness of the wedding. But he fell silent. For at that time there will be no place for impudence and no power of denial, since all the angels and the world itself are witness of sins.

Then the king said to the attendants: With his feet and hands bound, cast him into the outer darkness; there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth. The bound hands and feet, and the weeping of the eyes and the gnashing of teeth, understand either as proving the truth of the resurrection, or else the hands and feet are bound for this reason, that they may cease to work evil and to run to shed blood. In the weeping of the eyes, too, and the gnashing of teeth, the greatness of the torments is shown through a metaphor drawn from the members of the body.

Many, however, are called, but few chosen. In a brief little sentence he sums up all the parables: that in the work of the vineyard, and in the building of the house, and in the wedding banquet, it is not the beginning but the end that is sought.

Then the Pharisees went off and took counsel how to catch him in his speech, and they send their disciples with the Herodians, saying. Not long before, under Caesar Augustus, Judea, subjected to the Romans when the census was held throughout the whole world, had been made tributary, and there was great sedition among the people: some said that for the security and quiet for which the Romans fought on behalf of all, the tributes ought to be paid; but the Pharisees, who applauded themselves in their righteousness, strove on the contrary that the people of God, which paid tithes and gave firstfruits and the other things that are written in the law, ought not to be subject to human laws. Caesar Augustus had established Herod, the son of Antipater, a foreigner and a proselyte, as king over the Jews, to preside over the tributes and obey the Roman empire. The Pharisees therefore send their disciples with the Herodians, that is, with Herod's soldiers, or with those whom the Pharisees, in mockery, because they paid tributes to the Romans, called Herodians and not men devoted to divine worship. Some of the Latins absurdly think that the Herodians were those who believed Herod to be the Christ, something we read nowhere at all.

Teacher, we know that you are truthful and teach the way of God in truth, and you have no care for anyone; for you do not regard the person of men. Tell us therefore what you think: Is it lawful to give tax to Caesar or not? The flattering and deceitful question lures the one answering into fearing God rather than Caesar and saying that the tributes ought not to be paid, so that the Herodians, hearing it, might at once seize him as the leader of a sedition against the Romans.

But Jesus, knowing their wickedness, said: Why do you tempt me, hypocrites? The first virtue of one who answers is to know the mind of those who question him, and to call them not disciples but tempters. A hypocrite, then, is the name for one who is one thing and pretends another, that is, does one thing in deed and puts forward another with his voice.

Show me the coin of the tax. And they brought him a denarius. Wisdom always acts wisely, so that tempters are refuted above all by their own words. Show me, he says, the denarius. This is the kind of coin that was reckoned at ten coins and bore the image of Caesar.

And Jesus said to them: Whose image and superscription is this? Let those who think that the Savior's questioning is ignorance and not dispensation learn from the present passage that Jesus certainly could have known whose image was on the coin; but he asks so that he may answer their words fittingly.

They say to him: Caesar's. Then he said to them: Render therefore the things that are Caesar's to Caesar, and the things that are God's to God. Let us not think that Augustus is signified by Caesar, but Tiberius, his stepson, who had succeeded to his stepfather's place, and under whom the Lord also suffered. But all the Roman kings, from the first, Gaius Caesar, who had seized the empire, were called Caesars. Moreover, as for his saying: Render the things that are Caesar's to Caesar, that is, coin, tribute and money, and the things that are God's to God, let us understand tithes, firstfruits, oblations and victims; just as he himself both renders tributes for himself and Peter, and renders to God the things that are God's, doing the will of the Father.

And hearing it they marveled. Those who ought to have believed marveled at such great wisdom, because their cunning had found no opening for laying a snare.

And leaving him they went away, carrying back unbelief together with their wonder.

On that day the Sadducees, who say that there is no resurrection, came to him. There were two heresies among the Jews, one of the Pharisees and the other of the Sadducees. The Pharisees held up a righteousness of traditions and observances, which they call deuteroses, and hence they were also called 'divided' by the people; while the Sadducees, which is interpreted 'just', themselves also claimed for themselves what they were not. And whereas the former believed and confessed the resurrection of both body and soul, and angels and spirit, the latter, according to the Acts of the Apostles, denied it all. These are the two houses of which Isaiah teaches quite plainly that they would stumble on the stone of stumbling.

And they questioned him, saying: Teacher, Moses said: If anyone dies not having a son, his brother is to marry his wife and raise up seed for his brother. Now there were among us seven brothers, and the first, having married a wife, died, and the rest. Those who did not believe in the resurrection of bodies, and thought that the soul perished together with the bodies, aptly invent a fable of this kind, to convict of madness those who assert the resurrection of the dead. But it may be that this really happened at some time among their people.

In the resurrection, therefore, whose wife of the seven will she be? For they all had her. They set the foulness of the fable against it in order to deny the truth of the resurrection.

Jesus answered and said to them: You err, not knowing the Scriptures nor the power of God. They err for this reason, that they do not know the Scriptures; and because they are ignorant of the Scriptures, they consequently do not know the power of God, that is, Christ, who is the power of God and the wisdom of God.

For in the resurrection they will neither marry nor be married. Latin usage does not correspond to the Greek idiom. For properly it is women who are said to wed, and men to take wives. But let us understand it as said simply, so that 'marry' is written of the men and 'be married' of the wives. Thus in the resurrection they will not marry nor be married: therefore bodies will rise which are able to marry and be married. For no one says of a stone or a tree or of those things that have no genital members that they do not marry nor are married, but of those who, though they could marry, yet for another reason do not marry. But as for what is added:

But they are like the angels of God in heaven, a spiritual way of life is promised.

But concerning the resurrection of the dead, have you not read what was spoken by God, who says to you: I am the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob? He is not the God of the dead but of the living. To prove the truth of the resurrection he could have used other, much plainer examples, among which is this one: The dead shall be raised, and those who are in the tombs shall rise again, and in another place: Many of those who sleep in the dust of the earth shall arise, some to life, others to reproach and everlasting confusion. It is asked, therefore, what the Lord meant by bringing forward this testimony, which seems ambiguous or not sufficiently pertinent to the truth of the resurrection: I am the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob, and why, as though by bringing it forward he had proved what he wished, he at once added: He is not the God of the dead but of the living. The crowds standing around, too, recognizing the mystery of this matter, marveled at the teaching of his answer. We said above that the Sadducees, confessing neither angel nor spirit nor the resurrection of bodies, also preached the destruction of souls. They received only the five books of Moses, rejecting the oracles of the prophets. It would therefore have been foolish to bring forward testimonies from a source whose authority they did not follow. Moreover, to prove the eternity of souls he sets down an example from Moses: I am the God of Abraham and the God of Isaac and the God of Jacob, and at once adds: He is not the God of the dead but of the living, so that, once he had proved that souls endure after death (for it could not be that he was the God of those who did not subsist at all), the resurrection of the bodies too might consequently be introduced, the bodies which together with the souls did good or evil. The apostle Paul pursues this passage more fully in the last part of the first letter to the Corinthians.

When the Pharisees heard that he had imposed silence on the Sadducees, they gathered together; and one of them, a doctor of the law, questioned him, tempting him: Teacher, which is the great commandment in the law? Jesus said to him: You shall love the Lord your God with your whole heart, and the rest. What we read of Herod and Pontius Pilate, that they made concord over the Lord's death, we now see also of the Pharisees and the Sadducees. They are contrary to each other, but to tempt Jesus they agree with one mind. Those, then, who had already been refuted above in the showing of the denarius, and had seen the faction of the opposing party overthrown, ought to have been warned by the example not to contrive snares any further; but ill will and envy nourish impudence. One of the doctors of the law asks, not desiring to know but tempting, whether he, when asked, knew what he was being asked, which is the greater commandment: not asking about the commandments, but which is the first and great commandment, so that, since all that God has commanded is great, whatever he answered, the questioner might have an occasion for slander, asserting that another, out of the many, is great. Whoever, then, knows and asks, not from a wish to learn but from eagerness to find out whether the one who is to answer knows, approaches after the likeness of the Pharisees, not as a disciple but as a tempter.

Book Four

Matthew 22:41-46

Now when the Pharisees were gathered together, Jesus asked them, saying: What do you think about the Christ? Whose son is he? They say to him: David's. He says to them: How then does David in the Spirit call him Lord, saying: The Lord said to my Lord: Sit at my right until I make your enemies the footstool of your feet. Those who had gathered to tempt Jesus, and were striving to catch the truth with a deceitful question, provided the occasion for their own refutation, and they are asked about the Christ, whose son he is. The question of Jesus profits us against the Jews even to this day. For these too, who confess that the Christ is to come, assert that he is a mere man and a holy man of the line of David. Let us therefore, taught by the Lord, ask them: If he is a mere man and only the son of David, how does David call him his Lord, not in the uncertainty of error nor by his own will, but in the Holy Spirit? Now the testimony that he set down is taken from Psalm 109 (110). David's Lord, therefore, he is called not according to that by which he was born of him, but according to that by which, born of the Father, he always was, going before the very father of his flesh. The Jews, to make sport of the truth of the question, invent many frivolous things, asserting that it was Abraham's home-born servant, whose son was Eliezer of Damascus, and that the psalm was written in his person, because after the slaughter of the five kings the Lord God said to his lord Abraham: Sit at my right hand until I make all your enemies the footstool of your feet. Let us ask them: How did God say to Abraham the words that follow: With you is the beginning in the day of your power, in the splendors of the saints; from the womb before the morning star I begot you; and: The Lord has sworn and will not repent: You are a priest for ever according to the order of Melchizedek, and let us compel them to answer how Abraham was begotten before the morning star and was a priest according to the order of Melchizedek, for whom Melchizedek offered bread and wine, and from whom he received tithes of the spoil.

And no one could answer him a word, nor did anyone dare from that day to ask him any more. The Pharisees and Sadducees, seeking an occasion for false accusation and to find some word that would lie open to their snares, because they have been refuted in argument, ask no further, but seize him quite openly and hand him over to the Roman power. From this we understand that the poisons of envy can indeed be overcome, but with difficulty are laid to rest.

Matthew 23

Then Jesus spoke to the crowds and to his disciples, saying: The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the chair of Moses; therefore all that they say to you, observe and do, but do not do according to their works; for they say and do not do. What is gentler, what is kinder than the Lord? He is tested by the Pharisees, their snares are broken, and, according to the psalmist: The arrows of little children have become their wounds, and nonetheless, on account of the priesthood and the dignity of their name, he exhorts the peoples to be subject to them, considering not their works but their teaching. As for his saying: The scribes and Pharisees have sat upon the chair of Moses, by the chair he signifies the teaching of the law. Therefore that also which is said in the psalm: He has not sat in the chair of pestilence; and: He overturned the chairs of those who sold doves, we ought to take as teaching.

But they bind heavy and unbearable burdens and lay them on men's shoulders, but with their own finger they will not move them. This is said generally against all teachers who command great things and do not do even lesser ones. But it must be noted that the shoulders and the finger and the burdens and the bonds with which the burdens are bound are to be understood spiritually.

But they do all their works in order to be seen by men. Whoever, therefore, does anything in such a way as to be seen by men is a scribe and a Pharisee.

For they broaden their phylacteries and enlarge their fringes; they love also the first places at table at dinners and the first chairs in the synagogues and greetings in the marketplace and to be called rabbi by men. Woe to us wretches, to whom the vices of the Pharisees have passed! The Lord, when he had given the commandments of the law through Moses, added at the end: You shall bind them on your hand, and they shall be unmoved before your eyes; and the sense is: Let my precepts be in your hand, that they may be fulfilled in deed; let them be before your eyes, that you may meditate on them day and night. The Pharisees, interpreting this badly, used to write the decalogue of Moses, that is, the ten words of the law, on little strips of parchment, folding them up and binding them on the forehead and making as it were a crown for the head, so that they might always move before their eyes, as the Indians and Babylonians do to this day; and whoever has this is judged among the peoples to be, as it were, religious. Moses had also commanded another thing: that they should make violet-blue fringes on the four corners of their cloaks to distinguish the people of Israel, so that, as circumcision in their bodies gave a sign of the Jewish nation, so their clothing might have some distinction. The superstitious teachers, courting popular favor and pursuing gain from silly women, used to make large fringes and bind very sharp thorns in them, so that, namely, as they walked and sat they might now and then be pricked and, as by this reminder, be drawn back to the duties of God and the ministries of his service. Since, therefore, the Lord had said: They do all their works in order to be seen by men, what he had charged in general he now divides into parts. Those little slips of the decalogue they called phylacteries, because whoever had them would have them as it were for his own guarding and protection, the Pharisees not understanding that these things are to be carried in the heart, not on the body; otherwise bookcases and chests too have books, and do not have the knowledge of God. This, among us, superstitious little women do to this day with little Gospels and with the wood of the cross and things of this kind, women who have indeed a zeal for God, but not according to knowledge, straining out the gnat and swallowing the camel. Of this kind was the fringe, small and short, prescribed by the law, which that woman also who suffered a flow of blood touched on the Lord's cloak; but she was not pricked by the superstitious thorns of the Pharisees, but rather was healed at its touch. And since they broaden their phylacteries beyond measure and make large fringes, courting glory from men, they are rebuked in the rest for seeking the first places at table at dinners and the first chairs in the synagogues, and for pursuing gluttony and glory in public, and for being called rabbi by men, which in the Latin tongue means master. In short, there follows:

But do not you be called rabbi, for one is your master; and do not call anyone your father on earth, for one is your Father, who is in heaven; nor be called masters, because your master is one, Christ. Neither master nor father is anyone else to be called except God the Father and our Lord Jesus Christ: Father, because from him are all things; master, because through him are all things, or because through the dispensation of the flesh we have all been reconciled to God. It is asked why, against this precept, the Apostle said that he was the teacher of the Gentiles, or how, in common speech, especially in the monasteries of Palestine and of Egypt, they call one another fathers. This is solved thus: it is one thing to be a father or a master by nature, another by indulgence. If we call a man father, we pay honor to his age; we do not show him to be the author of our life. A master too is so called from fellowship with the true Master. And, not to unfold endless matters: just as one God by nature and one Son does not prejudice others from being called gods and sons by adoption, so also one Father and Master does not prejudice others from being called fathers and masters in a looser sense.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you shut the kingdom of heaven before men; you yourselves do not enter, nor do you allow those entering to enter. The scribes and Pharisees have knowledge of the law and the prophets; they know that Christ is the Son of God; they are not ignorant that he was born of a virgin; but while they seek plunder from the common people subject to them, they neither enter the kingdoms of heaven themselves nor permit those who could enter. This is what he rebukes in the prophet Hosea: The priests have hidden the way, they have killed Shechem; and again: The priests did not say: Where is the Lord? Or else every teacher who causes his disciples to stumble by evil works shuts the kingdom of heaven before them.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you go around sea and dry land to make one proselyte, and when he has been made, you make him a son of Gehenna twice as much as yourselves. We do not keep what we have sought with the same zeal with which we seek it. The scribes and Pharisees, traversing the whole world for the sake of trade and various gains, to be captured as much from their disciples as through a show of holiness, made it their zeal to make a proselyte from the Gentiles, that is, to mingle a stranger, an uncircumcised man, with the people of God. But he who before, while he was a heathen, went astray in simplicity and was a son of Gehenna once, seeing the vices of his teachers and understanding that they destroy by deed what they taught by words, returns to his vomit and, having become a Gentile, will be worthy of greater punishment as a transgressor. He is called a son of Gehenna in the way one is called a son of perdition and a son of this world. For each one is called the son of him whose works he does.

Woe to you, blind guides, who say: Whoever swears by the temple, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gold of the temple is bound. Fools and blind, for which is greater: the gold, or the temple that sanctifies the gold? And: Whoever swears by the altar, it is nothing; but whoever swears by the gift that is upon it is bound. Blind ones, for which is greater: the gift, or the altar that sanctifies the gift? He, therefore, who swears by the altar swears by it and by all things that are upon it; and he who swears by the temple swears by it and by him who dwells in it; and he who swears by heaven swears by the throne of God and by him who sits upon it. Above, as seemed good to us, we explained what was signified by the tradition of the Pharisees who said: Whatever gift is from me will profit you. Now a tradition of the Pharisees is condemned that is double and draws toward the one occasion of avarice, so that they are convicted of doing all for gain and not for the fear of God. For as, in the broadened phylacteries and fringes, the reputation for holiness courted glory, and through the occasion of glory sought gain, so another scheme devised by their tradition convicts the teachers of impiety. If anyone, in a dispute or in some quarrel or in a doubtful case, had sworn by the temple and was afterwards convicted of a lie, he was not held guilty of the crime; but if he had sworn by the gold and the money that was offered to the priests in the temple, he was at once compelled to pay that by which he had sworn. Again, if anyone had sworn by the altar, no one held him guilty of perjury; but if he had perjured himself by the gift or by the offerings, that is, by the sacrifice or by the victims, by fine flour and the other things that are offered to God upon the altar, these they demanded back most zealously. The Lord therefore convicts them both of folly and of fraud, because the temple is much greater than the gold which is sanctified by the temple, and the altar than the sacrifices which are sanctified by the altar. And they did all this not out of the fear of God but out of desire for riches.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you tithe mint and dill and cumin and have left aside the weightier things of the law, judgment and mercy and faith; these you ought to have done, and not to omit those. There are many precepts in the law which display types of things to come, but others which are plain, according to the psalmist who says: The commandment of the Lord is bright, enlightening the eyes, which demand works at once, for example: You shall not commit adultery, you shall not steal, you shall not bear false witness, and the rest. But the Pharisees, because the Lord had commanded (to leave aside the mystical senses for the moment) that, for the support of the priests and Levites, whose portion was the Lord, tithes of all things be offered in the temple, had this one zeal: that what had been ordered should be brought in; the other things, which were greater, whether anyone did them or not, they counted of little worth. And so from this passage too he convicts them of avarice, because they zealously exact tithes even of cheap herbs, and pass over judgment in the settling of business, and mercy toward the poor, orphans and widows, and faith in God, which are great things.

Blind guides, straining out the gnat, but swallowing the camel. By the camel I think is meant, according to the sense of the present passage, the greatness of the precepts, judgment and mercy and faith; and by the gnat the tithes of mint and dill and cumin and of the other cheap herbs. These things, which are great, we devour and neglect against the precept of God, and in small things that bring gain we make a show of religion and of diligence.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you clean what is outside of the cup and the dish, but inside you are full of plunder and uncleanness. Blind Pharisee, first clean what is inside of the cup and the dish, so that what is outside may become clean. In different words, with the same sense as above, he convicts the Pharisees of pretense and falsehood, because they show one thing to men outside and do another at home; not that their superstition dwelt in the cup and the dish, but that outside they showed men holiness in dress, in speech, in phylacteries, in fringes, in the length of their prayers and in other things of this kind, while inwardly they were full of the filth of vices.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you are like whitewashed tombs, which outside appear beautiful to men, but inside are full of the bones of the dead and of all filthiness. What he had shown in the cup and the dish, in that they were washed outside and filthy within, he now repeats through the example of tombs: that just as tombs are washed outwardly with lime and adorned with marbles and picked out with gold and colors, but within are full of the bones of the dead, so too the perverse teachers, who teach one thing and do another, display cleanness in the fashion of their dress and in humility of words, but inside are full of all filthiness and avarice and lust. In short, he expresses this very thing more plainly, adding:

So you too outwardly indeed appear just to men, but inside you are full of hypocrisy and iniquity.

Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites, because you build the tombs of the prophets and adorn the monuments of the just, and say: If we had been in the days of our fathers, we would not have been their partners in the blood of the prophets. Thus you are witnesses against yourselves that you are sons of those who killed the prophets. With a most prudent syllogism he convicts them of being sons of murderers, since they themselves, for a reputation of goodness and glory among the peoples, build the tombs of the prophets whom their forefathers killed, and say: If we had been at that time, we would not have done the things that our fathers did. And this, even if they do not say it in word, they speak in deed, in that they build, ambitiously and magnificently, memorials of the slain, who, they do not deny, were slaughtered by their fathers.

And you, fill up the measure of your fathers. Having proved by what was said above that they were sons of murderers and of those who had killed the prophets, he now concludes what he had intended and sets down, as it were, the last part of the syllogism: And you, fill up the measure of your fathers, what was lacking in them, you fill up; they killed the servants, you, crucify the Lord; they the prophets, you him who was preached by the prophets.

Serpents, brood of vipers, how will you flee from the judgment of Gehenna? John the Baptist too had said this very thing. Just as, then, vipers are born of vipers, so you, he says, born of murderous fathers, are murderers.

Therefore, behold, I send to you prophets and wise men and scribes; some of them you will kill and crucify, and some of them you will scourge in your synagogues, and you persecute them from city to city. This which we said earlier, Fill up the measure of your fathers, to pertain to the person of the Lord, since he was to be killed by them, can also be referred to his disciples, of whom he now says: Behold, I send to you prophets and wise men and scribes; some of them you will kill and crucify, and you will scourge in your synagogues, and you persecute them from city to city, so that you may fill up the measure of your fathers. At the same time note, according to the Apostle writing to the Corinthians, that the gifts of the disciples of Christ are various: some are prophets, who foretell what is to come; others wise men, who know when they ought to bring forth speech; others scribes, most learned in the law; of whom Stephen was stoned, Paul killed, Peter crucified, the disciples scourged in the Acts of the Apostles; and they persecuted them from city to city, driving them out of Judea, so that they passed over to the people of the Gentiles.

That upon you may come all the just blood that has been shed upon the earth, from the blood of Abel the just to the blood of Zechariah son of Barachiah, who was killed between the temple and the altar. Amen I say to you, all these things will come upon this generation. Concerning Abel there is no ambiguity that he is the one whom Cain his brother killed. That he was just is proved not only from the Lord's present judgment but from the testimony of Genesis, where his gifts are related as accepted by God. We ask who this Zechariah son of Barachiah is, since we read of many Zechariahs. And lest a free license for error be granted us, there is added: Whom you killed between the temple and the altar. I have read different things in different writers, and I ought to set down the opinions of each. Some say it is Zechariah son of Barachiah, who is the eleventh among the twelve prophets, and the name of the father agrees in his case; but where he was killed between the temple and the altar Scripture does not say, especially since in his times there were scarcely even ruins of the temple. Others want it understood as Zechariah the father of John, proving from certain dreams of the apocrypha that he was killed because he had preached the coming of the Savior. This, because it has no authority from the Scriptures, is despised with the same ease with which it is proved. Others will have this to be the Zechariah who was killed by Joash king of Judea between the temple and the altar, as the history of Kings relates. But it must be observed that that Zechariah is not the son of Barachiah but the son of Jehoiada the priest. Hence Scripture also relates: Joash did not remember the good things that Jehoiada his father had done for him. Since, then, we have both the Zechariah and a place of killing that agrees, we ask why he is called the son of Barachiah and not of Jehoiada. Barachiah in our language means blessed of the Lord, and the justice of the priest Jehoiada is shown by his Hebrew name. In the Gospel which the Nazarenes use we find written son of Jehoiada in place of son of Barachiah. The simpler brethren, among the ruins of the temple and of the altar, or at the exits of the gates that lead to Siloam, point out red stones and think they were stained with the blood of Zechariah. We do not condemn the error, which comes down from hatred of the Jews and from the piety of faith. Let us say briefly why the blood of Abel the just, up to Zechariah son of Barachiah, is required of that generation, though it killed neither of them. It is a rule of the Scriptures to set forth two generations, of the good or of the evil, that is, one for each. Let us take examples from the good: Who shall ascend into the mountain of the Lord, or who shall rest on his holy mountain? and when he had described the many who are to ascend into the mountain of the Lord, who lived in different ages, he afterwards adds: This is the generation of those who seek the Lord, who seek the face of the God of Jacob; and in another place, of all the saints: The generation of the just shall be blessed; but of the evil, as in the present passage: generation of vipers and all things shall be required of this generation; and in Ezekiel, when he had described the sins of the land, the prophetic word added: If Noah and Job and Daniel are found there, the sins of that land will not be forgiven, wishing all the just who would be like them in virtues to be understood through Noah and Job and Daniel. Therefore these too, who did things against the apostles like those of Cain and Joash, are reported to be of one generation.

Jerusalem, Jerusalem, you who kill the prophets and stone those who are sent to you, how often I wished to gather your children as a hen gathers her chicks under her wings, and you would not. Jerusalem (he means not the stones and buildings of the city but its inhabitants), which he laments with a father's affection, as we also read in another place that, seeing it, he wept. And in saying, how often I wished to gather your children, he testifies that all the prophets of the past were sent by him. The likeness of the hen gathering her chicks under her wings we read also in the song of Deuteronomy: As an eagle protects its nest and has longed over its young, spreading its wings it took them up and bore them on its pinions.

Behold, your house will be left to you deserted. He had said this very thing long before, in the person of Jeremiah: I have left my house, I have abandoned my inheritance; my inheritance has become to me like the cave of a hyena. We prove that the house of the Jews, that is, that temple which before shone more majestically to the eyes, is deserted, because it lost Christ its inhabitant and, eager to snatch the inheritance, killed the heir.

For I say to you: You will not see me henceforth until you say: Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord. He speaks to Jerusalem and to the people of the Jews. Now this verse, which the little ones too and the sucklings used at the entry of the Lord Savior into Jerusalem, when they said: Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord, Hosanna in the highest; he took from Psalm 117 (118), which was plainly written about the coming of the Lord; and by what he says he wants this understood: Unless you repent and confess that I myself am he of whom the prophets sang, the Son of the almighty Father, you will not see my face. The Jews have a time of repentance given them; let them confess him blessed who comes in the name of the Lord, and they will behold the face of Christ.

Matthew 24

And Jesus went out from the temple and was going away. And his disciples came to him to show him the buildings of the temple. But he answered and said to them: Do you see all these things? Amen I say to you: There shall not be left here a stone upon a stone that shall not be destroyed. According to the history the sense is plain. But as the Lord withdrew from the temple, all the buildings of the law and the structure of the commandments were so destroyed that nothing can be fulfilled by the Jews, and, the head having been taken away, all the members fight against one another.

But as he sat upon the Mount of Olives, the disciples came to him privately, saying: Tell us when these things will be, and what is the sign of your coming and of the consummation of the age? He sat on the Mount of Olives, where the true light of knowledge was being born; and the disciples, who longed to know the mysteries and the revelation of things to come, come to him privately and ask three things: at what time Jerusalem is to be destroyed, at what time Christ will come, at what time the consummation of the age will be.

For many will come in my name, saying: I am the Christ, and they will lead many astray. One of these is Simon the Samaritan, of whom we read in the Acts of the Apostles, who said that he was the great power of God, and who left behind, among other things written in his books, these words too: I am the word of God, I am the beautiful one, I the Paraclete, I the almighty, I all things of God. But John the apostle too says in his letter: You have heard that antichrist is to come, but now there are many antichrists. I think that all heresiarchs are antichrists and teach, under the name of Christ, the things that are contrary to Christ. Nor is it any wonder if we see some led astray by them, since the Lord said: and they will lead many astray.

For you are going to hear of battles and rumors of battles; see that you are not troubled; for these things must happen, but the end is not yet. So when we see these things happen, let us not think that the day of judgment is coming in, but that it is reserved for that time whose sign is clearly set out in what follows.

For nation will rise against nation and kingdom against kingdom, and there will be pestilences and famines and earthquakes in various places; all these are the beginnings of sorrows. I do not doubt that these things too which are written will come to pass according to the letter; but it seems to me that kingdom against kingdom, and the pestilences of those whose speech creeps like a cancer, and the famine of hearing the word of God, and the shaking of the whole earth and the separation from the true faith, are rather to be understood of the heretics, who, fighting against one another, bring about a victory for the Church. But as for what he said: But these are the beginnings of sorrows, it is better translated of birth pangs, so that the coming of antichrist may be understood as a kind of conception, as it were, not the birth.

Then they will deliver you up to tribulation and will kill you. Through the apostles the person of all believers is signified, not that the apostles are signified as going to be found in the body at that time.

And because iniquity has abounded, the love of many will grow cold. It is not of all that he denied the faith, but of many. For many are called, but few chosen. For in the apostles and those like them the love will endure of which it is written: Much water will not be able to quench love; and Paul himself: Who will separate us from the love of Christ? tribulation or distress and the rest.

And this Gospel of the kingdom will be preached in the whole world as a testimony to all nations, and then the consummation will come. The sign of the Lord's coming is that the Gospel be preached in the whole world, so that no one may be excusable; and this we see either already fulfilled or soon to be fulfilled. For I do not think that any nation remains which does not know the name of Christ; and although it may not have had a preacher, yet from the neighboring nations it cannot be ignorant of the report of the faith.

So when you see the abomination of desolation which was spoken of by Daniel the prophet standing in the holy place; let him who reads understand. When we are summoned to understanding, what was said is shown to be mystical. And we read in Daniel in this way: And in the middle of the week my sacrifice and libations will be taken away, and in the temple there will be the abomination of desolations until the consummation of time, and consummation will be given upon the desolation. Of this the Apostle also speaks, saying that the man of iniquity and the adversary is to be lifted up against everything that is called God or that is worshiped, so that he dares to stand in the temple of God and to show that he himself is God; whose coming, according to the working of Satan, is to destroy those who have received him and reduce them to a desolation of God. But it can also be taken simply, either of antichrist, or of the image of Caesar which Pilate set up in the temple, or of the equestrian statue of Hadrian which has stood in the very place of the holy of holies up to the present day. Also, according to the old Scripture, an idol is called an abomination, and for that reason of desolation is added, because the idol was set up in the desolated and destroyed temple.

Then let those who are in Judea flee to the mountains, and let him who is on the roof not come down to take anything from his house, and let him who is in the field not turn back to take his tunic. The abomination of desolation can also be understood as every perverse doctrine; and when we see it standing in the holy place, that is, in the Church, and showing itself as God, we must flee from Judea to the mountains, that is, leaving behind the letter that kills and Jewish perversity, draw near to the eternal mountains from which God gives light wonderfully; and be on the roof and on the housetop, where the fiery darts of the devil cannot reach; and not come down and take anything from the house of our former way of life, nor seek the things behind, but rather sow in the field of the spiritual Scriptures so that we may gather fruit from it; nor take a second tunic, which the apostles are forbidden to have. On this passage, that is, on the abomination of desolation which was spoken of by Daniel the prophet standing in the holy place, Porphyry uttered many blasphemies against us in the thirteenth volume of his work; Eusebius, bishop of Caesarea, answered him in three volumes, the eighteenth, the nineteenth and the twentieth; Apollinaris too wrote most fully; and it was a superfluous attempt to want to discuss in one passage a matter that has been disputed in so many thousands of lines.

But woe to those who are pregnant and nursing in those days. Woe to those souls which have not brought their offspring to the perfect man, but have only the beginnings of faith, so that they need the nurture of teachers. This too can be said: that in the persecution of antichrist, or of the Roman captivity, those who were pregnant and nursing, weighed down by the burden of womb and of children, could not have an unencumbered flight.

But pray that your flight may not be in winter or on the Sabbath. If we choose to take this of the captivity of Jerusalem, when it was taken by Titus and Vespasian, they ought to pray that their flight may not be in winter or on the Sabbath, because in the one the harshness of the cold prevents them from going into the wildernesses and hiding in the mountains and deserts, and in the other there is either transgression of the law, if they choose to flee, or imminent death, if they remain. But if it is understood of the consummation of the world, he commands this: that our faith and our love toward Christ may not grow cold, and that we may not, idle in the work of God, grow sluggish in a Sabbath of the virtues.

And unless those days had been shortened, no flesh would be saved; but for the sake of the elect those days will be shortened. We ought to understand the shortened days not according to the ravings of some who think that the measures of the times are changed, and who do not remember that writing: By your ordinance the day continues, but according to the quality of the times; that is, shortened not in measure but in number, so that, just as it is said in the blessing: With length of days I will fill him, so here too the days may be understood as shortened, lest the faith of believers be shaken by the delay of the times.

Then if anyone says to you: Behold, here is the Christ, or there, do not believe it. At the time of the Jewish captivity many leaders arose who said that they were Christs, so much so that, while the Romans were besieging the city, there were three factions within. But it is better understood of the consummation of the world.

For false Christs and false prophets will arise and will give great signs and wonders, so that even the elect, if it can be, are led into error. Behold, I have told you beforehand. This passage, as I have already said before, is to be discussed in a threefold way: either of the time of the Roman siege, or of the consummation of the world, or of the fight of the heretics against the Church and of antichrists of this kind, who fight against Christ under the pretense of a false knowledge.

If therefore they say to you: Behold, he is in the desert, do not go out. Behold, in the inner rooms, do not believe it. If anyone promises you that Christ dwells in the desert of the doctrine of the Gentiles and the philosophers, or in the inner rooms of the heretics, who promise the secrets of God, do not go out, do not believe; or else: because in a time of persecution and distress false prophets always find room for deceiving, if anyone wishes to vaunt himself under the name of Christ, do not at once give him credence.

For as the lightning goes out from the east and appears even to the west, so also will be the coming of the Son of Man. Do not go out, do not believe that the Son of Man is either in the desert of the Gentiles or in the inner rooms of the heretics, but believe that from the east even to the west his faith shines in the catholic churches. This too must be said: that the second coming of the Savior is to be shown not in humility as before, but in glory. It is foolish, therefore, to seek in a small or hidden place him who is the light of the whole world.

Wherever the body is, there the eagles will be gathered together. By a natural example which we see daily we are instructed in the mystery of Christ. Eagles and vultures are said to perceive corpses even across the seas and to gather to food of this kind. If, then, irrational birds, by a natural sense, though separated by such great spaces of land and by the waves of the sea, perceive where a small corpse lies, how much more ought we and the whole multitude of believers to hasten to him whose lightning goes out from the east and appears even to the west! But by the body, that is, πτῶμα, which is more expressively called in Latin a cadaver, from the fact that it falls through death, we can understand the passion of Christ, to which we are summoned, so that wherever it is read in the Scriptures we may gather together; and through it we can come to the word of God, as in that saying: They have dug my hands and feet; and in Isaiah: As a sheep led to the slaughter, and other things like these. And the saints are called eagles, whose youth is renewed like the eagle's, and who, according to Isaiah, grow feathers and take on wings so as to come to the passion of Christ.

But immediately after the tribulation of those days the sun will be darkened, and the moon will not give its light, and the stars of heaven will fall from heaven, and the powers of the heavens will be shaken. The sun and moon will be darkened and will not give their light, and the other stars will fall from heaven, and the powers of the heavens will be moved, not by a diminution of light, for we read, after all, that the sun will have sevenfold light, but because in comparison with the true light all things are dark to the sight. If, then, this sun which now glows red over the whole world, and the moon which is the second luminary, and the stars which are kindled for the solace of the night, and all the powers, which we understand as the multitudes of angels, will be reckoned as darkness at the coming of Christ, let the haughty brow of those be struck down who, thinking themselves holy, do not dread the presence of the judge.

And then the sign of the Son of Man will appear in heaven. Let us understand the sign here either as that of the cross, so that, according to Zechariah and John, the Jews may see him whom they had pierced, or as the standard of a triumphant victory.

Then all the tribes of the earth will mourn. Those will mourn who have had no citizenship in heaven but are written on earth.

And he will send his angels with a trumpet. Of this trumpet the Apostle also speaks, and we read of it in the Apocalypse of John; and in the Old Testament trumpets of beaten work are commanded to be made of gold, bronze and silver, so that the sublime mysteries of the teachings may resound.

Learn a parable from the fig tree. When its branch has already become tender and the leaves have come out, you know that summer is near, and the rest. Under the example of the tree he taught the coming of the consummation. Just as, he says, when the shoots on the fig tree have become tender, and the bud breaks into flower, and the bark brings forth leaves, you understand the coming of summer and the arrival of the west wind and of spring; so when you see all these things that are written, do not think that the consummation of the world is already present, but that certain preludes and forerunners, as it were, are coming to show that it is near and at the doors.

Amen I say to you, that this generation will not pass away until all these things happen. We said above that the good and, on the contrary, the wicked each form a single generation. Therefore it signifies either the whole race of men or specifically that of the Jews.

Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away. Heaven and earth will pass away by a change, not by an abolition of themselves; otherwise how will the sun be darkened and the moon not give its light and the stars fall, if the heaven in which these things are, and the earth, will not exist?

But of that day and hour no one knows, not even the angels of heaven, but the Father alone. In some Latin codices there is added: nor the Son, whereas in the Greek copies, and especially those of Adamantius and Pierius, this is not found written; but because it is read in some, it seems that it should be discussed. Arius and Eunomius rejoice, as though the ignorance of the master were the glory of the disciples, and they say: He who knows and he who is ignorant cannot be equal. Against them these things must briefly be said: Since Jesus, that is, the Word of God, made all times, for all things indeed were made through him, and without him nothing was made; and since among all the times there is also the day of judgment, by what logic can he be ignorant of a part of that whose whole he knows? This too must be said: Which is greater, the knowledge of the Father or that of the judgment? If he knows the greater, how is he ignorant of what is less? We read it written: All things that are the Father's have been delivered to me. If all things of the Father are the Son's, for what reason did he reserve to himself the knowledge of one day and refuse to share it with the Son? But this too must be inferred: If he is ignorant of the last day of the times, he is ignorant also of the next to last and of all those before it. For it cannot be that he who is ignorant of the first knows what the second is. Therefore, since we have proved that the Son is not ignorant of the day of the consummation, a reason must be given why he is said to be ignorant of it. The Apostle writes concerning the Savior: In whom are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge. So all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge are in Christ, but they are hidden. Why hidden? After the resurrection, when asked by the apostles about the day, he answered more plainly: It is not yours to know the times and the moments which the Father has set in his own power. When he says: It is not yours to know, he shows that he himself knows, but that it is not expedient for the apostles to know, so that, always uncertain about the coming of the judge, they may live each day as though they were to be judged on the next. Finally, the words of the Gospel that follow also compel the same understanding, saying too that the Father alone knows. In the Father he includes the Son as well; for every father is the name of a son.

But as in the days of Noah, so also will be the coming of the Son of Man. For as in the days before the flood they were eating and drinking, marrying and giving in marriage, and the rest. It is asked how it is written above: For nation will rise against nation and kingdom against kingdom, and there will be pestilences and famines and earthquakes, and now those things are mentioned as about to come which are signs of peace.
