But it must be reckoned, following the Apostle, that after the battles and dissensions, the pestilences, famines and earthquakes, and the other things by which the human race is laid waste, a brief peace will follow, which will promise that all things are quiet, so that the faith of believers may be tested, whether, when the evils have passed, they still hope that the judge is to come. For this is what we read in Paul: When they say: Peace and security, then sudden destruction will come upon them, like the pain of a woman in labor, and they will not escape.

Then two will be in the field; one will be taken and one will be left; two women grinding at the mill; one will be taken and the other will be left. Then, he says, two will be in the field, namely when, at the time of the consummation and judgment, two will be found together in the field, having the same labor and, as it were, an equal sowing, but not receiving the fruit of their labor equally; two women also will be grinding together; one will be taken and the other left. In the two who are staying in the field and in the two women who grind together, understand either the synagogue and the Church, because they seem to grind together in the law and to grind the flour of God's commandments from the same Scriptures; or the other heresies, which seem to grind the flour of their doctrines from both Testaments or from one of them, and which, though they have one profession of the Christian name, will not receive the same reward, some being taken and others left behind.

Watch therefore, because you do not know at what hour your Lord is to come. He shows clearly why he said above: But of that day no one knows, neither the Son of Man nor the angels, but the Father alone, namely because it is not expedient for the apostles to know, so that, in the uncertainty of a suspended expectation, they may always believe that he is about to come of whom they do not know when he will come. And he did not say: because we do not know at what hour the Lord is to come, but you do not know, and having set forth the example of the householder, he teaches more plainly why he keeps silent about the day of the consummation, saying:

Be ready, because you do not know at what hour the Son of Man is to come. Who, do you think, is the faithful and prudent servant whom his master has set over his household, to give them food in due season? Blessed is that servant whom his master, when he comes, finds so doing. He presses home more fully and repeats why he foretold that neither the angels nor he himself knows the day and hour of the consummation, but only the Father: because it is not expedient for the apostles to know; and he inserts the example of the householder, that is, of himself, and of the faithful servants, that is, of the apostles, as an exhortation to a watchful mind, so that in hope of rewards they may minister to their fellow servants in due season the food of the teachings.

But if that wicked servant says in his heart: My master is delaying his coming, and begins to strike his fellow servants, and the rest. It depends on what precedes: that just as the watchful servant, always awaiting his master's coming, hands out food to his fellow servants in due season and afterward is set over all the goods of the householder, so, on the contrary, he who, in Ezekiel's words, says: This will come to pass in distant times, and does not think that the master will come soon, having grown more careless, gives himself to feasts and luxury, and will find not a gentle householder but a most severe judge.

The master of that servant will come on a day when he does not expect and at an hour he does not know, and will divide him and put his portion with the hypocrites. He teaches this very thing: that they should know that the Lord will come at the time when he is not expected; and he admonishes the stewards to vigilance and watchfulness. Moreover, as for his saying: will divide him, it does not mean that he will cut him in two with a sword, but that he will separate him from the fellowship of the saints and put his portion with the hypocrites, namely with those who were in the field and who were grinding and were nonetheless left behind. We have often said that a hypocrite is one who is one thing and shows another, just as both in the field and at the mill he seemed to do the same as the man of the Church, but the outcome revealed a different will.

Matthew 25

Then the kingdom of heaven will be like ten virgins who, taking their lamps, went out to meet the bridegroom and the bride; but five of them were foolish and five wise, and the rest. This parable, that is, likeness, of the ten foolish and wise virgins some interpret simply of virgins, of whom some, according to the Apostle, are virgins both in body and in mind, while others, preserving only the virginity of their bodies, either have other works not matching their profession, or, kept safe by the guardianship of their parents, have nonetheless married in mind. But to me it seems, from what precedes, that what is said has another sense, and that the comparison pertains not to virginal bodies but to the whole race of men. For just as the two in the field and the two women grinding signify two peoples, of Christians and of Jews, or of saints and of sinners, who, though placed in the Church, seem indeed themselves to plow and to grind but do everything in hypocrisy, so now too the ten virgins embrace all men who seem to believe in God and pride themselves on the holy Scriptures, men of the Church as well as Jews and heretics. They are all called virgins for this reason, that they glory in the knowledge of the one God and their mind is not defiled by the throng of idolatry. Those virgins have oil who, besides their faith, are adorned with works too; those do not have oil who seem indeed to confess the Lord with a like faith but neglect the works of the virtues. We can interpret the five wise and the five foolish virgins as the five senses, of which some hasten toward heavenly things and long for the things above, while others, gaping after the dregs of the earth, do not have the fuel of truth with which to light up their hearts. Of sight and hearing and touch it is said spiritually: What we have seen, what we have heard, what we have looked upon with our eyes and our hands have handled; of taste: Taste and see that the Lord is sweet; of smell: We run in the fragrance of your ointments; and: We are the good fragrance of Christ.

But while the bridegroom delayed, they all grew drowsy and slept. For no little time passes between the first and the second coming of the Lord. They all grew drowsy, that is, they died, because the death of the saints is called sleep. But it then says they slept, because they are afterward to be raised.

But at midnight a cry arose: Behold, the bridegroom comes; go out to meet him. For suddenly, as in the dead of night, when all are unconcerned and sleep is at its heaviest, the coming of Christ will resound through the cry of the angels and the trumpets of the powers that go before him. Let us say something that may perhaps be useful to the reader. It is a tradition of the Jews that Christ will come at midnight, after the likeness of the time in Egypt, when the Passover was celebrated and the destroyer came and the Lord passed over the tents and the doorposts of our foreheads were consecrated with the blood of the lamb. Hence I think the apostolic tradition too has persisted, that on the day of the vigil of the Passover it is not permitted to dismiss the people, who are awaiting the coming of Christ, before the middle of the night; and that after that time has passed, confidence being presumed, all keep the day as a feast. Hence the psalmist too said: At midnight I rose to give thanks to you over the judgments of your justice.

Then all those virgins arose and trimmed their lamps. All the virgins arose and each trimmed her lamps, that is, the senses, in which they received the oil of knowledge, so as to nourish the works of the virtues that would shine out before the true judge.

But the foolish said to the wise: Give us some of your oil, because our lamps are going out. Those who complain that their lamps are going out show that they are partly shining, and yet they do not have an unfailing light nor lasting works. If anyone, therefore, has a virginal soul and is a lover of chastity, he must not be content with middling virtues, which quickly fade and wither when the heat comes up, but let him pursue perfect virtues, so that he may have everlasting light.

The prudent answered and said: Lest perhaps there not be enough for us and for you. They answer this not out of avarice but out of fear. For each will receive the wage for his own works, nor on the day of judgment can the virtues of some relieve the vices of others. And just as in the time of the Babylonian captivity Jeremiah could not help the sinners, and it is said to him: Do not pray for this people, so fearful will that day be, when each will be anxious for himself.

Go rather to those who sell and buy for yourselves. This oil is sold, and bought at a great price, and acquired by hard labor; we understand it of almsgiving and all the virtues and the counsels of teachers.

But while they were going to buy, the bridegroom came. They give advice indeed, as prudent ones, that they ought not to go to meet the bridegroom without oil for their lamps. But because the time for buying had already gone by, and with the day of judgment arriving there was no place for repentance, as the psalmist says: But in hell who will give thanks to you, they are compelled not to accomplish new works but to render an account of past ones.

The bridegroom came, and those who were ready went in with him to the wedding, and the door was shut. After the day of judgment no opportunity for good works and justice will be left.

Last of all the other virgins also come, saying: Lord, Lord, open to us. Excellent indeed is the confession in calling him Lord, and its repetition is a sign of faith. But what does it profit to call with the voice on him whom you deny by your works?

But he answered and said: Amen I say to you: I do not know you. The Lord knows those who are his, and he who does not know will not be known. The Lord does not know the workers of iniquity; and although they are virgins and, according to the twofold understanding, glory both in purity of body and in the confession of the true faith, yet, because they do not have the oil of knowledge, it is enough for their punishment that they are not known by the bridegroom.

Watch therefore, because you know neither the day nor the hour. I always warn the prudent reader not to acquiesce in superstitious interpretations and in things said commatically, phrase by phrase, at the whim of those who invent them, but to consider what comes before, what lies in the middle and what follows, and to bind together for himself all that is written. And so from this which he adds: Watch, because you know neither the day nor the hour, it is understood that all that he said, that is, about the two who are in the field, and the two women grinding, and the householder who entrusted his substance to his servant, and the ten virgins, these parables were set forth beforehand so that, because we men do not know the day of judgment, we may carefully prepare for ourselves the light of good works, lest the judge come while we do not know it.

For just as a man setting out on a journey called his servants and handed over his goods to them, and to one he gave five talents, to another two, and to another one. This man, the householder, is beyond doubt Christ, who, ascending as victor to the Father after the resurrection, called the apostles and handed over to them the teaching of the Gospel, giving more to one and less to another, not out of lavishness or stinginess but according to the strength of those who received it, just as the Apostle too says that he gave milk to drink to those who could not take solid food. In short, he receives with like joy both the one who had made ten out of five talents and the one who had made four out of two, considering not the size of the profit but the will behind the effort. In the five and the two and the one talent let us understand either the diverse graces that have been handed over to each, or else in the first all the senses tested, in the second understanding and works, in the third reason, by which we men are set apart from the beasts.

Then he who had received five talents went away and traded with them; he gained five more. Having received earthly senses, he doubled for himself the knowledge of heavenly things, understanding the Creator from creatures, the incorporeal from the corporeal, the invisible from the visible, the eternal from the brief.

He who had received two; he gained two more. This one too, according to his strength, doubled in the Gospel whatever he had learned in the law; or else he understood both the knowledge and the works of the present life as types of the future blessedness.

But he who had received one went off, dug in the earth and hid his lord's money. The wicked servant, by earthly works and the pleasure of the world, neglected and defiled the commandments of God; although in another evangelist it is written that he tied it up in a napkin, that is, that by living softly and delicately he enfeebled the teaching of the householder.

But after a long time the lord of those servants came. A great time lies between the ascension of the Savior and his second coming. But if the apostles are to render an account and to rise again under fear of the judge, what ought we to do?

His lord said to him: Well done, good and faithful servant; because you were faithful over a few things, I will set you over many; enter into the joy of your lord. To both servants, as I have already said before, both the one who had made ten out of five talents and the one who had made four out of two, the same word of the householder speaks kindly. And it is to be noted that all the things we have in the present, though they seem great and very many, are yet small and few in comparison with the things to come. Enter, he says, into the joy of your lord, and receive what neither eye has seen nor ear heard, nor has it ascended into the heart of man. But what greater thing can be given to a faithful servant than to be with his lord and to see the joy of his lord?

And he too who had received one talent came forward and said: Lord, I know that you are a hard man; you reap where you did not sow and gather where you did not scatter; and being afraid I went off and hid your talent in the earth; here you have what is yours. Truly, what is written: To make excuses in sins, befell this servant also, so that to sloth and negligence the charge of pride was added as well. For he who ought simply to have confessed his idleness and entreated the householder, on the contrary slanders him and says that he acted on prudent counsel, lest while he sought profit on the money he should put even the principal at risk.

But his lord answered and said to him: Wicked and lazy servant, you knew that I reap where I do not sow and gather where I did not scatter; you ought therefore to have put my money with the money-changers, and on coming I would surely have received what is mine with interest; take therefore the talent from him and give it to him who has ten talents. What he had thought he said by way of excuse is turned into his own guilt. He is called a wicked servant because he slanders his lord, lazy because he would not double the talent, so that in the one he is condemned for pride, in the other for negligence. If, he says, you knew that I am hard and cruel, and that I go after what belongs to others and reap there where I have not sown, why did not a thought of this kind strike fear into you, so that you would know that I would seek out my own more diligently, and would give my money, or silver, to the money-changers? For the Greek word ἀργύριον signifies both. The words, he says, of the Lord are pure words, silver tried by fire, proved, purged of earth sevenfold. The money, then, and the silver is the preaching of the Gospel and the divine word, which ought to have been given to the money-changers and bankers, that is, either to the other teachers, as the apostles also did, ordaining presbyters and bishops in the several provinces, or to all believers who can double the money and return it with interest, so that whatever they had learned by word they might fulfill in deed. Now the talent is taken away and given to him who had made ten talents, so that we may understand that, although the Lord's joy over the labor of both is equal, that is, both of him who had doubled five into ten and of him who had doubled two into four, yet a greater reward is owed to him who had labored more over the Lord's money. Hence the Apostle too says: Honor the presbyters who are truly presbyters, especially those who labor in the word of God. From the fact that the wicked servant dared to say: you reap where you did not sow and gather where you did not scatter, we understand that the Lord accepts the good life even of Gentiles and philosophers, and regards in one way those who act justly and in another those who act unjustly, and that, in comparison with him who serves the natural law, those who neglect the written law are condemned.

For to everyone who has will be given, and he will abound; but from him who does not have, even what he seems to have will be taken away from him. Many, though they are wise by nature and have keenness of mind, if they have been negligent and have spoiled the good of nature by sloth, in comparison with one who is somewhat slower but has made up by labor and diligence for what he had less of, lose the good of nature and see the reward that had been promised them pass to others. It can also be understood thus: to him who has faith and a good will in the Lord, even if, being a man, he has had something less in works, it will be given by the good judge; but he who has not had faith will lose even the other virtues that he seemed to possess by nature. And elegantly too: even what he seems, he says, to have will be taken away from him. For whatever is without faith in Christ ought not to be credited to him who has badly misused it, but to him who granted the good of nature even to a wicked servant.

And cast the useless servant into the outer darkness; there will be weeping and gnashing of teeth. The Lord is light. Whoever is sent out by him lacks the true light. But what the weeping and gnashing of teeth is, we have said above.

But when the Son of Man comes in his majesty, and all the angels with him, then he will sit upon the seat of his majesty, and all the nations will be gathered before him, and he will separate them from one another as a shepherd separates the sheep from the goats, and he will set the sheep on his right, but the goats on his left. About to keep the Passover after two days, and to be handed over to the cross, and to be mocked by men, and to be given vinegar and gall to drink, he rightly promises the glory of one in triumph, so that he might offset the stumbling blocks that were to follow with the reward of the promise. And it is to be noted that he who is to be seen in majesty is the Son of Man. And what follows: he will set the sheep on his right, but the goats on his left, understand according to what you read elsewhere: The heart of the wise man is in his right hand, and the heart of the fool in his left, and above in this same Gospel: Let your left hand not know what your right hand is doing. The sheep, on the side of the just, are bidden to stand at the right; the goats, that is, sinners, at the left, goats being always offered for sin in the law. Nor did he say she-goats, which can bear young and, when shorn, come up from the washing, all with twin offspring, and there is no barren one among them, but goats, a wanton and butting animal, always burning for coupling.

Come, blessed of my Father, possess the kingdom prepared for you from the foundation of the world. This is to be taken according to the foreknowledge of God, with whom future things are already done.

Amen I say to you: Inasmuch as you did it to one of these least brothers of mine, you did it to me. We would have been free to understand that in every poor man Christ, hungry, is fed; thirsty, is given drink; a stranger, is brought under a roof; naked, is clothed; sick, is visited; shut in prison, has the comfort of one who talks with him. But from what follows: When you did it to one of these least brothers of mine, you did it to me, he does not seem to me to have spoken generally of the poor, but of those who are poor in spirit, toward whom he stretched out his hand and had said: My brothers and my mother are these who do the will of my Father.

And these will go into eternal punishment, but the just into eternal life. Prudent reader, observe that both the punishments are eternal and the perpetual life henceforth has no fear of falling.

Matthew 26

And it came to pass, when Jesus had finished all these sayings, he said to his disciples: You know that after two days the Passover will take place, and the Son of Man will be handed over to be crucified. Let those blush who think that the Savior feared death and said from dread of the passion: Father, if it can be done, let this cup pass from me. About to keep the Passover after two days, he knows that he is to be handed over to be crucified, and yet he does not avoid the snares, he does not flee in terror; so much so that, even when the others were unwilling to go, he goes on undaunted, when Thomas says: Let us go, that we too may die with him. And, wishing to put an end to the carnal festival and, as the shadow passed away, to give back the truth of the Passover, he said: With desire I have desired to eat this Passover with you before I suffer. For Christ our Passover has been sacrificed, if only we eat it in the unleavened bread of sincerity and truth. Moreover, as to his saying: After two days the Passover will take place, let us pass over the simple understanding and seek what is hallowed in it. After two days of the most brilliant light of the Old and the New Testament, the true Passover is celebrated for the world. The Passover (pascha), which in Hebrew is called phase, is named not from the passion, as most people think, but from the passing over, because the destroyer, seeing the blood on the doors of the Israelites, passed over and did not strike them, or else the Lord himself, bringing help to his people, walked above them. Read the book of Exodus, of which we shall treat more fully, if life is granted to us. But our passing over, that is, phase, is celebrated in this way: if, leaving behind earthly things and Egypt, we hasten to heavenly things.

Then the chief priests and the elders of the people gathered in the court of the chief priest, who was called Caiaphas; and they took counsel to seize Jesus by guile and kill him. Those who, with the Passover near, ought to have been preparing the victims, polishing the walls of the temple, sweeping the pavements, cleaning the vessels, and purifying themselves according to the rite of the law, so that they might become worthy to eat the lamb, gather together and enter into counsel how they may kill the Lord: not fearing sedition, as the simple wording shows, but taking care lest he be taken out of their hands by the help of the people.

Now when Jesus was in Bethany, in the house of Simon the leper. About to suffer for the whole world and to redeem all the nations by his blood, he stays in Bethany, the house of obedience, which once was the house of Simon the leper: not that he still remained a leper at that time, but he who had been a leper before was afterwards cleansed by the Savior, the former name remaining so that the power of the one who healed might appear. For in the catalogue of the apostles too Matthew is called the tax collector, with his former fault and occupation, though he had certainly ceased to be a tax collector. Some want the house of Simon the leper to be understood as that part of the people which believed in the Lord and was healed by him. Simon himself, too, is said to mean obedient; according to another understanding he can be interpreted as pure, and in his house the Church was healed.

A woman came to him having an alabaster of precious ointment, and she poured it over his head as he reclined. Let no one think that she who poured ointment over the head is the same as she who poured it over the feet. For the latter both washes with tears and wipes with her hair and is openly called a harlot, whereas of this one nothing of the kind is written. Nor indeed could a harlot at once become worthy of the Lord's head. Another evangelist, in place of the alabaster of precious ointment (which is a kind of marble), put pistic nard, that is, true and without guile, to show the faith of the Church and of the Gentiles.

But the disciples, seeing it, were indignant, saying: To what purpose is this waste? For this could have been sold for much and given to the poor. I know that some cavil at this passage, asking why another evangelist said that Judas alone was saddened, because he held the purse and had been a thief from the beginning, while Matthew writes that all the apostles were indignant; they do not know the figure that is called σύλληψις (syllepsis), by which all are customarily named for one and one for many. For Paul the apostle too, in his letter which is written to the Hebrews (although many of the Latins have doubts about it), when he had described the sufferings and merits of the saints, added: They were stoned, they were tempted, they were sawn asunder, they died by the slaughter of the sword, though the Jews hold that only one, the prophet Isaiah, was sawn. We can also say otherwise: that the apostles were truly indignant on account of the poor, but Judas on account of his own gain. Hence his muttering too is set down together with a charge: that he had no care for the poor but wished to provide for his own theft.

But Jesus, knowing it, said to them: Why do you trouble the woman? She has wrought a good work on me; for you always have the poor with you, but me you will not always have. Another question arises: why the Lord said to the disciples after the resurrection: Behold, I am with you even to the consummation of the world, and now says: but me you will not always have. But it seems to me that in this place he speaks of his bodily presence: that after the resurrection he would by no means be with them in such a way as he is now, in all shared life and familiarity. Mindful of this, the Apostle says: And if we had known Jesus Christ according to the flesh, yet now we know him no longer.

For in putting this ointment on my body, she has done it for my burial. What you think to be a waste of ointment is the office of burial. Nor is it strange if she has given me the good odor of her faith, since I am going to pour out my blood for her.

Amen I say to you: Wherever this Gospel shall be preached in the whole world, what she has done shall also be told in memory of her. In the whole world it is not so much this woman as the Church that is proclaimed, because she buried the Savior, because she anointed his head. And note his knowledge of things to come: that, about to suffer after two days and to die, he knows that his Gospel is to be celebrated in all the world.

And he said to them: What will you give me, and I will hand him over to you? But they fixed for him thirty pieces of silver. Unhappy Judas! The loss which he believed he had suffered from the pouring out of the ointment he wishes to make good with the price of his master. And yet he does not demand a fixed sum, so that the betrayal might at least seem profitable, but, as though handing over a cheap slave, he left it in the power of the buyers to give as much as they wished. Who fixed for him thirty pieces of silver. Joseph was not sold, as many think, for twenty pieces of gold, according to the LXX translators, but, according to the Hebrew truth, for twenty pieces of silver. For the servant could not be more precious than the master.

Now on the first day of the Unleavened Bread the disciples came to Jesus, saying: Where do you wish us to prepare for you to eat the Passover? The first day of the Unleavened Bread is the fourteenth day of the first month, when the lamb is sacrificed and the moon is at its fullest and leaven is cast out. And among those disciples who came to the Lord asking: Where do you wish us to prepare for you to eat the Passover, I reckon that Judas the betrayer was also present.

But Jesus said: Go into the city to a certain man. The new Scripture keeps the custom of the Old Testament. We often read: So-and-so said to so-and-so; and: In such and such a place, which in Hebrew is called phelmoni and helmoni; and yet the name of the persons and of the places is not given. And you will find, he says, a certain man carrying a jar of water. Their names were passed over for this reason: that a free occasion for the festival might be opened to all who are going to keep the Passover.

And the disciples did as Jesus had appointed them, and they prepared the Passover. In another evangelist it is written that they found a large upper room, furnished and cleaned, and there they made ready for him. But it seems to me that the upper room is to be understood as the spiritual law, which, going out from the narrowness of the letter, receives the Savior in a lofty place, Paul saying this very thing: that the things which he formerly reckoned as gain he despised as refuse and rubbish, so that he might prepare a lodging worthy of the Lord.

When evening had come, he reclined with the twelve disciples. Judas does everything so that suspicion of the betrayer may be removed.

And as they were eating he said: Amen I say to you that one of you is going to betray me. He who had foretold his passion foretells the betrayer too, giving room for repentance, so that, when he understood that his thoughts and secret counsels were known, he might repent of his deed; and yet he does not point him out specifically, lest, being openly convicted, he should become more shameless. He casts the charge among the whole number, so that the one who is conscious of it may repent.

And being greatly saddened they began each to say: Is it I, Lord? And surely the eleven apostles knew that they were planning nothing of the kind against the Lord; but they trust the master more than themselves, and, fearing their own frailty, they sorrowfully ask about a sin of which they had no consciousness.

But he answered and said: He who dips his hand with me in the dish, he will betray me. O the wonderful patience of the Lord! First he had said: One of you is going to betray me. The betrayer persists in evil; he reproves him more plainly, and yet he does not point out his name specifically. Judas, while the others were saddened and drew back their hands and forbade food to their mouths, with the same rashness and shamelessness with which he was going to betray him, even puts his hand into the dish with his master, so that by his boldness he might feign a good conscience.

The Son of Man indeed goes as it is written of him; but woe to that man by whom the Son of Man will be betrayed. Reproved neither the first time nor the second, he does not draw back his foot from the betrayal; rather, he feeds his shamelessness on the patience of the Lord and stores up for himself wrath on the day of wrath. Punishment is foretold, so that the torments announced might correct one whom shame had not overcome. But as to what follows:

It would have been good for him if that man had not been born, he is not on that account to be thought to have existed before he was born, on the ground that it can be well with no one except one who has existed; it is simply said that it is much better not to exist than to exist badly.

And Judas, who betrayed him, answered and said: Is it I, rabbi? He said to him: You have said it. Because the others, sad and very sad, had asked: Is it I, Lord, lest he should seem to give himself away by keeping silent, he too, whom his conscience was gnawing, who had boldly put his hand into the dish, asks in the same way: Is it I, rabbi, and adds the feeling of one who flatters, or else a sign of unbelief. For the others, who were not going to betray him, say: Is it I, Lord; this one, who was going to betray him, calls him not lord but master, as if he would have an excuse if, having denied him as lord, he had at least betrayed a master. And he said to him: You have said it. The betrayer is refuted by the same reply with which he will afterwards answer Pilate.

And while they were dining, Jesus took bread and blessed and broke it, and gave it to his disciples and said: Take and eat, this is my body. And taking the cup he gave thanks and gave it to them, saying, and the rest. After the typical Passover had been fulfilled and he had eaten the flesh of the lamb with the apostles, he takes bread, which strengthens the heart of man, and passes over to the true sacrament of the Passover, so that, just as Melchizedek, the priest of the most high God, had done in prefiguration of it when he offered bread and wine, he himself also might represent it in the truth of his own body and blood. In Luke we read of two cups with which he drank to the disciples, one of the first month and the other of the second, so that he who could not eat the lamb among the saints in the first month may eat the kid among the penitents in the second month.

But I say to you: I will not drink from now on of this fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it new with you in the kingdom of my Father. He passes from carnal things to spiritual ones. That the vine transplanted from Egypt is the people of Israel, to whom the Lord speaks through Jeremiah: I planted you a true vine; how have you been changed into the bitterness of a foreign vine, both Isaiah the prophet testifies, in the song which he sings to his beloved, and all Scripture here and there. The Lord therefore says that he will by no means drink of this vine except in the kingdom of his Father. The kingdom of the Father I take to be the faith of believers, the Apostle also confirming this very thing: The kingdom of God is within you. Therefore, when the Jews have received the kingdom of the Father (note that he says Father, not God: every name of Father refers to a son), when, I say, they have believed in God the Father and the Father has brought them to the Son, then the Lord will drink of their wine, and, in the likeness of Joseph reigning in Egypt, he will be made drunk with his brothers.

And when a hymn had been said, they went out to the Mount of Olives. This is what we read in a certain psalm: They have eaten and worshiped, all the fat ones of the earth. According to this example, whoever has been filled and made drunk with the bread and the cup of the Savior can praise the Lord and ascend the Mount of Olives, where there is refreshment from labors, solace for pain, and the knowledge of the true light.

Then Jesus says to them: All of you will suffer a stumbling block in me on this night. He foretells what they are going to suffer, so that, when they have suffered it, they may not despair of salvation, but may be set free by repenting. And he pointedly added: on this night you will suffer a stumbling block, because, just as those who get drunk get drunk at night, so also those who suffer a stumbling block undergo it in the night and in darkness. But let us say: The night has passed, and the day has drawn near.

For it is written: I will strike the shepherd, and the sheep of the flock will be scattered. This is written in other words in Zechariah the prophet, and, unless I am mistaken, it is said in the person of the prophet to God: Strike the shepherd, and let the sheep be scattered, Psalm 68 (69) also, which is sung throughout in the Lord's person, agreeing with this sense: For they have persecuted him whom you have struck. And the good shepherd is struck so that he may lay down his life for his sheep, and that out of many flocks of errors there may be made one flock and one shepherd. Of this testimony more has been said in the little book which we wrote on the best kind of translating.

But Peter answered and said to him: Even if all are scandalized, I will never be scandalized. It is not rashness, nor is it a lie; it is the faith of the apostle Peter and his burning affection toward the Lord Savior, of which we spoke above.

Jesus said to him: Amen I say to you that on this night, before the cock crows, you will deny me three times. Peter made his promise out of the ardor of faith, and the Savior, as God, knew what was to come. And note that Peter denies in the night, and denies a third time; but after the cock crowed and, as the darkness waned, the approaching light was announced, he turned and wept bitterly, washing away the filth of the denial with tears.

Then Jesus came with them to an estate which is called Gethsemane, and he said to his disciples: Sit here while I go over there and pray. Gethsemane is interpreted most fertile valley; there he ordered the disciples to sit for a little while and to wait for his return, while the Lord alone prayed for all.

And taking Peter and the two sons of Zebedee, he began to be saddened and to be sorrowful. What we said above about passion and propassion is shown also in the present passage: that the Lord, in order to prove the reality of the man he had assumed, was indeed truly saddened, but, lest passion should rule in his soul, began to be saddened by way of propassion. For it is one thing to be saddened and another to begin to be saddened. Yet he was saddened not by fear of suffering, he who had come for this very purpose, that he might suffer, and who had rebuked Peter for timidity, but on account of the most unhappy Judas, and the stumbling block of all the apostles, and the rejection of the people of the Jews, and the overthrow of wretched Jerusalem. Hence Jonah too is saddened over the withering of the gourd or ivy, not wishing his former shelter to perish. But if the heretics interpret the sadness of soul not as the Savior's affection toward those who would perish but as passion, let them answer how they expound what is said in the person of God through Ezekiel: And in all these things you saddened me.

Then he said to them: My soul is sad even to death; stay here and keep watch with me. What is saddened is the soul; and it is saddened not on account of death but even to death, until he should free the apostles by his passion. But as to his command: stay here and keep watch with me, he does not forbid them sleep, for which it was not the time with the danger at hand, but the sleep of unbelief and the torpor of the mind. Let those who suspect that Jesus took on an irrational soul tell us how he is saddened and knows the time of sadness. For although brute animals too grieve, yet they know neither the causes nor the time up to which they ought to be saddened.

And going forward a little he fell on his face, worshiping and saying: My Father, if it is possible, let this cup pass from me; yet not as I will, but as you. Having given the apostles the command to stay and keep watch with the Lord, he goes forward a little, falls on his face, and shows the humility of his mind by the posture of his flesh; and he says tenderly: My Father, and asks that, if it is possible, the cup of the passion, of which we spoke above, may pass from him. But he asks this not from fear of suffering but from mercy toward the former people, lest he drink the cup handed to him by them. Hence he pointedly did not say: let the cup pass from me, but this cup, that is, the cup of the people of the Jews, which can have no excuse of ignorance if it kills me, since it has the Law and the Prophets who prophesy of me daily. Nevertheless, returning to himself, what he had shrunk from in trepidation in the person of a man, he confirms in that of God and of the Son: yet not as I will, but as you. Let not this be done, he says, which I speak from human feeling, but that for which I came down to earth by your will.

And he came to the disciples and found them sleeping, and he says to Peter: So you could not keep watch with me one hour? He who had said above: Even if all are scandalized in you, I will never be scandalized, now cannot overcome sleep for the greatness of his sadness.

Keep watch and pray, that you may not enter into temptation. It is impossible for the human soul not to be tempted. Hence in the Lord's prayer too we say: Lead us not into temptation, namely the temptation which we cannot bear, not refusing temptation altogether but praying for the strength to endure in temptations. So here too he does not say: Keep watch and pray that you may not be tempted, but that you may not enter into temptation, that is, lest temptation overcome and conquer you and hold you within its toils. For example, the martyr who shed his blood for confessing the Lord was indeed tempted, but was not bound in the nets of temptations; but he who denies runs into the snares of temptation.

The spirit indeed is willing, but the flesh is weak. This is against the rash, who think that whatever they have believed they can attain. So, as much as we trust in the ardor of the mind, so much let us fear from the frailty of the flesh. And yet, according to the Apostle, the works of the flesh are put to death by the spirit.

Again a second time he went away and prayed, saying: My Father, if this cup cannot pass unless I drink it, your will be done. A second time he prays that, if Nineveh cannot be saved otherwise than by the withering of the gourd, the will of the Father may be done, which is not contrary to the will of the Son, as he himself says through the prophet: To do your will, my God, I have willed.

And he came again and found them sleeping; for their eyes were heavy. He alone prays for all, just as he alone suffers for all. But the eyes of the apostles were growing faint and weighed down, with the denial near.

Then he comes to his disciples and says to them: Sleep now and take your rest; behold, the hour has drawn near. After he had prayed a third time, so that in the mouth of two and three witnesses every word might stand, and had obtained that the fear of the apostles should be corrected by the repentance that followed, secure about his passion he goes on to meet the persecutors and offers himself of his own accord to be killed, and he says to his disciples:

Rise, let us go; behold, he who is going to betray me has drawn near. Let them not find us as though afraid and drawing back; let us go of our own accord to death, so that they may see the confidence and joy of one who is about to suffer.

Now he who betrayed him had given them a sign, saying: Whomever I kiss, he is the one; seize him. Wretched Judas, and yet not to be pitied! With the same unbelief with which he betrayed his master and Lord, he thought that the signs which he had seen the Savior performing were done not by divine majesty but by magic arts; and because he had perhaps heard that he was transfigured on the mountain, he feared that by a similar transformation he might slip out of the hands of the attendants. So he gives a sign, so that they might know that the one he pointed out with a kiss was the man.

And at once coming up to Jesus, he said: Hail, rabbi, and he kissed him. Shameless indeed and wicked is the confidence that calls him master and presses a kiss on the one whom he was betraying. Yet he still has something of a disciple's modesty, since he does not hand him over openly to the persecutors, but through the sign of a kiss. This is the sign which God set on Cain, so that whoever found him should not kill him.

And Jesus said to him: Friend, for what have you come? The word friend is to be understood either κατὰ ἀντίφρασιν (by antiphrasis), or at any rate according to what we read above: Friend, how did you come in here not having a wedding garment.

And behold, one of those who were with Jesus, stretching out his hand, drew his sword, and striking the servant of the chief priest cut off his ear. In another Gospel it is written that Peter did this, with the same ardor of mind as in everything else. The servant of the chief priest, too, is called Malchus; and the ear that is cut off is the right one. It should be said in passing that Malchus, that is, king, the people of the Jews, once became the servant of the impiety and greed of the priests, and lost its right ear, so as to hear the whole meanness of the letter with the left; but the Lord, in those of the Jews who were willing to believe, restored the right ear and made the servant a royal and priestly race.

Then Jesus said to him: Turn your sword back into its place; for all who take the sword will perish by the sword. Although he does not bear the sword in vain who has been appointed the avenger of the Lord's wrath against him who does evil, nevertheless whoever takes up the sword will perish by the sword. By what sword? Surely by that fiery one which turns before paradise, and by the sword of the Spirit which is described in the armor of God.

Or do you think that I cannot ask my Father, and he will furnish me now with more than twelve legions of angels? How then will the Scriptures be fulfilled, that thus it must be done? I do not need the help of twelve apostles, even if they all were to defend me, I who can have twelve legions of the angelic army. Among the ancients one legion was made up of six thousand men. For the shortness of the time we cannot set out the number in full; let it be enough to have stated the type alone: that from twelve legions come seventy-two thousand angels (the number of the nations into which the language of men was divided). The following sentence shows a mind ready to suffer: that the prophets would have sung in vain unless the Lord had affirmed by his passion that they had spoken the truth.

At that hour Jesus said: You have come out as against a robber with swords and clubs to arrest me? Daily I sat among you teaching in the temple, and you did not seize me. It is foolish, he says, to seek with swords and clubs one who hands himself over of his own accord into your hands, and to track down at night through a betrayer, as if he were hiding and avoiding your eyes, one who teaches daily in the temple. But you gather against me in darkness for this reason, that your power is in darkness.

But all this was done that the Scriptures of the prophets might be fulfilled. What are the Scriptures of the prophets? They pierced my hands and feet; and elsewhere: He was led like a sheep to the sacrifice; and in another place: By the iniquities of my people he was led to death.

But they, holding Jesus, led him to Caiaphas the chief priest, where the scribes and the elders had gathered. Moses, at God's command, had ordered that the high priests should succeed their fathers and that a line of descent should be woven among the priests. Josephus relates that this Caiaphas bought the high priesthood for one year only from Herod for a price. It is no wonder, then, if an unjust high priest judges unjustly.

But Peter followed him from afar. He who was going to deny the Lord followed from afar.

And going inside he sat with the attendants, to see the end. Either out of a disciple's love or out of human curiosity he wished to know what the high priest would decide about the Lord: whether he would condemn him to death or would release him after beating him with scourges. And in this lies the difference between the ten apostles and Peter: they flee; he, although from a distance, still follows the Savior.

At last two false witnesses came and said: This man said: I can destroy the temple of God and after three days build it. How are they false witnesses, if they say the things which we read above that the Lord said? But a false witness is one who does not understand what is said in the same sense in which it is said. For the Lord had spoken of the temple of his body; but they falsely accuse him in the very words too, and by adding or changing a few they make, as it were, a just accusation. The Savior had said: Dissolve this temple; they change it and say: I can destroy the temple of God. You, he says, dissolve, not I, because it is unlawful for us to lay hands on ourselves. Then they twist it: and after three days build it, so that he might seem to have spoken properly of the Jewish temple. But the Lord, to show a living and breathing temple, had said: And I in three days will raise it up. It is one thing to build, another to raise up.

And the chief priest, rising up, said to him: Do you answer nothing to the things these men testify against you? But Jesus was silent. Headlong anger and impatience, finding no ground for a false accusation, shake the high priest from his throne, so that he might show the madness of his mind by the movement of his body. The more Jesus kept silent before the false witnesses and the impious priests, unworthy of his reply, the more the high priest, overcome by fury, provokes him to answer, so that from any occasion of speech whatever he might find ground for accusing him. Nevertheless Jesus is silent. For he knew, as God, that whatever he answered would be twisted into a false accusation.

And the chief priest said to him: I adjure you by the living God that you tell us whether you are the Christ, the Son of God. Why do you adjure him, most impious of priests: in order to accuse, or in order to believe? If in order to accuse, others are accusing him: condemn him in his silence; if in order to believe, why did you refuse to believe him when he confessed?

Jesus says to him: You have said it. Both to Pilate and to Caiaphas the reply is alike, so that they may be condemned by their own sentence.

Then the chief priest tore his garments, saying: He has blasphemed; what further need have we of witnesses? The man whom fury had shaken from the priestly throne the same rage provokes to tear his clothes. And he tears his garments to show that the Jews had lost the glory of the priesthood and that the high priests have an empty seat. But it is also a Jewish custom to tear their garments when they hear anything blasphemous and as it were against God, as we read that Paul and Barnabas also did when in Lycaonia they were being honored with the worship of gods. But Herod, because he did not give honor to God but acquiesced in the immoderate applause of the people, was at once struck by an angel.

Then they spat in his face and struck him with their fists. That what was written might be fulfilled: I gave my cheeks to blows and did not turn my face away from the shame of spitting.

And others struck his face with their palms, saying: Prophesy to us, Christ, who is it that struck you? It was foolish to answer those who were beating him and to prophesy who was striking him, when the madness of the one who struck was plain to see. But just as he did not prophesy this to you, so he foretold that other thing most plainly: that Jerusalem would be surrounded by an army and that not a stone would be left upon a stone in the temple.

But Peter was sitting outside in the court. He sat outside to see the outcome of the affair, and he did not come near Jesus, lest any suspicion should arise among the attendants.

And again he denied with an oath: I do not know the man. I know that some, out of pious affection toward the apostle Peter, have interpreted this passage so as to say that Peter denied not God but a man, and that the sense is: I do not know the man, because I know God. How frivolous this is the prudent reader understands: they so defend the apostle as to make the Lord guilty of a lie. For if he did not deny, then the Lord lied, who had said: Amen I say to you that on this night, before the cock crows, you will deny me three times. See what he says: you will deny me, not the man.

Surely you too are one of them, for your speech also makes you plain. Not that Peter was of another language or of a foreign nation, for all of them were Hebrews, both those who accused and he who was accused, but because each province and region had its own peculiarities and could not avoid its native sound of speech. Hence too the Ephraimites in the book of Judges cannot say the σύνθημα (the password).

Then he began to curse and to swear that he did not know the man. And immediately the cock crowed. In another Gospel we read that after Peter's denial and the crowing of the cock the Savior looked at Peter and by his gaze moved him to bitter tears; nor could it be that he should remain in the darkness of denial on whom the light of the world had looked.

And going outside he wept bitterly. Sitting in the court of Caiaphas he could not repent. He goes outside, away from the council of the impious, so that he may wash away the filth of a fearful denial with bitter weeping.

Matthew 27

When morning had come, all the chief priests and the elders of the people took counsel against Jesus, to hand him over to death; and they led him bound and handed him over to Pontius Pilate the governor. He was led not only to Pilate but also to Herod, so that both might mock the Lord. And see the zeal of the priests for evil: they kept watch the whole night in order to commit murder. And they handed him over bound to Pilate. For they had this custom, that whomever they had adjudged to death they handed over bound to the judge.

Then Judas, who betrayed him, seeing that he had been condemned, moved by repentance brought back the thirty pieces of silver to the chief priests and the elders, saying: I have sinned in betraying just blood. The weight of his impiety shut out the greatness of his avarice. Judas, seeing the Lord adjudged to death, brought the price back to the priests, as if it were in his power to change the sentence of the persecutors. And so, although he changed his will, yet he did not change the outcome of his first will. But if he sinned who betrayed just blood, how much more did they sin who had bought just blood and, by offering a price, had provoked the disciple to betrayal! Let those who try to introduce different natures, and say that Judas the traitor was of an evil nature and could not have been preserved by his election to the apostleship, answer how an evil nature repented.

But they said: What is that to us? You see to it. And throwing down the pieces of silver in the temple he withdrew, and going away he hanged himself with a noose. It profited him nothing to have repented, since by that repentance he could not correct the crime. If ever a brother so sins against a brother that he is able to amend what he has sinned, it can be forgiven him; but if the works remain, repentance is taken up in vain by the voice. This is what is said in the psalm about this same most unhappy Judas: And let his prayer become sin, so that not only was he unable to amend the wickedness of the betrayal, but to the former crime he added also the charge of murdering himself. The Apostle too says something of the kind in the second letter to the Corinthians: Lest the brother be swallowed up by excessive sorrow.

But the chief priests, having taken the pieces of silver, said: It is not lawful to put them into the corbanan, because it is the price of blood. Truly straining out the gnat and swallowing the camel! For if they do not put the money into the corbanam, that is, into the treasury and the gifts of God, because it is the price of blood, why is the blood itself shed?

And having taken counsel, they bought with them the potter's field, as a burial place for strangers. They indeed did it with another intention, to leave from the purchase of the field an everlasting monument of their impiety. But we, who were strangers to the Law and the Prophets, have taken up their perverse endeavors for our salvation, and we rest in the price of his blood. And it is called the potter's field because our potter is Christ.

Then was fulfilled what was spoken through the prophet Jeremiah, saying: And they took the thirty pieces of silver, the price of the one valued, whom they valued from the sons of Israel, and they gave them for the potter's field, as the Lord appointed for me. This testimony is not found in Jeremiah; but in Zechariah, who is almost the last of the twelve prophets, a certain likeness is reported, and although the sense does not differ much, yet both the order and the words are different. I read recently, in a certain Hebrew volume which a Hebrew of the Nazarene sect brought to me, an apocryphal Jeremiah, in which I found these things written word for word. But still it seems to me that the testimony is rather taken from Zechariah, in the common manner of the evangelists and apostles, who, passing over the order of the words, bring forward only the sense from the Old Testament as an example.

And Jesus stood before the governor. And the governor questioned him, saying: Are you the king of the Jews? Since Pilate questions him on no other charge than whether he is king of the Jews, the Jews are convicted of impiety, because they could not find, even falsely, anything to lay against the Savior.

Jesus says to him: You say so. He answered in such a way that he both spoke the truth and his word lay open to no false accusation. And note that to Pilate, who was pronouncing sentence unwillingly, he answered in some part, but to the priests and rulers he would not answer, judging them unworthy of his speech.

Then Pilate says to him: Do you not hear how many testimonies they speak against you? He who condemns Jesus is indeed a heathen, but he lays the cause upon the people of the Jews. Do you not hear how many testimonies they speak against you? But Jesus would answer nothing, lest by refuting the charge he should be released by the governor and the benefit of the cross be put off.

Now he had at that time a notable prisoner, who was called Barabbas. This man, in the Gospel which is written according to the Hebrews, is interpreted as son of their master; he had been condemned for sedition and murder. Pilate offers them the choice of whom they wished him to release, the robber or Jesus, not doubting that Jesus would rather be chosen, since he knew that he had been handed over out of envy. Therefore the cause of the cross is plainly envy.

While he was sitting on the judgment seat, his wife sent to him, saying: Let there be nothing between you and that just man; for I have suffered many things today in a vision because of him. Note that dreams are often revealed by God to Gentiles, and that in Pilate and his wife, who confess the Lord to be just, there is the testimony of the Gentile people.

Pilate says to them: What then shall I do with Jesus who is called Christ? They all say: Let him be crucified. The governor says to them: Why, what evil has he done? But they cried out the more, saying: Let him be crucified. Pilate gave many occasions for freeing the Savior: first, by comparing the robber with the just man; then by adding: What then shall I do with Jesus who is called Christ, that is, who is your king; and when they answered: Let him be crucified, he did not acquiesce at once, but, following the suggestion of his wife, who had sent word: Let there be nothing between you and that just man, he too answered: Why, what evil has he done? In saying this Pilate acquitted Jesus. But they cried out the more, saying: Let him be crucified, so that what he had said in Psalm 21 (22) might be fulfilled: Many dogs have surrounded me; the assembly of evildoers has besieged me; and that word of Jeremiah: My inheritance has become to me like a lion in the forest; they have raised their voice against me; Isaiah too agreeing in this sense: And I waited for them to do judgment, but they did iniquity, and not justice but an outcry.

But Pilate, seeing that he was gaining nothing, but that rather a tumult was arising, took water and washed his hands before the people, saying: I am innocent of the blood of this just man; you see to it. Pilate took water according to that prophetic word: I will wash my hands among the innocent, so that in the washing of his hands the works of the Gentiles might be cleansed, and that he might in some way make us strangers to the impiety of the Jews, who cried out: Crucify him, testifying to this and saying: I indeed wished to free an innocent man, but since a sedition is arising and the charge of treason against Caesar is being thrust upon me: I am innocent of the blood of this just man. The judge who is forced to pass sentence against the Lord does not condemn the one brought before him, but convicts those who bring him, pronouncing that he who is to be crucified is just. You, he says, see to it; I am the minister of the laws, your voice sheds the blood.

And all the people answered and said: His blood be upon us and upon our children. This imprecation persists upon the Jews to the present day, and the blood of the Lord will not be taken away from them. Hence he speaks through Isaiah: If you lift up your hands to me, I will not hear you; for your hands are full of blood. An excellent inheritance the Jews have left their children, saying: His blood be upon us and upon our children.

Then he released Barabbas to them; but Jesus, scourged, he handed over to them to be crucified. Barabbas the robber, who stirred up seditions among the crowds, who was the author of murders, was released to the people of the Jews, that is, the devil, who reigns in them to this day, and for that reason they cannot have peace; but Jesus, handed over by the Jews, is acquitted by Pilate's wife and is called just by the governor himself, and the centurion confesses that he is truly the Son of God. Let the learned reader ask how it is consistent that Pilate washed his hands and said: I am innocent of the blood of this just man, and afterwards handed over Jesus, scourged, to be crucified. But it must be known that he was serving the Roman laws, by which it was decreed that one who is crucified should first be beaten with scourges. So Jesus was handed over to the soldiers to be beaten, and scourges cut that most sacred body and the breast that could contain God. And this was done so that, since it was written: Many are the scourges of sinners; by his being scourged we might be freed from blows, Scripture saying to the just man: The scourge has not come near your tent.

Then the soldiers of the governor, taking Jesus into the praetorium, gathered the whole cohort to him; and stripping him, they put a scarlet cloak around him, and plaiting a crown of thorns they put it on his head, and a reed in his right hand, and bending the knee before him they mocked him, saying: Hail, king of the Jews. The soldiers indeed, because he had been called king of the Jews, and the scribes and priests had laid this charge against him, that he was usurping rule for himself among the people of Israel, do this in mockery: having stripped him of his former garments, they clothe him in a scarlet cloak in place of the red border which the ancient kings used to wear, and in place of a diadem they put on him a thorny crown, in place of a royal scepter they give him a reed, and they worship him as a king. But let us understand all these things mystically. For just as Caiaphas said: It is necessary that one man die for all, not knowing what he said, so these too, whatever they did, although they did it with another mind, were nevertheless bestowing mysteries on us who believe. In the scarlet cloak he bears up the bloody works of the Gentiles; in the crown of thorns he dissolves the ancient curse; with the reed he kills venomous animals; or else he held the reed in his hand to write down the sacrilege of the Jews.

And spitting on him they took the reed and struck his head. At that time was fulfilled: I did not turn away my face from the shame of spitting. And yet, though they strike his head with a reed, he bears all things patiently, to show true the prophecy of Isaiah, who says: A shaken reed he will not break.

And after they had mocked him, they stripped him of the cloak and clothed him in his own garments, and led him away to crucify him. When Jesus is scourged and spat upon and derided, he does not have his own garments, but those which he had taken on for our sins; but when he is crucified, and the pageant of mockery and derision has passed, then he receives back his former clothes and takes up his own adornment, and at once the elements are thrown into confusion and the creature gives testimony to the Creator.

And going out they found a man of Cyrene named Simon; him they pressed into service to carry his cross. Let no one think that the history of John the evangelist is contrary to this passage. For he says that the Lord, going out of the praetorium, carried his own cross; but Matthew reports that they found a man of Cyrene named Simon, on whom, pressing him into service, they laid the cross of Jesus. But this is to be understood: that Jesus, going out of the praetorium, carried his cross himself, and afterwards met Simon, on whom they laid the cross to be carried. According to anagogy, however, the nations take up the cross of Jesus, and the obedient stranger carries the ignominy of the Savior.

And they came to the place which is called Golgotha, which is the place of Calvary. I have heard someone expound the place of Calvary as the place in which Adam was buried, and say that it was so called because the head of the ancient man was laid there, and that this is what the Apostle says: Arise, you who sleep, and rise from the dead, and Christ will enlighten you. A pleasing interpretation, and soothing to the ear of the people, but not true. For outside the city and beyond the gate there are places in which the heads of the condemned are cut off, and they have taken the name of Calvary, that is, of the beheaded. And the Lord was crucified there for this reason: that where formerly was the ground of the condemned, there the standards of martyrdom might be raised; and just as for us he was made the curse of the cross, and was scourged and crucified, so for the salvation of all he is crucified as a guilty man among the guilty. But if anyone should wish to contend that the Lord was crucified there so that his blood might drip upon the tomb of Adam, let us ask him why other robbers too were crucified in the same place. From this it is evident that Calvary signifies not the sepulcher of the first man but the place of the beheaded, so that where sin abounded, grace might superabound. Indeed, that Adam was buried near Hebron and Arba we read in the book of Joshua son of Nun.

And they gave him wine mixed with gall to drink, and when he had tasted it he would not drink. God speaks to Jerusalem: I planted you a true vine; how have you turned into the bitterness of a foreign vine? A bitter vine makes bitter wine, which it gives the Lord Jesus to drink, so that what is written might be fulfilled: They gave gall for my food, and in my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink. But what is said: when he had tasted it he would not drink, indicates that he tasted indeed the bitterness of death for us, but on the third day he rose again.

After they had crucified him, they divided his garments, casting lots. This too had been prophesied in the same psalm: They divided my garments among themselves, and upon my vesture they cast lots.

And sitting down they kept watch over him. The diligence of the soldiers and priests profits us, so that the power of the one rising again may appear greater and more manifest.

And they set above his head the charge against him, written: This is Jesus the king of the Jews. I cannot sufficiently wonder, as the greatness of the matter deserves, that after false witnesses had been bought and the unhappy people had been stirred up to sedition and clamor, he found no other cause for putting him to death than that he was king of the Jews. And they perhaps did this mocking and laughing. But Pilate answered them, even against their will: What I have written, I have written. Whether you will or not, O Jews, the whole crowd of the Gentiles has answered you: Jesus is king of the Jews, that is, the commander of those who believe and confess.

Then two robbers were crucified with him, one on the right and one on the left. If Golgotha is the tomb of Adam and not the place of the condemned, and the Lord is crucified there in order to raise up Adam, why are two robbers crucified in the same place?

And those passing by blasphemed him, moving their heads. They blasphemed because they were passing by the way and would not walk on the true road of the Scriptures. They moved their heads because they had already moved their feet before and did not stand upon the rock. And the foolish people, insulting him, says the very same thing that the false witnesses had made up.

He saved others; himself he cannot save. Even against their will the scribes and Pharisees confess that he saved others. So your own sentence condemns you. For he who saved others could surely have saved himself too, if he had wished.

Let him come down now from the cross, and we believe him. A deceitful promise! Which is greater: to come down from the cross while still alive, or to rise from the sepulcher when dead? He rose again, and you do not believe. Therefore, even if he had come down from the cross, you would likewise not believe. But it seems to me that the demons put this into them. For as soon as the Lord was crucified, they felt the power of the cross and understood that their strength was broken, and they are working for this, that he should come down from the cross. But the Lord, knowing the snares of his adversaries, remains on the gibbet in order to destroy the devil.

And the robbers too who had been fixed with him reproached him with the same. Here, by the trope which is called σύλληψις (syllepsis), both are presented as having blasphemed, in place of one robber. But Luke asserts that, while one blasphemed, the other confessed and on the contrary rebuked the blasphemer; not that the Gospels disagree, but because at first both blasphemed, and then, when the sun fled, the earth was shaken, the rocks were split and darkness came on, one believed in Jesus and corrected his former denial by his subsequent confession. In the two robbers both peoples, of the Gentiles and of the Jews, at first blasphemed the Lord; afterwards one, terrified by the greatness of the signs, repented, and to this day rebukes the Jews when they blaspheme.

Now from the sixth hour there was darkness over the whole earth until the ninth hour. Those who have written against the Gospels suspect that the disciples of Christ, out of ignorance, interpreted as concerning the resurrection of the Lord an eclipse of the sun, which usually occurs at fixed and appointed times, although a failure of the sun never usually occurs except at the rising of the moon. But no one doubts that at the time of the Passover the moon was at its fullest. And lest perhaps the shadow of the earth, or the disk of the moon set against the sun, should seem to have caused a short and rust-colored darkness, the space of three hours is stated, so that every pretext of the cavilers might be taken away. And I think this was done so that the prophecy might be fulfilled which says: The sun shall go down at midday, and the light shall be darkened over the earth in the day, and in another place: The sun went down while it was still midday. And it seems to me that the brightest light of the world, that is, the greater luminary, drew back its rays, so that it might neither see the Lord hanging nor let the impious blasphemers enjoy its light.

And about the ninth hour Jesus cried out with a loud voice, saying: Heli Heli lema sabacthani, that is: My God, my God, why have you forsaken me? He made use of the beginning of Psalm 21 (22); and what is read in the middle of the little verse: Look upon me, is superfluous. For in the Hebrew it is read: My God, my God, why have you forsaken me? Therefore they are impious who think that the psalm was spoken in the person of David, or of Esther and Mordecai, since even the evangelists understand testimonies taken from it as concerning the Savior, such as this: They divided my garments among themselves, and upon my vesture they cast lots; and another: They dug through my hands and my feet. Nor should you wonder at the lowliness of the words and the complaints of one forsaken, since, knowing the form of a servant, you see the stumbling block of the cross.

But some who were standing there and heard it said: This man is calling Elijah. Not all, but some, whom I judge to have been Roman soldiers who did not understand the idiom of the Hebrew language, but from the fact that he said: Heli Heli, thought that Elijah was being invoked by him. But if you would rather understand that it was Jews who said this, they do this too in their usual manner, to defame the Lord with weakness, as one who begs the help of Elijah.

And at once one of them ran, and taking a sponge he filled it with vinegar and put it on a reed and gave him to drink. And these things were done so that the prophecy might be fulfilled: In my thirst they gave me vinegar to drink. To this day the Jews and all who do not believe in the Lord's resurrection give Jesus vinegar and gall to drink, and give him wine mixed with myrrh to put him to sleep, so that he may not see their evils.

And Jesus, crying out again with a loud voice, sent forth his spirit. It is a sign of divine power to send forth the spirit, as he himself had also said: No one can take my soul from me, but I lay it down of myself, that I may take it up again.

And the veil of the temple was torn in two, from the top to the bottom. The veil of the temple was torn, and all the mysteries of the law, which were previously covered, were laid open and passed over to the people of the Gentiles. In the Gospel of which we often make mention, we read that the lintel of the temple, of immense size, was broken and split apart. Josephus also reports that the angelic powers, once the guardians of the temple, then cried out together: Let us depart from these abodes.

The earth was shaken, and the rocks were split, and the tombs were opened. No one doubts what the greatness of the signs signifies according to the letter: that heaven and earth and all things were showing that the crucified was their Lord. But it seems to me that the earthquake and the rest bear the type of believers: that, having left behind the former vices of their errors and having softened the hardness of their heart, those who previously were like the tombs of the dead afterwards came to know the Creator.

And many bodies of the saints who had fallen asleep arose, and coming out of the tombs after his resurrection they came into the holy city and appeared to many. As Lazarus, having died, rose again, so also many bodies of the saints rose again, to show the Lord rising. And yet, though the tombs were opened, they did not rise before the Lord rose, so that he might be the firstborn of the resurrection from the dead. As for the holy city in which those rising were seen, let us understand either the heavenly Jerusalem or this earthly one, which formerly was holy. Just as Matthew too is called the tax collector, not because, now an apostle, he still remains a tax collector, but because he keeps his former name, so the city of Jerusalem was called holy because of the temple and the holy of holies, and to distinguish it from the other cities in which idols were worshiped. But when it is said: appeared to many, it is shown that the resurrection was not general, such as to appear to all, but special, to a great many, so that those might see it who deserved to behold it.

But the centurion, and those who were with him keeping watch over Jesus, when they saw the earthquake and the things that were happening, were greatly afraid, saying: Truly this was the Son of God. In another Gospel, after the earthquake, the cause of the centurion's wonder is set forth more plainly: that when he had seen him yield up the spirit, he said: Truly this was the Son of God. For no one has the power of yielding up the spirit except him who is the creator of souls. And let us understand spirit in this place for soul, either because it makes the body spiritual and living, or because the substance of the soul itself is spirit, according to what is written: You will take away their spirit, and they will fail. And this is to be considered, that the centurion, before the cross, in the very stumbling block of the passion, confesses him truly the Son of God, and Arius in the Church preaches him a creature.

And there were many women there at a distance, who had followed Jesus from Galilee, ministering to him. It was a Jewish custom, and by the ancient usage of the nation it was not held to be a fault, that women should supply food and clothing to their teachers from their own substance. Because this could cause a stumbling block among the nations, Paul records that he had cast it aside: Have we not the power to lead about women as sisters, as the other apostles also do? And they ministered to the Lord from their own substance, so that he whose spiritual things they reaped might reap their carnal things; not that the Lord of creatures needed food, but to show a type for teachers, that they ought to be content with food and clothing from their disciples. But let us see what sort of companions he had: Mary Magdalene, from whom he had cast out seven demons; and Mary the mother of James and Joseph, his aunt, the sister of Mary the mother of the Lord; and the mother of the sons of Zebedee, who a little before had asked for the kingdom for her children; and others, whom we read of in the other Gospels.

When it was evening, there came a certain rich man from Arimathea named Joseph, who himself also was a disciple of Jesus. He is said to be rich, not out of the boastfulness of the writer, as if to report that a noble and very wealthy man was a disciple of Jesus, but to show the reason why he was able to obtain the body of Jesus from Pilate. For it was not for a poor and unknown man to approach Pilate, the governor of the Roman power, and obtain the body of one crucified. In another evangelist this Joseph is called βουλευτής, that is, counselor, and some think that the first psalm was composed about him: Blessed is the man who has not walked in the counsel of the impious, and the rest.

And having taken the body of Jesus, he wrapped it in a clean linen cloth. From the simple burial of the Lord the ambition of the rich is condemned, who cannot do without riches even in their tombs. And according to spiritual understanding we can also take this to mean that the body of the Lord is to be wrapped not in gold, not in gems and silk, but in pure linen; although this too may be signified, that he wraps Jesus in a clean linen cloth who has received him with a pure mind.

And he laid it in his own new tomb, which he had hewn in the rock, and he rolled a great stone to the door of the tomb and went away. He is laid in a new tomb, lest after the resurrection, while the other bodies remained, it should be pretended that another had risen.
